# Evidence, Methods and Actions for Steering Cities: A Survey by Block

*The methods, evidence, open terms and actions that an urban analyst's engagement with a client city needs, read through the eight blocks of kybernesis's loop: across cities that are green, inclusive, resilient and productive, disaster risk management, territorial development, land, spatial data infrastructure, tourism, economic evaluation and allocation, and the practitioner programmes of the Tokyo Development Learning Center.*

Status: `seed` (2026-11-19). It was assembled from eight parts written by agents with web research. Activity: research, in service of use. It serves the design [An Engagement with a City, from Evidence to Action](../../../development/ideas/2026-11-19-an-engagement-with-a-city.md) and the foundation kybernesis ([ADR 0130](../../../development/decisions/0130-kybernesis-steering-by-evidence.md)). Every bibliographic item carries one of three markers: **[V]**, verified in the agent's session against a primary record (the issuing body's page, the document itself, or a DOI resolved through Crossref); **[S]**, confirmed only from secondary sources; **[M]**, from memory, not verified.

## 0. Preface

### 0.1 Purpose

An urban analyst at the Tokyo Development Learning Center (TDLC, a partnership of the Government of Japan and the World Bank Group) serves several missions:

- cities that are green, inclusive, resilient and productive;
- disaster risk management;
- territorial development and rural–urban linkages;
- land and spatial data infrastructure;
- tourism;
- the practitioner programmes, whose journey runs from needs identification through collaborative learning, a technical deep dive in Japan, advisory support and community engagement to results capture.

The tools that would serve these missions are built on the foundation kybernesis. Its loop steers a system known through evidence, in eight blocks:

1. **Collection** gathers evidence by declared designs.
2. **Evidence** keeps each trace with its provenance.
3. **Interpretation** declares readings of open terms, each with its source.
4. **Estimation** forms a belief about the state, admitted only if it is sound, that is, if it never excludes the true state.
5. **Evaluation** applies operators to the belief. They yield features, needs, problems, risks and assets, on declared scale types, judged against standards held by someone.
6. **PolicyMaking** makes, appraises and selects options, and judges the transfer of a practice from elsewhere as a lens with support factors.
7. **Actions** are interventions and inquiries.
8. **Results capture** shows what an action did.

This survey asks the design's eight questions of every mission, one for each block. It cannot be complete, and the design does not need it to be: a block that has no method yet is filled by a person, and the record says so (design §4).

### 0.2 How the Survey Was Made

Eight parts were written in parallel by agents. Each agent had web research and worked to one brief:

- **Part A**, green cities;
- **Part B**, inclusive cities, land tenure and community engagement;
- **Part C**, resilient cities: disaster risk management, climate adaptation and recovery after conflict;
- **Part D**, the productive city: economies, municipal finance, land value, territory, tourism statistics and spatial data;
- **Part E**, knowledge programmes and the transfer of practice;
- **Part F**, methods of estimating and evaluating risk;
- **Part G**, methods of economic evaluation and allocation;
- **Part H**, the promotion, development and management of tourism.

Each part restates its mission block by block and ends with tables of its indexes, its blocks and its actions. Parts A and G were shortened at the coordinator's request. Part H's web searches ran out before Nara, Bhutan's visitor fee and Barcelona's licences were checked (§H.0).

The first five sections of this report were written from the parts alone. They introduce no source, figure or address that the parts do not give. The parts follow, verbatim, as Chapters A to H. A script demoted each heading by one level and prefixed each section number with its chapter's letter, so §C.5.2 is section 5.2 of Part C. A cross-reference inside a chapter keeps the part's own numbering: "§5.2" in Chapter C means §C.5.2.

### 0.3 Verification Markers

The parts define the markers in the same way. A marker vouches for the bibliographic record. A finding attributed to the item rests on the record only where the part says so, and several parts mark a finding apart from its record; Part B, for example, writes "(findings [S])". No marker has been changed here. Where two chapters mark the same item differently, the synthesis gives the marker of the chapter it draws on, and the weaker marker governs before the item is quoted.

The closing note counts the items and markers chapter by chapter. The reference lists hold 448 entries by the script's count: 353 [V], 72 [S] and 23 [M]. Chapter H cites a further 85 or so items in its text.

Three disagreements between parts are left as the parts give them, since they could not be settled from the parts themselves:

- **The 2025 deep dive on land.** The October 2025 deep dive on urban planning and land had "about 40 practitioners from 10 client countries" in Chapter B (§B.5.3, [V]), "about 65 participants from nine countries" in Chapter D (§D.6.2, [V]), and "about forty participants from ten countries" in Chapter E (§E.2.2, [S]).
- **Kitakyushu's partnership.** Chapter A dates Kitakyushu's city partnership with TDLC from March 2017 (§A.4, [V]). Chapter E has the City Partnership Program beginning in 2016 with Kitakyushu among its first four cities (§E.2.3, [V]).
- **The number of partner cities.** Chapter C counts seven Japanese partner cities (§C.4.1, [V]). Chapter E explains that the count depends on which page is read (§E.2.3).

### 0.4 How to Read It

The report is read in three layers.

- **The synthesis.** §1 takes the blocks in order and gives, across all missions, the methods, the evidence with its licences and biases, the open terms that recur, the state that is better estimated than observed, and what the infrastructure already computes.
- **The integration.** §2 shows the trade-offs and complementarities that appear only when the missions are read together. §3 merges the parts' actions into one table. §4 consolidates the new modules the parts propose.
- **The chapters.** They give the depth: frameworks, Japanese practice with the support factors of its transfer, indexes, and the full references.

Citations in §1 to §4 give a short author and year with the marker of the chapter drawn on, then that chapter's section, for example "(Cutter et al. 2003 [V]; §F.7.1)". Two terms are kybernesis's own:

- the **standing** of a finding is clear, arguable or untenable over the admissible readings of its open terms;
- its **measure** is the estimate with its interval.

## 1. A Synthesis by Block

The design organises the survey by block because a method, a kind of evidence or an action belongs to a block, not to a sector. Read across the eight chapters, three findings hold in every block.

1. **Most of what matters is latent.** Disaster risk, the capacity of an agency, informal employment, the visitor stock, the land value surface and a city's own agglomeration elasticity are estimated, never observed. Estimation therefore carries the weight, and Evaluation always applies its operators to an estimate (§C.2.1, §D.7.4, §E.1, §H.4.4).
2. **Readings and scales govern conclusions.** Almost every index has open terms whose readings move its value or its rank, and many evaluation spaces are ordinal (§B.3.9, §C.2.4, §D.7.3, §F.7.1).
3. **Most of the general methods exist.** What is missing is chiefly domain content, readers of evidence, and a few general estimators (§4).

### 1.1 Collection

Every source is a design (who could be observed) and a mechanism (who was observed, and how). The chapters agree that the mechanism should travel with each trace, in peira's language of full data coarsened by a mechanism (§B.4.1, §C.5.1). Seven designs recur.

- **Probability surveys.** The Waste Wise Cities Tool's stratified cluster survey takes 90 households over 8 days, and 150 in megacities [V] (§A.5). GPSC asks at least 100 households per district for perception indicators [V] (§B.1.3). Visitor intercept surveys sample visits in proportion to stay, and so need weights (§H.4.1).
- **Community enumeration and participatory mapping.** Here residents are the observers: Know Your City has profiled 7,712 slums in 224 cities [V]. Participatory appraisal (Chambers 1994 [V]), Her City [V] and STDM [V] work the same way (§B.4.1).
- **Expert panels and workshops.** These include LGAF's nine panels [V], SDG 11.3.2's five evaluators [V], and the Scorecard's workshop, where "the conversation is often as important as the score" [V]. The MFSA, PEFA and IGIF self-assessments are of the same kind (§B.4.1, §C.5.1, §D.7.1). A panel's composition is part of its evidence.
- **Administrative extraction.** Budgets, rolls, registers and land records come under data-sharing agreements, as do the grievance logs that ESS10 requires [V] (§B.4.1, §D.7.1).
- **Remote sensing and hazard models.** Overpasses are daytime and clear-sky [V] (§A.5). Footprints and heights are inferred from imagery (Sirko et al. 2021 [V]; Pesaresi and Politis 2023 [V]; §F.6.1). A hazard map's return periods, defences and resolution are part of the observation (§C.5.1).
- **Digital traces.** Mobile positioning correlated up to 0.99 with accommodation statistics in Estonia's most visited regions (Ahas et al. 2008 [V]). Eurostat found access under privacy law the main barrier [V] (§H.4.1).
- **Drills.** A drill is an experiment on the evacuation system (§C.5.7.6). People depart earlier in drills than in real events [M] (§F.8.4).

A knowledge programme gathers evidence of its own: needs assessments after the Capacity Development Results Framework (Otoo, Agapitova and Behrens 2009 [V]), follow-up surveys, outcome harvests (Wilson-Grau and Britt 2012 [V]) and stories of change (Davies and Dart 2005 [V]). Participants are chosen to serve operations already under way [V], so selection on the outcome is built in (§E.2.2, §E.7.1).

The mechanisms most used exclude the excluded.

- Censuses under-enumerate informal settlements and omit the homeless [V].
- DHS displaces cluster coordinates by up to 2 km in towns [S].
- A national sample of about 1,000, such as Prindex's [V], resolves no city.
- Registers miss informal firms, accommodation surveys miss private lodging [S], and GTFS misses informal transit [V] (§B.2.2, §B.3.4, §B.4.1, §D.7.2, §H.3.5).

Each source should therefore carry its frame and its known exclusions as data, so that Estimation can weight or bound for them. An inquiry should be proposed where an exclusion is largest (§B.4.1).

*Remark.* What to collect first is a decision judged by the value of information: a city deciding whether to borrow for a station needs its debt register before its firm census (§D.7.1). TDLC's needs identification is this block's scoping. Licences and the terms agreed with a community travel with every trace (§B.4.2).

*Coverage.* peira's designs, surveys and protocols, the data library's readers, and tyche's tests within a budget cover the general part. Missing are readers for GTFS, J-SHIS, platform data and mobile aggregates, and the Waste Wise Cities Tool's schema (§4.3).

### 1.2 Evidence

Each trace enters with its provenance, licence, resolution, custody and known biases (§C.5.2, §D.7.2). Chapter D distinguishes five kinds of provenance: an official record, an official estimate, a modelled product, a third party's observation, and a survey. A modelled product is evidence of a model applied to the city. Used again to estimate the same quantity, it counts the model's error twice unless the dependence is declared. Reports that share an origin are therefore counted once, as tekmerion counts them (§D.7.2, §E.7.2, §H.4.2).

**Licences** vary widely and must travel with the data.

- *CC BY 4.0*: EDGAR, whose IEA CO2 data are CC BY-NC-ND, ACAG, WorldCover and Dynamic World [V] (§A.5).
- *CC BY 4.0 or ODbL*: Open Buildings [V].
- *Non-commercial use*: Prindex and EM-DAT [V].
- *No stated terms*: Know Your City [V] (§B.4.2, §C.3.11).
- *No restriction*: JRC's flood maps [S], against Fathom's commercial terms [M] (§C.5.2).
- *Inside Airbnb*: twelve months under CC BY 4.0, without scraping or republishing [V] (§H.4.1).
- *Contracts*: mobile and card data.

A licence's reading is itself an open term (§B.7.2).

**Resolution** limits what may be asked.

- Global flood maps at about 90 m cannot resolve streets, so pluvial flooding, the urban flood of TDLC's deep dives, is mostly missing (§C.5.2).
- Sentinel-5P measures a column of NO2, not the air at the surface, and MODIS's Terra record ends on 1 December 2026 [V] (§A.5).
- The Relative Wealth Index is a 2.4 km grid, relative within each country [V] (§B.4.2).
- GHS-POP shows where people live, not where they are at the hour of a flood [M] (§C.5.2).

A question asked finer than its evidence is answered by Estimation, and the work should say so (§D.7.2).

**Biases** recur across missions.

- *Informality.* It is missing from registers, rolls, OSM and GTFS, and maps are least complete where vulnerability is greatest (§C.5.2, §D.7.2).
- *Lights and prices.* Lights saturate in cores and underweight agriculture (Henderson, Storeygard and Weil 2012 [V]). Declared prices are understated where transfers are taxed [M] (§D.7.2).
- *Loss databases.* EM-DAT's thresholds (10 deaths, 100 affected, a declaration or an appeal [V]) drop the small, frequent losses that DesInventar records [M]. The two are read together, never summed (§C.5.2).
- *Scorecards.* The Scorecard asks that each score's justification be recorded [V]. The justification becomes the trace and the score its reading (§C.5.2).
- *Self-selection.* Grievance logs record who chose to complain (§B.4.2). Crowdsourced reports select themselves, and damage labels learned from imagery are imbalanced (Gupta et al. 2019 [V]; §F.6.4).
- *Platforms and conflict.* Platforms show offers, not stays (§H.4.2). After conflict, disclosure control and custody protect people (§C.5.2).

**Schemas.** The Risk Data Library Standard describes risk data [V] (§C.3.5, §F.2.2), codebooks after DDI hold survey variables and budget classifications (§B.4.2, §D.5.4), and W3C PROV holds lineage (§E.7.8). tekmerion's `located` holds evidence placed in space and time: a camera, a phone count and a ticket count at one gate in one hour are three reports, not three proofs (§H.4.2).

*Coverage.* archeion (holdings, custody, `datasets`, access rules), tekmerion (`located`, shared origins, channels of credibility) and peira's `disclosure` cover the block. Missing are the readers and the RDLS schema (§4.3).

### 1.3 Interpretation

Most disagreement between diagnostics is over readings of open terms. kybernesis settles them before estimation and carries them as a standing (§A.1). A reading's source is an authority, a convention, an analyst, data or a proposal (§B.4.3, §D.7.3). Readings change rankings: moving shared taxes from "own" to "transfer" can change a city's rank among its peers. Such a conclusion is arguable, and its pivot should be shown (§D.7.3). Ten families of open term recur.

- **The unit.** It may be an administrative boundary, built-up extent, or a unit of the Degree of Urbanisation or of the functional urban area.
  - *Degree of Urbanisation*: urban centres are contiguous 1 km² cells of at least 1,500 inhabitants per km² holding 50,000 people [V].
  - *Functional urban area*: the city plus local units where at least 15 percent of employed residents work in it [V].

  The version of the Degree of Urbanisation is itself a reading (§D.2.6), and every per-capita or per-area index inherits the choice (§A.5, §B.4.3).
- **Reach.** Open public space within 400 m along streets (SDG 11.7.1 [V]) competes with WHO Europe's 0.5–1 ha within 300 m [V] and the proposed 3-30-300 rule [V] (§A.3). Transit is within 500 m or 1 km (SDG 11.2.1 [V]; §B.3.4), jobs within 30, 45 or 60 minutes (§D.3.4), and safety within five minutes where tsunamis arrive quickly [V], at a walking speed slower for the old (§C.5.3).
- **Design events.**
  - *Return periods.* "The 1-in-100-year event" is an exceedance probability, a mean recurrence interval, or a probability over a design life. The readings part under non-stationarity (Milly et al. 2008 [V]; Salas and Obeysekera 2014 [V]; §F.2.4).
  - *Joint events.* Joint return periods have three readings: either driver exceeds, both do, or a level set of the copula (§F.3.2).
  - *Japan after 2011.* Japan read "design event" twice: evacuation for the largest class, structures for the frequent class [V]. It is the clearest declared reading in the survey (§C.4.2).
- **Harm.** "Affected" is direct or indirect (Sendai [V]). A "disaster" may be defined by EM-DAT's criteria [V], by a national declaration that also triggers a Cat DDO [V], or by a parametric index, "with money depending on it" (§C.5.3). Damage, loss, need and "recovered" each have several readings (§C.5.3, §F.4.3).
- **Cover.**
  - *Insured*: MOF's attachment rate (69.7%) or household penetration (35.1%) [V].
  - *Seismically adequate*: built after 1981, or diagnosed so [V].
  - *Covered by a community organisation*: within its activity area, or covered by one shown active (§C.4.3–§C.4.8).
- **Housing and tenure.**
  - *Tenure*: documented, perceived (cut at "likely or very likely" [V]) or de facto.
  - *Affordable*: cost at most 30% of income, a price-to-income ratio at most 3.0, or rent at most 25% of income [V].
  - *Who counts*: "the poor"; "disabled", cut at "a lot of difficulty" by the Washington Group [V]; and "vulnerable", identified project by project under ESS10 [V] (§B.4.3).
- **Money.** Own-source revenue may include shared taxes or not, on which SNG-WOFI cautions [V]. Debt may include leases, utilities and PPP commitments, and cost recovery may be on operating or on full cost. Value captured depends on the counterfactual of its uplift (§D.3.7, §D.7.3). In appraisal the open terms are the discount schedule, the value of a statistical life, distributional weights, the threshold, the numeraire and the horizon. A changed discount rate is a different operator, not an admissible transformation (§G.1).
- **Green.** A "controlled" waste facility (an operational ladder [V]); a city's emissions (territorial, BASIC, BASIC+ or consumption-based [V]); the grid factor; "hot" (WBGT 33 [V], or 35 [S]) (§A.5).
- **Tourism.** Visitor and usual environment (IRTS [M]). Tourism's contribution is the TSA's direct value added [V] or the WTTC's total [S], and the "over 10 percent of global GDP" in TDLC's remit is the latter (§D.2.8). Carrying capacity gives way to limits of acceptable change (Stankey et al. 1985 [V]; §H.4.3).
- **Knowledge.** Capacity, uptake (Weiss 1979 [V]), contribution (Mayne 2012 [V]) and "transferable" (Cartwright and Hardie 2012 [V]; Pearl and Bareinboim 2014 [V]). "Contributing to 430 projects … worth $75 billion" is linkage, not effect (§E.2.2, §E.7.3).

Who reads matters as much as what is read. After conflict, readings may come from parties to it (§C.5.3). An interval reading of a Likert scorecard is an analyst's (§B.3.9).

*Coverage.* tekmerion's `construal` reads a finding under every combination of readings and reports its standing and pivots, and the needs domain already declares points of interpretation (§B.4.3). What is missing is not the mechanism but a catalogue of readings as data, each with its source. The parts' tables of open terms begin it (§B.4.3, §D.7.3).

### 1.4 Estimation

Disaster risk is never observed. It is a distribution of losses implied by hazard, exposure and vulnerability (§C.2.1). Soundness then has three consequences (§F.1.2).

1. Point estimates from one chain of models are unsound.
2. Epistemic uncertainty is a set before it is a law (Der Kiureghian and Ditlevsen 2009 [V]).
3. Coverage must be tested.

The chapters show why.

- *Damage functions dominate.* Flood damage is most sensitive to the depth–damage function (Jongman et al. 2012 [V]), and across 272 functions damage is uncertain by a factor of 2 to 5 (Wagenaar et al. 2016 [V]).
- *Hazard models disagree.* Six global flood models agree on extent only 30–40% of the time (Trigg et al. 2016 [V]; §F.2.5).
- *Means mislead.* A logic tree's mean curve is a weighted average of curves none of which need be true (Bommer and Scherbaum 2008 [V]; §F.2.3).

Two working rules apply to every mission (§D.7.4). Each estimate enters Evaluation with its interval. Each estimate trained elsewhere is a transfer, whose support factors are checked first.

The state that is better estimated than observed is, by mission:

- **Hazard.** Hazard curves (Cornell 1968 [V]) are read for Japan from J-SHIS [V]. Event sets carry their loss tables (Mitchell-Wallace et al. 2017 [V]). Shaking after an event is conditioned on stations (Worden et al. 2018 [V]), which is tyche's Gaussian conditioning (§F.2, §F.8.1).
- **Exposure.**
  - Footprints and heights, with their error carried and withheld outside the model's area of applicability (§F.6.1).
  - Daytime population, by dasymetric carriage (§F.8.5).
  - The visitor stock by place and hour, which no register holds; exposure is "this stock at the wrong hour" (§H.4.4).
- **Vulnerability.** Fragility is fitted by building class (Porter, Kennedy and Bachman 2007 [V]; Baker 2015 [V]) and updated by observed damage (Singhal and Kiremidjian 1998 [V]). Transferred depth–damage functions (Huizinga, de Moel and Szewczyk 2017 [V]) are kept as a credal set (§F.6.2, §F.6.3).
- **Networks and behaviour.**
  - Functionality after a shock, from partial observations (§F.8.3).
  - Cascades between networks (Buldyrev et al. 2010 [V]).
  - Indirect loss, by an adaptive input–output model (Hallegatte 2008 [V]; §F.3.3).
  - Departure, as a law conditioned on surveys and drills (Lindell and Perry 2012 [V]; §F.8.4).
- **Well-being, recovery and capacity.**
  - Household well-being losses (Hallegatte, Bangalore and Vogt-Schilb 2016 [V]; Markhvida et al. 2020 [V]; §F.7.2).
  - Recovery, as survival on records of reopenings (§C.5.4).
  - Institutional capacity, an agency's capacity factors and residents' sentiment, as latent traits measured by ordinal items rather than summed levels (§C.5.4, §E.7.4, §H.4.4).
- **Inclusion.**
  - Small-area poverty (Corral et al. 2022 [V]; Fay and Herriot 1979 [V]; Elbers, Lanjouw and Lanjouw 2003 [V]; Molina and Rao 2010 [V]).
  - Wealth from imagery and traces (Jean et al. 2016 [V]). Chi et al. 2022 [V] report R² ≈ 0.56 under spatially stratified validation, relative within each country.
  - Informal rents, and perceived tenure by area.
  - Slum extent, with the warning that morphology seen from space is not deprivation and that tenure is never visible (Kuffer, Pfeffer and Sliuzas 2016 [V]).

  Distance to services is better computed than surveyed (§B.4.4).
- **Green.** PM2.5 over people, by fusion (DIMAQ [V]); sectoral emissions, where bottom-up and downscaled inventories can part widely [V]; uncollected waste, WBGT and building energy (§A.5).
- **Productivity and finance.**
  - Output by place, downscaled with lights (Henderson, Storeygard and Weil 2012 [V]).
  - Employment by place of work, by dasymetric allocation and small areas.
  - Commuting zones, by calibrated spatial interaction.
  - The land value surface, by hedonic models with spatial dependence.
  - The agglomeration elasticity, from a meta-analytic prior (Melo, Graham and Noland 2009 [V]). Sorting (Combes, Duranton and Gobillon 2008 [V]) makes a city's own area-level estimate an upper bound [M].
  - Tax potential, cash flows and debt capacity under declared scenarios (§D.7.4).
- **Tourism.**
  - Spending and impact, through input–output with leakage. Impact studies are often commissioned to legitimise a position (Crompton 2006 [V]), and CGE is preferred to multipliers (Dwyer, Forsyth and Spurr 2004 [V]).
  - Demand ahead (Song, Qiu and Park 2019 [V]; Nguyen 2020 [V]).
  - Housing pressure (Garcia-López et al. 2020 [V]; §H.4.4).
- **Knowledge.** The persistence of action-plan items, by survival with censoring; contribution, by process tracing; counterfactuals, confounded by the selection of participants, with sensitivity bounds (§E.7.4).

Signs of approaching critical transitions are not sound beliefs that a transition is near. Variance and autocorrelation gave true positives in 9% and 13% of 126 ecological series (Burthe et al. 2016 [V]). Rate- and noise-induced tipping are not announced by slowing down (Ashwin et al. 2012 [V]). The signs' honest role is to trigger an inquiry, with the false-alarm rate stated (§F.4.2). Basin stability (Menck et al. 2013 [V]) is usable once a model is declared (§F.4.5).

*Coverage.* Most estimators exist:

- *tyche*: change of support, conditioning, the area of applicability, `bayes`, `uncertainty`, `latent`, `graphical` and exceedance curves;
- *peira*: `small_areas` (Fay–Herriot) and `survival`;
- *kinesis*: `scales`, basins and signs of criticality;
- *nomos*: `interaction`, `microsimulation` and `psychometrics`;
- *urban science*: `exposure`, `demand` and `access`, with the Lifelines plug-in.

The missing ones are listed in §4.

### 1.5 Evaluation

Evaluation applies operators in parallel to one estimate. Each operator has a declared scale type and judges against a standard held by someone. Each finding has a standing over readings and a measure (§A.1, §D.7.5, §G.1).

**Ordinal judgements are never averaged.** On this the chapters agree more firmly than on anything else.

- *SDG 11.3.2.* It averages five evaluators' Likert scores and counts a city above 2.5. Two recodings that keep the order of answers can put a city on either side (§B.3.9).
- *São Paulo's index of exclusion.* It summed scores of −1, 0 and +1, and the Bank recorded economists' reservations [V] (§B.1.2).
- *The Scorecard.* Its scores "are not normative and therefore not comparable across different cities" [V] (§C.2.4).
- *INFORM.* Its geometric means treat normalised indicators as ratio quantities, a reading to declare [V] (§C.3.10).
- *SoVI.* It is at best ordinal within one computation (Spielman et al. 2020 [V]; §F.7.1).
- *Other scorecards.* PEFA, credit ratings, IGIF and LGAF are ordinal (§D.7.5). AHP reverses ranks when an option is added (Belton and Gear 1983 [S]; §G.4).

The admissible operators follow (§A.5, §B.4.5, §C.2.4).

- Within a city, a level is compared over time.
- Across criteria, the criteria are counted at each level, or their median taken.
- A composite is formed only within a scale type, or by a non-compensatory rule.

Some levels are thresholds in disguise. The Scorecard's P9.1 gives level 3 when over 90% are reachable by warnings, level 2 over 75% and level 1 over half [V]. The quantity is estimated first, and the cut points applied after (§C.3.2).

**One estimate supports parallel evaluation spaces.** Assets, services and well-being rank cities differently (§C.2.2).

- *Services.* Disruptions of power, water and transport cost households and firms in low- and middle-income countries $391–647 billion a year (Hallegatte, Rentschler and Rozenberg 2019 [V]).
- *Well-being.* The poorest 20% bear about 11% of asset losses but 47% of well-being losses [V].

The triple dividend and well-being losses are alternative operators on one estimate (§G.3). Distributional weights are reported as a standing over η ∈ {0, 1, 1.25, 2} (§G.7).

**Every standard is held by someone.**

- *Normative standards*, held by authorities:
  - WHO's PM2.5 guideline of 5 µg/m³ [V] (§A.3);
  - UN-Habitat's thresholds (§B.3);
  - Japan's ladder for the real debt service ratio (notification below 18%, permission at 18%, a soundness plan at 25%, rehabilitation at 35% [V]), which Chapter D calls the clearest example of declared thresholds held by an authority (§D.6.3);
  - Japan's retrofit targets [V] (§C.5.5);
  - GSTC's destination criteria [V] (§H.2.3);
  - ADB's minimum rate of return of 9%, or 6% for social projects [V] (§G.2).
- *Relative standards.* Sendai's "substantially reduce" is judged against a city's own baseline (§C.5.5).
- *Comparative standards.* These need their reference group declared (§B.4.5).
- *Felt and expressed standards.* Surveys and grievance logs carry them. Resident satisfaction is now a mandatory KPI of Japan's registered DMOs [S], which changes who holds the standard (§H.3.2).
- *Financiers' standards.* The Cat DDO's access criteria are one example [V] (§C.3.7).

Bradshaw's four kinds organise these standards (§B.4.5, §C.5.5). "Overtourism here" may be clear under residents' standard and untenable under operators' (§H.4.5).

**Economic evaluation consists of operators on scales.** A net present value is ratio-scale money, multi-attribute value is interval at best, and outranking is ordinal. Chapter G proposes admission tests on a finite instance, among them invariance under adding a dominated option (§G.4). Kirkpatrick's levels are ordinal too, and Most Significant Change makes explicit whose values judge (§E.7.5).

*Coverage.* Most of the block is covered by nomos's `measurement` and `appraisal`, kybernesis's kernel of scales and meaningfulness, the needs domain, urban science's catalogue of indexes and risk management's `criticality`. Missing are ordinal measurement models (not built, ADR 0128), declared-scheme scorecards, limits of acceptable change, outranking and distributional weights (§4).

### 1.6 Policy Making

**Options.** They come from the repertoire (§3), filtered by what evaluation found: a problem selects the actions whose mechanism acts on its state variable (§D.7.6). They also come from catalogues such as UN Tourism's 68 measures [V] (§H.2.2), from communities' own proposals (§B.4.6), and from search by heuresis. A model's proposal enters only as grade C (§A.5).

**Appraisal.** Development practice appraises by cost–benefit analysis at economic prices (Belli et al. 2001 [V]; ADB 2017 [V]), though IEG found its use at appraisal falling for decades [V] (§G.2). Discount rates are held at different values:

- the World Bank's advisory note ties the rate to growth, so 3% growth gives 6% [V];
- the Green Book sets 3.00%, then 2.50% and 2.25% [V];
- MLIT used 4% "for the time being", adopted reference values of 1% and 2% from FY2024, and took up a review in October 2026 [V] (§G.2).

The reading set is plural and unstable, so standing is reported over it. Chapter D adds four appraisals (§D.5.5, §D.7.6):

1. wider economic benefits, with a transferred agglomeration elasticity;
2. fiscal impact;
3. the feasibility of value capture: uplift times stock times capture rate, discounted;
4. debt sustainability.

**Uncertainty.** *Lifelines*' 3,000 scenarios put the benefit–cost ratio above 1 in 96% of them and above 2 in 77% [V] (§C.3.6). Under deep uncertainty five methods are available (§F.5):

- robust decision making (Lempert, Popper and Bankes 2003 [V]);
- scenario discovery (Bryant and Lempert 2010 [V]);
- adaptive pathways (Haasnoot et al. 2013 [V]);
- decision scaling (Brown et al. 2012 [V]);
- info-gap, with Sniedovich's (2010 [V]) objection that a horizon around a poor estimate may exclude the truth.

Robustness metrics rank differently, so the metric is a reading (McPhail et al. 2018 [S]; §G.5).

**Allocation** (§G.6).

- *Indivisibility.* Funding down a score ranking is not optimal for indivisible projects, and cost inside the score double-counts the budget (Kaganova 2011 [V]).
- *Portfolios.* Portfolios are knapsack and integer programmes (Salo, Keisler and Morton 2011 [V]). Robust portfolio modelling sorts projects into core, borderline and exterior over admissible weights, which is a standing over readings (Liesiö, Mild and Salo 2007 [S]).
- *Participatory budgets.* They may be divided by the method of equal shares (Peters, Pierczyński and Skowron 2021 [V]).
- *Risk finance.* It is layered, because "no single financial instrument can provide suitable and affordable financial protection against all possible risks" [V] (§C.3.7). Instruments are compared by economic opportunity cost, including late funds (Clarke et al. 2016 [V]).
- *PPPs.* A PPP must pass a CBA and beat a contestable public comparator (PPP Reference Guide 3.0 [V]).
- *Double counting.* Capitalised land gains are not added to the user benefits they capitalise [M].

Selection adopts, rejects or studies first by the value of information, under a debt ceiling and a capital budget (§D.7.6). Induced participation works best with a responsive state (Mansuri and Rao 2013 [V], findings [S]).

**Transfer.** A practice transfers when its support factors hold in the target (Cartwright and Hardie 2012 [V]). A transport formula on a selection diagram decides when an effect can be computed there (Pearl and Bareinboim 2014 [V]; §E.5.3). Land readjustment abroad is the best-documented record (De Souza, Ochi and Hosono 2018 [V]; §E.5.5). Thailand took about seventeen years from study to Act. Malaysia carried out no project, since expropriation was a cheaper substitute. Colombia's Law 388 and its returned trainees produced about fifty projects by 2013. From these cases Chapter E reads nine support factors (§E.5.6):

1. a legal basis;
2. no cheaper substitute;
3. simple enough tenure;
4. self-financing demand;
5. champions employed as counterparts;
6. political priority;
7. a local adapter;
8. pilots that are recorded and evaluated;
9. time.

It also names five failure modes (§E.5.4):

- isomorphic mimicry (Andrews, Pritchett and Woolcock 2013 [V]);
- uninformed, incomplete or inappropriate transfer (Dolowitz and Marsh 2000 [V], the types [S]);
- urban policy tourism (González 2011 [V]);
- an idealised model presented abroad (Sorensen 2000 [V], content [S]);
- linked lending taken for effect.

Each chapter gives the support factors of its Japanese practices (§A.4, §B.5, §C.5.6, §D.6, §H.3.4). Japan builds beyond the solutions *Lifelines* costs [V] (§C.3.6), so a Japanese level of protection is not the unit that transfers. A transfer whose factors are unestablished is refused, or carried as arguable (§D.7.6).

*Coverage.* The block is covered by the policy domain (`change`, `evidence`, `transfer`, `options`, `adoption`), nomos's `appraisal`, tyche's `decision` with credal sets and minimax regret, calx's knapsack, heuresis, risk management's `financing`, tekmerion's `warrant` and peira's transport. Missing are:

- transfer as a lens in kybernesis, with a register of practices;
- scenario discovery and pathway graphs;
- robust portfolios;
- transferred values and distributional weights (§4).

### 1.7 Actions

An action is a record: its kind, its mechanism as a theory of change, the states it acts on, the evidence that would show it worked, its typical risks and its support factors (design §5.4). The parts' tables of actions hold 103 rows, which §3 merges into sixty. Four observations hold across the missions.

**Displacement is the risk most often named.**

- Upgrading raises rents that displace tenants (§B.4.7).
- Of Ahmedabad's housing-lottery winners, 34% never took up the housing and 32% eventually left (Barnhardt, Field and Pande 2017 [V]; §B.4.7).
- Regulating hazard zones moves building to hazards nobody has mapped (§C.5.7.1).
- New roads attract establishments partly by relocation (Gibbons et al. 2019 [V]; §D.7.7).
- Levies and dispersal push visitors to untaxed lodging and to residents' streets (§H.6.2).

Flood defences invite the levee effect (Di Baldassarre et al. 2015 [S]). A response that raises vulnerability elsewhere is maladaptation (Barnett and O'Neill 2010 [S]; §C.5.7.1).

**Institutional actions show their evidence slowly.** Their typical risk is "a mandate without a budget" (§C.5.7.5). Plans that are unused, unfunded, or filed and forgotten recur across the repertoires (§A.7.3, §C.8, §E.9).

**Inquiries are actions on the belief.** They include:

- enumerations, survey modules and pilots (§B.4.7);
- assessments, drills and post-event reviews (§C.5.7.6);
- diagnostics, valuations and studies (§D.7.7);
- site investigations, inventories and sensors (§F.9.1);
- resident surveys, a pilot levy and a purchase of data (§H.4.7).

Their worth is judged before they are made, by the expected value of perfect or sample information (Straub 2014 [V]). Sobol' indices show which uncertainty dominates the quantity, and scenario discovery which changes the decision; only the second bears on worth (§F.9.2). A randomised pilot shows what an inquiry can buy. Anticipatory cash before a forecast flood in Bangladesh made recipients 36% less likely to go a day without eating (Pople et al. 2021 [V]; §F.9.1).

**The knowledge programme has its own repertoire.** Deep dives, peer exchange, twinning, expert dispatch, notes, action plans, training with follow-up and pilot grants act on capacity and on the design of operations (§E.7.7). Pilots that are not recorded or evaluated spread nothing, as JICA's ex-post evaluation of BOSAI found [V] (§E.5.5).

*Coverage.* The policy domain's `options` and `adoption`, the Lifelines plug-in, tyche's `decision` and risk management's `financing` cover the block. Missing are a register of practices and actions, the design of levies and fares, and declared limits with triggered actions (§4).

### 1.8 Results Capture

For kybernesis, results capture is the return path from decision to evidence (§D.7.8). The chapters share seven principles.

- **Designs are declared before results arrive.** peira's `protocol` and archeion's sealed journal hold them (§A.5, §C.5.8, §D.7.8, §H.4.8), because promoters' impact studies overstate (Crompton 2006 [V]; §H.4.8).
- **Readings are frozen at baseline.** A reading changed at follow-up breaks comparability, which archeion's `datasets` reports between versions of a codebook (§D.7.8).
- **The designs are the familiar ones.** They are matched differences in differences, as for Tokyo's diesel low emission zone [V] (§A.4); staggered adoption against units not yet reached; regression discontinuity (Michaels et al. 2021 [V]); synthetic control (Drugova, Kim and Jakus 2021 [V]; Cavallo et al. 2013 [V]); and interference by rings for spillovers and displacement (§B.4.8, §D.7.8, §H.4.8). The operational reference is Gertler et al. 2016 [V].
- **Results are measured on people, not places.** Displacement is checked by tracking people, because "an area's improvement can be the replacement of its residents" (§B.4.8).
- **Disaster risk needs designs for rare events.** Results are slow and rarely attributable, and like events are compared (§C.5.8).
  - A model's estimate of avoided loss inherits the vulnerability model's factor of 2 to 5 unless the model is back-tested.
  - In 45 pairs of events, a second, much more hazardous event almost always did more harm (Kreibich et al. 2022 [V]).
  - A decade of exceedance counts cannot confirm a 1-in-100-year loss, so an avoided loss from an unvalidated model is arguable (§F.10).
  - About $11 is saved per $1 for adopting current model codes in the United States (NIBS 2019 [V]), though Mechler (2016 [V]) calls the evidence base limited (§F.10.2).
- **Appraisals are checked against outcomes.** JICA recalculates rates of return ex post [V]. Reference class forecasting (Flyvbjerg 2006 [V]) is disputed [S] (§G.8).
- **Knowledge results are contributions, not attributions.** IEG finds that country self-evaluations rarely cover the use of advisory work [S]. A sealed journal of outcome statements and contribution claims, written as W3C PROV, would let a programme select formats on what was substantiated rather than what was linked (§E.7.8).

Chapter D adds three points (§D.7.8).

- Japan's yearly publication of every authority's fiscal soundness ratios [V] is "the strongest form of results capture for fiscal reforms".
- Land values respond within a year or two and the fiscal loop only after revaluation [M].
- Measured effects become the priors of the next city's transfer.

*Coverage.* The block is covered by peira's `quasi`, `interference`, `protocol`, `sensitivity` and `synthesis`; archeion's journal and `datasets`; zetesis's `coding` and `tracing`; and tyche's testing and scoring. Missing are the back-testing of exceedance curves, Sendai's indicators from loss databases, and records of outcome statements and contribution claims (§4).

## 2. Where the Missions Meet

The design holds that integration happens when several plug-ins run on one place and feed one belief about it (design §2.1). Read together, the chapters show eight places where missions trade against each other or support each other, and which no single chapter reports as such.

### 2.1 Density Serves Productivity and the Climate, and Raises Exposure

The operative variable of the productive city is effective density, the jobs and firms reachable at a given travel cost (§D.1.1). Compact urban form is a mitigation wedge [V] (§A.3), and Toyama's compact strategy raised the centre's share of residents from 28% to 39% while CO2 fell [V] (§A.4). The same density has three costs.

- *Crowding.* Density without access is crowding, not agglomeration: African cities are "crowded, disconnected and costly" (Lall, Henderson and Venables 2017 [V]; §D.2.2).
- *Heat.* Urban areas of 100 East Asian cities are about 2 °C warmer, with gaps of up to 7 °C within a city [V] (§A.2).
- *Exposure.* Whatever stands where a hazard reaches is exposure (§C.2.1).

Japan's 2020 amendments join the readings: red zones are excluded in principle from residential inducement areas, and location normalisation plans must carry a disaster prevention guideline [S] (§C.4.7). Density is therefore read by the productive, green and resilient operators side by side on one estimate, never by one alone.

### 2.2 The Fiscal Loop Against Tenure and Displacement

Public investment in access raises land values, which property taxes and capture instruments recover (§D.1.2). The same uplift does three other things.

- It raises rents that push tenants out of upgraded settlements (§B.4.7).
- Titles can set off sales to wealthier buyers (§B.4.7).
- Land readjustment's consent rule counts owners and leaseholders of land, not building tenants or informal occupiers (§B.5.3).

Capture also depends on rising land values (§B.4.7, §D.6.2), so it is weakest where decline, not growth, is the problem, as it was in Toyama (§A.4). Value captured and residents retained are two operators on one estimate. Results capture must track who left (§B.4.8), and a transfer of readjustment must state its rule for tenants (§B.5.3; UN-Habitat 2016 [S], §E.5.5).

### 2.3 Tourism Against Housing and Resilience

Tourism turns a visitor flow into revenue and jobs: Kyoto expects about ¥12.6 billion a year from its revised levy [V] (§D.6.5). It also does three harms.

- It raises rents. In Barcelona, Airbnb raised rents 1.9% on average and 7% in the top decile (Garcia-López et al. 2020 [V]; §H.4.4).
- It puts a population that no register holds where hazards arrive (§H.4.4).
- It concentrates risk. Dependence on few origins is a risk (§D.7.5), and disasters had large, prolonged effects on Japan's arrivals (Nguyen 2020 [V]; §H.4.4).

Even seasonality's direction is a reading: lower is usually better, though a low season gives a destination time to recover [V] (§D.3.8). Japan's DMOs must now report residents' satisfaction beside visitors' [S] (§H.3.2). Chapter H proposes that the visitor stock become a population for the evacuation and warning modules (§H.5).

### 2.4 Ordinal Scorecards Cannot Be Averaged into One Quality

Each mission is assessed partly by ordinal instruments:

- resilience by the Scorecard and the City Resilience Index (§C.3.2, §C.3.3);
- land, finance and spatial data by LGAF, PEFA and IGIF, and creditworthiness by ratings (§D.7.5);
- participation by SDG 11.3.2 (§B.3.9);
- green building by EDGE's levels (§A.5);
- destinations by resident satisfaction (§H.4.5);
- vulnerability by SoVI (§F.7.1).

A composite score of a city's greenness, inclusiveness, resilience and productivity would sum across scale types and across cities that the instruments themselves forbid comparing (§C.2.4). The design's refusal of a default composite follows (design §2.5). Where a comparison is wanted, the admissible forms are:

- counts at each level;
- non-compensatory rules;
- robust portfolio classes over admissible weights (§G.4, §G.6).

### 2.5 Asset Losses Against Well-Being Losses

Ranked by avoided asset losses, resilience investments favour wealthy districts. Ranked by well-being losses, they favour informal settlements (§C.2.2, §F.7.2). Three practices tilt the choice towards assets:

- retrofit "reaching the wealthy first" is a named risk (§C.5.7.1);
- MLIT's flood manual admits only monetisable categories into the benefit–cost ratio [V] (§G.2);
- building back better can exclude renters and the informal (§C.5.7.3).

The inclusive and resilient missions meet in the choice of evaluation operator. The chapters advise holding asset, service and well-being losses as parallel spaces, with distributional weights reported as a standing (§C.2.2, §G.7).

### 2.6 Green Infrastructure Serves Several Missions and Competes for Land

Nature-based solutions cool streets (§A.7.3) and attenuate floods (§C.5.7.1). They also have four costs (§A.7.3, §C.8):

- they need land, water and maintenance;
- they perform uncertainly at extremes;
- they compete with housing and density for land;
- they may displace residents [M].

Defences, the grey alternative, invite the levee effect (§C.5.7.1). Land-use regulation in hazard zones is the complement of both.

### 2.7 Informality Is Mismeasured by Every Mission in the Same Places

The same places fall out of every mission's evidence:

- informal firms are missing from registers (productive);
- informal households from censuses (inclusive);
- informal buildings from OSM, where vulnerability is greatest (resilient);
- informal transit from GTFS;
- private lodging from accommodation statistics (§B.4.1, §C.5.2, §D.7.2, §H.3.5).

One inquiry, such as an enumeration or a roll matched to building footprints, can therefore serve several missions at once (§B.4.7, §D.8.1). Its worth should be judged across all the decisions it informs, not one.

### 2.8 Fiscal Capacity and the Cadastre Are Support Factors of Nearly Every Transfer

Budgets for operation and maintenance carry drainage (§C.5.6), nature-based solutions (§A.7.3) and transit (§D.7.7). Recorded boundaries carry four further instruments (§D.7.7, §B.2):

- the property tax;
- value capture;
- readjustment;
- tenure security.

Contingent credit adds debt when fiscal space is weakest (§G.9.3). Japan's cadastre covers 27% of its densely inhabited districts beside a land market that works [V] (§D.3.9). Spatial data maturity and cadastral coverage are therefore kept apart, and land readjustment needs agreed boundaries within its scheme, not a complete national cadastre (§D.6.2). Municipal finance diagnostics measure the capacity to run the fiscal loop (§D.1.2), on which these instruments draw.

## 3. The Repertoire of Actions

The table merges the actions of Chapters A to H; duplicates are joined, and inquiries are grouped into three rows. "Mission" names the missions an action serves. The chapters' tables give each action's evidence of success and typical risks, which are omitted here for space.

| Action | Mission | Mechanism | States it acts on | Key support factors | Example |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Transit with transit-oriented development | Green, productive | Lower travel cost raises effective density and access; homes and floor space near stations | Modal share, CO2, access, land values, location of jobs | Right of way; co-finance; operator's right to develop land; transport and planning integrated; growth | Toyama's Portram [V] (§A.4); Tama Den-en-toshi [S], Hong Kong's Rail plus Property [V] (§D.7.7) |
| Solid waste: reduction, separation and controlled disposal | Green | Enforceable separation and norms cut generation; a low-cost landfill raises the level of control | Waste generated, share controlled, incineration, methane | Enforceable separation; neighbourhood associations; operating budget and staff | Yokohama G30, waste −43% [V]; Fukuoka method [V] (§A.4, §A.7.3) |
| Low emission zone | Green | Exclude high-emitting vehicles | Particulates, fleet | Vehicle inspection; retrofit supply | Greater Tokyo 2003 [V] (§A.4) |
| Facility cap-and-trade | Green | Tradable obligations against base years | Facility energy and CO2 | Metered, verified data; concentrated ownership; local legal basis | Tokyo 2010– [V] (§A.4) |
| Green building certification and procurement | Green | A buyer's standard for buildings | Building energy | Procurement law; certifiers | IFC EDGE [V] (§A.2) |
| Non-revenue water reduction | Green | District metering and leak repair | Non-revenue water, utility revenue | A utility leader; government backing | Phnom Penh, 72% to 6% with Kitakyushu [V] (§A.4) |
| Nature-based solutions and greening through permits | Green, resilient | Shade, evapotranspiration, retention, attenuation of hazards | Canopy, surface temperature, runoff, natural buffers | Land; maintenance; species; permitting capacity; monitoring | Tokyo's roof-greening ordinance [S] (§A.4); Scorecard Essential 5 [V] (§C.5.7.1) |
| Early warning, heat alerts included | Resilient, green | Lead time and information turn exposure into protective action | Exposure at impact; heat illness | Observation networks; a mandated service; dissemination channels | JMA's five levels [V] (§C.4.4); WBGT 33 alert [V] (§A.3); CREWS [V] (§C.3.8) |
| In-situ slum upgrading | Inclusive | Services and housing improved in place, residents kept | Deprivations, health, safety, land values | Organised communities and savings groups; a lender to communities; collective land | Baan Mankong (Boonyabancha 2005 [V]); cement floors (Cattaneo et al. 2009 [V]) (§B.4.7) |
| Sites and services | Inclusive, land | Serviced greenfield plots built incrementally | Future slum formation, layout, housing quality | Fringe land within reach of jobs; trunk infrastructure finance; allocation rules that hold | Tanzania (Michaels et al. 2021 [V]) (§B.4.7) |
| Tenure regularisation and fit-for-purpose registration | Inclusive, land | Rights recorded on a continuum reduce the risk of eviction | Documented and perceived tenure, investment | Legal form for the rights recorded; low cost; joint titling; general boundaries | Field 2007 [V]; Payne et al. 2009 [V]; FIG Publication 60 [V] (§B.4.7) |
| Land readjustment and urban redevelopment | Land, productive, inclusive | Plots pooled and re-plotted; land contributed for roads; reserve plots sold | Parcels, infrastructure, land values, tenure | Recorded title within the scheme; consent rule; accepted valuation; buyers for reserve land; a rule for tenants; councils | Shin-nagata, Kobe [V] (§B.5.3); Thailand, Nepal, Colombia [V] (§E.5.5) |
| Housing finance, subsidies and rental policy | Inclusive | Subsidies and state-built units; security for tenants and small landlords | Housing quality, affordability, location; tenants' tenure | Well-located land; targeting registers; enforceable low-cost rental contracts | Ahmedabad lottery (Barnhardt et al. 2017 [V]); Gilbert 2016 [V] (§B.4.7) |
| Targeted fares and accessible transit | Inclusive | Fare discounts; accessible stops and vehicles | Accessibility with cost; gender and disability gaps | Targeting register; smart cards; operators compensated | Bogotá (Guzman and Oviedo 2018 [V]) (§B.4.7) |
| Universal design and barrier-free standards | Inclusive | Standards for new works; area plans made with councils that include disabled people | Access for disabled and older people | Enforcing authority; retrofit finance; disability organisations on councils; published monitoring | Act No. 91 of 2006 [V] (§B.5.2) |
| Participatory budgeting | Inclusive | Residents allocate part of the capital budget | Allocation, participation, services | Discretionary capital budget; responsive government | Brazil (Gonçalves 2014 [V]); method of equal shares [V] (§B.4.7, §G.6) |
| Grievance and feedback mechanisms | Inclusive, knowledge | Logged cases, timelines, appeal, mediation | Knowledge of harms; accountability | A party with authority to act; anonymity | ESS10 [V] (§B.4.7, §E.4.2) |
| Social protection: registry targeting and shock-responsive transfers | Inclusive, resilient | Registry-based targeting; transfers that scale up after shocks | Coverage, consumption smoothing | Identification reaching informal residents; data shared between agencies; pre-arranged finance | Lindert et al. 2020 [V] (§B.4.7); Bowen et al. 2020 [V] (§C.3.7) |
| Machizukuri and community-driven councils | Inclusive | Certified neighbourhood bodies propose and agree plans | Participation, fit of plans, legitimacy | Ordinance recognition; duty to consider proposals; expert dispatch; a rule on whom a council represents | Kobe, 56 councils and 20 agreements [V] (§B.5.1) |

| Risk retention: reserves and contingency budgets | Resilient, economic | Retain frequent, low-severity losses | Fiscal liquidity | Fiscal discipline | Risk layering [V] (§C.3.7); World Bank 2014 [V] (§G.6) |
| Contingent credit | Resilient, economic | Liquidity on a declaration, conditioned on a reform programme | Fiscal liquidity | Macroeconomic framework; DRM programme | Cat DDO [V] (§C.3.7) |
| Risk transfer: insurance, parametric cover, catastrophe bonds | Resilient, economic | Indemnity or fast index-based payouts for rare, severe events | Funding of losses | Indices; premiums; government reinsurance; trust in payouts | CCRIF, about US$500 m paid [V] (§C.3.7); Japan's earthquake insurance (§C.4.8) |
| Risk information systems and open risk data | Resilient, spatial data | Shared hazard, exposure, vulnerability and loss data | Knowledge of the state | Data standards; custodians | RDLS [V]; ThinkHazard! [V] (§C.3.5) |
| Zoning and land-use reform | Productive, land | Raise permitted density | Supply of floor space, prices | Demand; infrastructure headroom; enforcement | Auckland 2016 (Greenaway-McGrevy and Phillips 2023 [V]) (§D.7.7) |
| Land value capture instruments | Productive, economic, land | Recover part of the uplift public action creates | Municipal revenue; land values; timing of development | Legal basis; valuation capacity; transparent accounts; trust | OECD–Lincoln profiles of 60 countries [V]; Japan's city planning tax [V] (§D.7.7) |
| Property tax reform and own-source revenue | Productive | Wider roll coverage, revaluation, better collection | Roll, collection efficiency, own-source revenue | A roll and cadastre; valuation capacity; legitimate enforcement | Kananga (Balán et al. 2022 [V]) (§D.7.7) |
| Intergovernmental transfers | Productive | Equalising, conditional or performance grants on a predictable formula | Revenue; gaps in fiscal capacity; own effort | National fiscal space; data for a formula of need | Local allocation tax, 25% retained [V] (§D.6.3) |
| Municipal borrowing and creditworthiness | Productive, economic | Long-term finance matched to the life of assets | Debt, capital investment, rating | Borrowing law; local capital market; accounting and audit | Japan's permission threshold of 18% [V]; JFM [V] (§D.6.3, §D.7.7) |
| Public–private partnerships | Productive, economic | Private finance under a contract that allocates risk | Capital stock; contingent liabilities | PPP law and unit; project preparation; accounts of commitments; a contestable comparator | Clean Plaza Fujimi, Shinagawa Season Terrace [V] (§D.7.7); PPP Reference Guide 3.0 [V] (§G.6) |
| Economic zones and cluster policy | Productive | Concentrated infrastructure and incentives | Location of firms, employment | Market access; labour pool; zone governance; fit with existing industry | Tennessee Valley Authority (Kline and Moretti 2014 [V]) (§D.7.7) |
| Rural–urban connectivity | Territorial | Lower trade costs between towns and hinterland | Rural market access; employment structure | Demand in towns; funded maintenance; targeting by Degree of Urbanisation | India's rural roads (Asher and Novosad 2020 [V]) (§D.7.7) |
| Cadastre and spatial data investment | Land, spatial data | Recorded boundaries and rights lower transaction costs | Cadastral and roll coverage; data | Legal recognition of general boundaries; maintenance funding; a mandate | Japan's cadastral survey, 53% and 27% in dense districts [V]; PLATEAU [V] (§D.6.6) |
| Capital investment programming | Economic | Projects selected within an envelope | Asset stock; operation and maintenance | An inventory; an envelope; selection respecting indivisibility | Kaganova 2011 [V] (§G.6) |
| User charges and tariffs | Economic, green | Prices for services | Demand, cost recovery, affordability | Connection subsidies rather than consumption subsidies | Komives et al. 2005 [V] (§G.7) |

| Products, festivals and heritage regeneration | Tourism | New reasons to visit; conservation joined to urban development | Regional nights; spending; condition of assets | A patron and curator; transport to sites; heritage law; residents' consent | Setouchi Triennale [S] (§H.3.4); *The Economics of Uniqueness* [V] (§H.2.1) |
| Regulation of short-term rentals | Tourism, inclusive | Licences or caps on supply | Housing pressure; nights | Enforcement capacity; access to platform data | Barcelona's rents (Garcia-López et al. 2020 [V]) (§H.4.4) |

| Visitor management: crowding information, dispersal, caps, separate services | Tourism, inclusive | Shift or limit visits in time and place; separate visitors' and residents' demand | Crowding; residents' access | Location data; forecasting capacity; a single gate; a fare system | Kyoto Tourism Comfort Map [V]; Mount Fuji's cap [S] (§H.3.3) |

| Inclusion in tourism value chains | Tourism, inclusive | Training, finance and procurement for women, youth and local firms | Distribution of benefits | Childcare; transport; credit | Women hold 54% of tourism jobs [V] (§H.2.2) |
| Technical deep dives and study tours, with action plans | Knowledge | Seeing practice, peer exchange and a deadline to plan | Knowledge, consensus, leaders' commitment; plans implemented | Delegates with authority; the task team leader in the delegation; follow-up funded | TDLC's deep dives [V] (§E.2.2) |
| Twinning and city-to-city cooperation | Knowledge, green | Sustained adaptation with a local partner | Skills, organisational arrangements | Commitment of both cities; a local intermediary; early results | Kitakyushu–Surabaya (Kurniawan et al. 2013 [V]) (§E.5.5); MOEJ City-to-City [V] (§A.4) |
| Expert dispatch and just-in-time advisory | Knowledge | Tacit knowledge brought to a design choice | An operation's design; procedures | A counterpart assigned; timing within preparation | TDLC's Operational Support, 104 projects in Phase 3 [V] (§E.2.3) |

| Inquiry: valuations, inventories, site investigations and sensors | Resilient, productive | Narrow the intervals that matter to a decision | Land values; building classes; site response; functionality | Records and valuers; a declared decision by which worth is judged | Uplift study (§D.7.7); boreholes and sensors (§F.9.1); Straub 2014 [V] (§F.9.2) |

## 4. What the Infrastructure Lacks

The parts propose new modules. They are gathered here without duplicates, by layer. Placing a module, naming it, or founding a domain is the user's decision (CLAUDE.md §6.5), and every placement below is the parts' suggestion or a provisional grouping. "Asked by" names the parts that propose the module. Priorities follow three rules:

- **first**: a first engagement cannot run a mission's evaluation operators without the module, or the module removes a gap that several parts meet;
- **next**: it widens what an engagement can estimate or appraise;
- **later**: it is needed only for particular data or questions.

### 4.1 Foundations

| Module | What it would hold | Priority | Asked by |
|---|---|---|---|
| kybernesis: ordinal evaluation spaces and declared-scheme scorecards | Scorecards (Disaster Resilience Scorecard, City Resilience Index, LGAF, PEFA, IGIF, SDG 11.3.2) as declared schemes of statements; aggregation that respects scale type (medians, counts at each level, non-compensatory rules); each score's justification kept as its trace | first | B, C, D |
| kybernesis: transfer as a lens, with a register of practices | Each practice a mechanism with its support factors, evidence and transfers on record, non-adoptions included; the target read in the source's terms, the source's actions realised in the target, and a tolerance; calling the policy domain's `transfer` and peira's transport | first | E (support factors in A, B, C, D, H) |
| kybernesis: declared limits of acceptable change | Indicators, standards and triggered actions as evaluation operators | next | H |
| nomos and tyche: ordinal measurement models | Ordinal item response and latent models for institutional capacity, capacity factors and residents' sentiment (not built, ADR 0128) | next | C, E, H |
| peira: unit-level small areas | The nested-error model, ELL and the empirical best predictor (already planned) | next | B |
| tyche: extreme values and copulas | Generalised extreme-value and Pareto laws with return levels; copulas with tail dependence; joint return periods under declared readings | next | F |
| tyche: value of information bound to risk models | Preposterior analysis with a set of exceedance curves as prior and a survey of buildings or a sensor network as sample | next | F |
| tyche: back-testing of exceedance curves | Exceedance counts, probability integral transforms, Brier and CRPS scores | next | F |
| tyche or the policy domain: scenario discovery and adaptive pathways | PRIM over ensembles; pathway graphs with tipping points, signposts and triggers; info-gap only as a declared reading, with Sniedovich's caveat | next | F, G |
| nomos: spatial segregation indices | Isolation, exposure and Reardon and O'Sullivan's spatial indices | next | B |
| nomos: shift-share with exposure-robust inference | The decomposition, and Borusyak, Hull and Jaravel's inference | next | D |
| nomos `appraisal`: values and weights | Shadow prices; transfer of the value of a statistical life and of benefits, with their errors; travel cost and contingent valuation; a cost-effectiveness frontier; distributional weights reported over η | next | G |
| nomos `appraisal`: robust portfolios and equal shares | Core, borderline and exterior projects over admissible weights; the method of equal shares for participatory budgets | next | G |
| nomos `appraisal`: outranking and admission tests | ELECTRE and PROMETHEE; an admission test that AHP fails; a public sector comparator; reference class forecasting | later | G |
| tyche: heavy tails and mixed-frequency nowcasting | Clauset's fit with the alternatives kept; MIDAS | later | F, H |
| tyche `panel`: matched worker–firm estimator | Agglomeration with worker and firm effects, only where matched data exist | later | D |
| kinesis: basin stability | The probability of returning after a shock, given a declared model | later | F |
| peira: Washington Group tabulation | A trivial addition to `surveys` | later | B |

# Chapter A. Green Cities

## Part A. The Green Pillar: Evidence, Estimation, Evaluation and Action

*Survey for a plug-in of the Urban Studio on kybernesis (ADR 0130), for an Urban Analyst at TDLC. Draft, 2026-11-19, shortened at the coordinator's request.*

**Markers.** **[V]** verified this session against a primary record (issuer's page, the document, or a DOI resolved through Crossref); **[S]** secondary sources only; **[M]** memory, not verified. Generalities already in *Indexes of cities across scales* (ISO 37120 family, UN-Habitat UMF, SDG 11 in general, OECD/Eurostat, small areas, change of support) are not repeated.

### A.1. The Concept: Green as Operators on an Estimated State

"Green" is a family of judgements, not a state. A city's state (particles over its people, flows of energy and materials, the extent of its land, the temperature of its surfaces, its vegetation, its travel) is observed in part, through mechanisms with biases. A green diagnostic applies evaluation operators in parallel to an estimate of that state, yielding features (land consumption against population growth), needs (residents beyond reach of open space), problems (guideline exceedances), risks (heat exposure of the old) and assets (canopy). Each operator has a scale type and, where it judges, a standard held by someone (WHO, UN-Habitat, IFC, the city's pledge). Most disagreement between diagnostics is over readings of open terms ("city", "green", "accessible", "controlled"), which kybernesis settles before estimation and carries as a standing (clear, arguable, untenable).

The World Bank's *Thriving* (2023) draws on more than 10,000 cities, attributes about 70% of anthropogenic GHG to cities, and organises recommendations as five instruments: information, incentives, insurance, integration, investments [V]. TDLC launched it with *Unlivable* at the Asia Smart Cities Conference, Yokohama, November 2023 [V].

### A.2. Frameworks and Diagnostics

- **GPSC Urban Sustainability Framework** (World Bank, GPSC, GEF; launched 9 Feb 2018): a four-stage process and a Measuring Framework of indicators for benchmarking [V: GEF]; two enabling and four outcome dimensions, natural environment and climate action among them [S]. Its indicator lists: *not yet surveyed* (hosts refused automated access).
- **CURB** (C40 with the World Bank and Bloomberg Philanthropies, built with AECOM): free scenario tool for buildings, energy, transport and waste, consistent with the GPC, with a default dataset a city replaces; piloted in seven cities [V: C40]. Its internals: *not yet surveyed*.
- **GPC** (WRI, C40, ICLEI, 2014; v1.1 aligned with the IPCC 2019 Refinement) [V]: six sectors; scopes by the city boundary; **BASIC** = scopes 1–2 of stationary energy and transport plus scopes 1 and 3 of waste; **BASIC+** adds IPPU, AFOLU, transboundary transport and transmission losses; gaps by notation keys [V].
- **Reporting:** the Global Covenant of Mayors' Common Reporting Framework (Jan 2019; a simplified level; an energy access pillar from 2023) [V]; CDP-ICLEI Track, 973 reporting cities in 2024 [S].
- **EBRD Green Cities** (from 2016): over 50 cities, 36 completed GCAPs, a further €3 bn and a refined methodology in 2024 [V]; GCAP baseline in a Pressure–State–Response frame, indicator count reported as 70 or 35 [S].
- **IFC EDGE:** at least 20% savings in energy, water and embodied energy (Certified), 40% on-site energy (Advanced), 100% renewables or offsets (Zero Carbon) [V].
- **Waste:** *What a Waste 3.0*: 2.56 bn t generated in 2022, 3.86 bn t by 2050 under business as usual, 217 economies and 262 cities, data on Data360 [V]; launched by TDLC on 29 May 2026 with a forthcoming Waste-to-Energy note naming its preconditions [V]. *What a Waste 2.0* [V: DOI 10.1596/978-1-4648-1329-0]. UN-Habitat's **Waste Wise Cities Tool** (2021), seven steps for SDG 11.6.1 [V].
- **NBS and heat:** World Bank NBS catalogue (2021) [S]; IUCN Global Standard for NbS [V: DOI 10.2305/IUCN.CH.2020.08.en]; ESMAP *Primer for Cool Cities* (2020) [S]; *Unlivable* (2023): 100 East Asian cities, urban areas about 2 °C warmer, intra-city gaps up to 7 °C [V: TDLC brief].
- **Japanese lineage:** *Eco² Cities* (2010) [V: DOI 10.1596/978-0-8213-8046-8]; *Transforming Cities with Transit* (2013) [V: DOI 10.1596/978-0-8213-9745-9]; OECD *Compact City Policies* (2012) [V: DOI 10.1787/9789264167865-en] and *Green Growth in Kitakyushu* (2013) [V: DOI 10.1787/9789264195134-en; content S].

### A.3. The Indexes: Definitions and Standards

Detail is in Table 7.1; here only what needs a source.

- **PM2.5 (11.6.2):** population-weighted annual mean, µg/m³, by Degree of Urbanisation; WHO custodian; satellite, model and monitor fusion (DIMAQ) [V: metadata 2023-12-15; DOI 10.1111/rssc.12227]. WHO 2021 guideline 5 µg/m³ annual, 15 µg/m³ 24-hour; interim targets 35, 25, 15, 10 [V].
- **MSW (11.6.1):** collected and managed in controlled facilities ÷ generated (t/day), with sub-indicators and a collection-service ladder; "controlled" is operational, not design, control (basic, improved, full) [V: metadata 2025-04-23].
- **Open public space (11.7.1):** share of built-up area that is open space for public use, free of charge, six size classes, streets counted apart; access within 400 m along streets [V: metadata 2025-04-23]. WHO Europe's rule of thumb differs: green space of at least 0.5–1 ha within 300 m linear distance [V].
- **LCRPGR (11.3.1):** land consumption rate ÷ population growth rate (ln-based), with built-up area per capita and total change as secondary indicators; the metadata warns values below or equal to 1 are not self-interpreting and zero or negative growth breaks the ratio [V].
- **Heat:** surface heat island by a simplified global algorithm [V: DOI 10.1016/j.jag.2018.09.015]; urban exposure in person-days [V: DOI 10.1073/pnas.2024792118]; Japan's Heat Stroke Alert at WBGT 33 by 58 regions [V], a special alert at 35 [S].
- **Canopy:** the 3-30-300 rule is its author's proposal [V: DOI 10.1007/s11676-022-01523-z]; 10 m classifiers disagree [V: DOI 10.3390/rs14164101].
- **Emissions:** consumption-based footprints of 13,000 cities [V: DOI 10.1088/1748-9326/aac72a]; urban form's mitigation wedge [V: DOI 10.1073/pnas.1315545112].
- **Water and sanitation (SDG 6 ladders, non-revenue water):** *not yet surveyed* beyond the Phnom Penh case.

### A.4. Japanese Practice and Support Factors for Transfer

- **Kitakyushu.** From the polluted Dokai Bay to the first Eco-Town (1997) [S]. TDLC's 2020 virtual deep dive (11 countries): household waste down 40% in two decades; City Partnership with TDLC since March 2017 [V]. Its Waterworks Bureau helped Phnom Penh cut non-revenue water from 72% (1993) to 6% [V: JICA]. *Support factors:* citizen–industry–city coalition; national Eco-Town framework; a utility with a leader and government backing [V]; long-serving staff.
- **Toyama** (World Bank/TDLC, Arai et al., © 2020) [V]: Portram LRT (2006, public build, private operation) plus housing incentives; centre's share of residents 28% (2005) to 39%; LRT riders more than doubled; CO2 4.00 Mt (2005) to 3.56 Mt (2017). *Support factors (named by the report):* national programmes fully used, sustained commitment (200+ public meetings), integrated policy, an integrating planning office, strong intergenerational ties; also an existing right of way and decline, not growth, as the problem.
- **Yokohama.** G30 (2002–2010): target −30% from FY2001; waste −43% despite population growth; two of seven incinerators decommissioned and one suspended, about ¥110 bn saved [V: City of Yokohama]. Active in MOEJ's City-to-City Collaboration, which can draw on JCM financing [V]. *Support factors:* an avoided capital cost; enforceable separation; neighbourhood associations.
- **Tokyo cap-and-trade** (2010–): facilities ≥1,500 kL oil-equivalent; base years FY2002–07; reduction rates 8%, 17%, 27%, 50% over four five-year periods; credits tradable [V: TMG outline]. *Support factors:* metered, verified facility data; concentrated ownership; local legal basis.
- **Tokyo diesel LEZ (2003):** matched DiD finds lower particulate concentrations and fewer low birthweights [V: DOI 10.1007/s10640-024-00875-w]; about −7% PM10 [S]. **Roof greening ordinance** (2001/2009): 20–25% of roofs on sites over 1,000 m²; about 180 ha [S: C40]. *Support factors:* vehicle inspection; permits as control points.
- **TDLC deep dive on circular waste:** Fukuoka semi-aerobic landfill as a low-cost alternative to waste-to-energy; action plans followed up [V].

### A.5. The Blocks of kybernesis

**5.1 Collection.** Designs and mechanisms: WaCT's stratified cluster survey (90 households: 10 per area, 3 areas per income stratum, 8 days, margin 10%; 150 for megacities) [V]; household travel surveys (Tokyo PT, 2018) [S]; facility registries with verification (Tokyo) [V]; monitors sited by authorities (WHO database about 6,000 settlements) [V]; satellite overpasses (daytime, clear sky; Landsat ST cannot be made at night) [V]; participatory mapping (UN-Habitat's Kobo form for 11.7.1) [V]. Inquiries are themselves actions (5.7).

**5.2 Evidence.**

| Source | Resolution, period | Licence | Limits |
|---|---|---|---|
| EDGAR 2024 [V] | 0.1°, 1970–2023 | CC BY 4.0; IEA CO2 CC BY-NC-ND | Proxy-downscaled |
| Sentinel-5P NO2 [V] | 3.5 × 5.5 km since Aug 2019 | Free (Copernicus) | Column, not surface |
| ACAG PM2.5 V6.GL.02.04 [V] | 0.01°, 1998–2023 | CC BY 4.0 | Finest gradients unresolved |
| CAMS reanalysis [V: DOI 10.5194/acp-19-3515-2019] | Coarse [M] | Copernicus [M] | Not intra-city |
| OpenAQ [V] | Points; API v3 | Providers' terms | Purposive siting |
| Landsat C2 ST [V] | 30 m, 1982– | [M] public domain | Daytime; gaps; cloud errors |
| MODIS MOD11A1 [V] | 1 km daily, 2000– | Unrestricted | Terra ends 1 Dec 2026 |
| ESA WorldCover [V] | 10 m, 2020–21 | CC BY 4.0 | Accuracy 74–77% |
| Dynamic World [V] | 10 m, 2–5 days | CC BY 4.0 | Probabilities |
| Canopy height [V: DOI 10.1016/j.rse.2023.113888] | ~1 m | [M] | Few lidar regions |
| GHSL [V] | 100 m–1 km, 1975–2030 | Reuse with acknowledgement | Modelled |
| OSM [V] | Vector | ODbL | Uneven completeness |
| What a Waste 3.0 [V] | Country, 262 cities | Not stated | Mixed definitions |
| CDP-ICLEI Track [S] | City | [M] | Self-reported |

**5.3 Interpretation.** Open terms and readings with sources: *city* (administrative; Degree of Urbanisation [V]; functional area); *green space* (public parks per UN-Habitat [V]; ≥0.5–1 ha per WHO Europe [V]; all vegetation; canopy); *accessible* (400 m network [V]; 300 m linear [V]; 3-30-300 proposal [V]); *controlled facility* (ladder levels [V]); *MSW* ("if managed as waste" [V]); *emissions of the city* (territorial, BASIC, BASIC+ [V]; consumption-based [V]); *grid factor* (location or market; Tokyo admits menu factors under conditions [V]); *hot* (WBGT 33 [V], 35 [S], percentiles); *built-up* (GHSL, WSF, national); *savings* (EDGE reference [V]; TMG base years [V]); *main mode* (survey conventions [M]).

**5.4 Estimation.** Better estimated than observed: PM2.5 over people (fusion, then population weighting; conformal intervals and area of applicability in tyche `spatial`); emissions by sector (activity × factor or downscaling; bottom-up and downscaled can part widely [V: DOI 10.1029/2020JD032974]; uncertainty by tyche `uncertainty`); uncollected waste (survey rate × population, mass balance in industrial-ecology `flows`); air temperature and WBGT (from LST, land cover, stations); building energy (archetypes from footprints, like industrial-ecology `buildings`); travel between surveys (urban-science `luti`, `movement`); population inside a boundary (dasymetric carriage).

**5.5 Evaluation.** Operators and holders: PM2.5 against WHO's guideline and interim targets (problem, risk; WHO) [V]; MSW control (need, problem; ordinal ladder; UN-Habitat) [V]; open space (asset, need; "acceptable minimum" unspecified [V]); LCRPGR (feature; no standard) [V]; heat (risk as hazard × exposure × vulnerability [V: DOI 10.1289/ehp.0900683]; MOEJ) ; canopy (asset; a proposal); emissions (problem against the city's pledge); EDGE levels (ordinal; IFC) [V]. Ordinal judgements are never averaged; composites only within a scale type (nomos `measurement`).

**5.6 Policy making.** Options from diagnostics, catalogues (NBS [S], CURB [V]), peers (Section 4) and search (heuresis); a model proposes as grade C only. Appraisal: MACCs with the cautions of Kesicki and Ekins [V: DOI 10.1080/14693062.2011.582347], scenarios, CBA and MCA (nomos `appraisal`). Selection: adopt, reject or study first by EVSI (policy domain `adoption`). Transfer: a lens valid when support factors hold (tekmerion `warrant`; policy `transfer`).

**5.7 Actions.** The repertoire (investments, regulations, pricing, programmes, services, NbS, procurement, capacity building, inquiries) is Table 7.3. *Not yet surveyed:* volume-based waste fees, green procurement examples in client cities, utility tariff conditions.

**5.8 Results capture.** Matched DiD and synthetic control where actions reach places first (the LEZ study [V]; peira `quasi`, `interference`); interrupted series with population controls (Yokohama [V]); verified accounts against base years (Tokyo [V]); repeated WaCT with the same design [V]; LST and canopy change against controls, same season; pre-registered protocols sealed in archeion's journal (peira `protocol`).

### A.6. Placement of New Modules

The modules marked "new" in Table 7.1 (a GPC inventory, PM2.5 fusion, WaCT's schema, the 11.7.1 procedure, a heat module, land-cover readers, building-energy archetypes, mode-share accounting) are proposals; placing a module or domain is the user's decision (CLAUDE.md §6.5).
