# Chapter B. Inclusive Cities, Land Tenure and Community Engagement

## Part B. Inclusive Cities, Land Tenure and Community Engagement

*Scope.* This part surveys the pillar "inclusive" of the TDLC mission, with land tenure and community engagement, for a plug-in of the Urban Studio built on kybernesis. It does not repeat what the infrastructure's survey *Indexes of cities across scales* already covers (ISO 37120/37122/37123, UN-Habitat's Urban Monitoring Framework, SDG 11 in general, the OECD and Eurostat frameworks, small-area estimation, change of support and proxies in general); those are cited as known. It goes deeper into what inclusion asks of each block of the loop.

*Markers.* **[V]** verified this session against a primary record (the issuing body's page, a DOI resolved through Crossref, or the document itself, read); **[S]** confirmed only from secondary sources; **[M]** from memory, not verified. Where a bibliographic item is [V] but a finding attributed to it was read only in a summary, the finding is marked [S] separately.

---

### B.1. The Concept of Inclusion and Its Dimensions

#### B.1.1 Inclusion as a Process over Terms of Participation

The World Bank's flagship *Inclusion Matters* (World Bank 2013, DOI 10.1596/978-1-4648-0010-8) **[V]** defines social inclusion as the process of improving the terms for individuals and groups to take part in society (as quoted in the Inclusive Cities Approach Paper **[V]**), and, in a second formulation, as improving the ability, opportunity and dignity of people disadvantaged on the basis of their identity to take part in society **[S]**; the repository record gives its licence as CC BY 3.0 IGO **[S]**. Two features matter for a computational treatment. Inclusion is a *process* over *terms*, not a level of a single variable: it is evaluated by comparing groups' terms of access, and it needs a declared partition of the population into groups (by identity: gender, disability, ethnicity, migrant status, tenure). And its three components (ability, opportunity, dignity) have different scale types: opportunity is often measurable on a ratio scale (minutes to jobs, share served), dignity mostly on ordinal or nominal scales (perceived respect, voice).

#### B.1.2 The Inclusive Cities Approach: Spatial, Social and Economic

The *Inclusive Cities Approach Paper* (World Bank, Report No. AUS8539, May 2015, GSURR) **[V]** organises urban inclusion in three interlinked dimensions:

- **spatial**: access to affordable land, housing and infrastructure (tools named: land tenure, land-use planning, land-based financing, slum upgrading and prevention, land regularisation);
- **social**: rights and participation (participatory planning and governance, community-driven development, crime and violence prevention, support to organisations of the urban poor);
- **economic**: opportunities for all (skills, access to finance, spatial access to jobs).

It states that it offers no new definition but a frame for operations, and its Annex 6 ("Measuring Inclusion") is the closest thing the Bank has to an inclusive-city indicator list. Its principles are directly usable as requirements on the evidence block: capture both process and result; measure quality as well as quantity of services; treat affordability as a share of income; keep indicators simple and cheap; and explore "self-reporting by informal dwellers or partnering with urban poor organizations". It asks that data say *who* is excluded, *what* is measured, *where* (the administrative unit), *how* (collected with informal dwellers or their organisations) and *when* (regular updates) **[V]**. The annex also reports São Paulo's index of exclusion, in which each indicator was scored −1/0/+1 against the city mean or a reference standard and summed, and notes economists' reservations about summing indexed values **[V]**: an early instance of the meaningfulness problem that kybernesis's kernel (scales and meaningfulness) is built to catch.

#### B.1.3 GPSC's Fourth Outcome Dimension

The Global Platform for Sustainable Cities' *Urban Sustainability Framework* (1st ed., World Bank 2018; reproduction for non-commercial purposes with attribution) **[V]** has four outcome dimensions, the fourth "Inclusivity and Quality of Life", goal "to reduce inequalities and provide a decent quality of life for all", in eight focus areas: housing; education; poverty, hunger and food security; drinking water and sanitation; basic physical infrastructure; health and well-being; safety; social cohesion. Its indicators are borrowed from the SDGs, ISO 37120, the IDB's ESCI, the EBRD and the City Resilience Index. Three are worth noting for their open terms:

- *Housing affordability index*: share of household income spent on housing (mortgage or rent) by the poorest 20 per cent (from the City Resilience Index); the quintile is a declared reading of "the poor".
- *Housing deprivation* (CRI): share of people in an overcrowded dwelling with at least one of a leaking roof or damp, no bath, shower or indoor flushing toilet, or "too dark".
- *Feeling safe walking alone*, with the guidance that a sample survey include at least 100 households in each district: a design requirement placed on the collection block.

Its social-cohesion indicators (women and ethnic minorities as shares of those elected to city office; turnout at municipal elections) are the only participation measures in the framework, and all are ratios of counts **[V]**.

#### B.1.4 Gender and Disability as Cross-Cutting Partitions

The *Handbook for Gender-Inclusive Urban Planning and Design* (Terraza, Orlando, Lakovits, Lopes Janik and Kalashyan; World Bank, February 2020) **[V]**, developed with Kounkuey Design Initiative and licensed CC BY 3.0 IGO according to the repository record **[S]**, organises its guidance around six issues: access, mobility, safety and freedom from violence, health and hygiene, climate resilience, and security of tenure. It is a process handbook more than an indicator set; its contribution to the loop is to require that every evaluation be disaggregated by gender (and sexual and gender minorities), and that the collection block include participatory activities with women and girls. UN-Habitat's *Her City* toolbox (with the Shared City Foundation and Block by Block, among others; its guide now in a fourth edition, 2025) **[V]** supplies participatory methods of the same kind.

For disability, the Washington Group Short Set on Functioning asks about difficulty in six domains (seeing, hearing, walking or climbing steps, remembering or concentrating, self-care, communication), each answered on a four-point ordinal scale; the recommended identifier "includes everyone with at least one domain that is coded as a lot of difficulty or cannot do it at all" **[V]**. The cut-off is a declared reading: the Group itself says countries need not be restricted to it.

The World Bank's Environmental and Social Framework makes "disadvantaged or vulnerable" individuals and groups a required category of every project's stakeholder identification (ESS10 Guidance Note for Borrowers) **[V]**.

---

### B.2. Frameworks for Land Tenure and Land Governance

#### B.2.1 Tenure as Documented Rights and as Perceived Security

SDG indicator 1.4.2 (custodians UN-Habitat and the World Bank; metadata updated 2024-09-27) **[V]** is the proportion of the adult population with secure tenure rights to land, (a) with legally recognised documentation and (b) who perceive their rights as secure, by sex and type of tenure. Both parts are needed: documentation "refers to the recording and publication of information on the nature and location of land, rights and right holders in a form that is recognized by government", and *which* documents count is defined by country-specific metadata; perception is "an individual's perception of the likelihood of involuntary loss of land … regardless of the formal status". Part A may come from censuses, surveys or a land agency's administrative records; Part B only from surveys with the agreed perception questions **[V]**. The metadata say plainly that the two can diverge: documented rights perceived as insecure, undocumented rights perceived as safe. In kybernesis terms the indicator is two evaluations over one state, and the admissible readings of "legally recognised" are country-indexed, each with an *authority* as source.

#### B.2.2 Prindex: Perceived Insecurity Measured Globally

Prindex measures perceived tenure insecurity by the question "In the next five years, how likely or unlikely is it that you could lose the right to use this property, or part of this property, against your will?"; respondents answering likely or very likely are classed insecure **[V]**. Most data were collected through the Gallup World Poll (adults 18+, face-to-face, telephone or web), about 1,000 interviews per country in 2020 and fewer in 2024; "all data are available for non-commercial use" **[V]**. Its 2024 round reports 1.1 billion adults in 108 countries (23 per cent) feeling insecure, up from 19 per cent in 2020 **[V]**. National samples of that size support national and broad subnational estimates, not city or neighbourhood ones: a city needs its own survey with the Prindex module, or small-area estimation.

#### B.2.3 The Land Governance Assessment Framework

LGAF (Deininger, Selod and Burns 2012, DOI 10.1596/978-0-8213-8758-0) **[V]** is a diagnostic of national or subnational land governance. Since its 2013 revision it has 116 dimensions in nine thematic areas (land tenure recognition; rights to forest and common lands and rural land-use rules; urban land use, planning and development; public land management; transparent process and economic benefit; land registry and cadastre; land valuation and taxation; dispute resolution; review of institutional arrangements and policies), each rated A–D (A good practice, D weak) by nine technical panels of local experts, summarised in a country scorecard; the Bank reports partnering with over 40 countries **[V]**. Its ratings are ordinal, assigned by panels: a scorecard is a *reading by convention and analysts*, whose standing a kybernesis evaluation should keep apart from any measure.

#### B.2.4 The Continuum of Land Rights, STDM and Fit-for-Purpose Administration

GLTN's continuum of land rights treats documented and undocumented, individual and group, formal, customary and informal rights as points on one range, not a binary of titled and untitled **[S]**. Its Social Tenure Domain Model records people-to-land relationships at any level of formality on the structure Party – Social Tenure Relationship – Spatial Unit; it is a specialisation of ISO 19152, the Land Administration Domain Model, which FIG Publication 86 (Morscher-Unger, Zevenbergen and Gitau, 2026) reports as now a normative annex of the revised standard **[V]**. The software is a QGIS plug-in on PostgreSQL/PostGIS, GPL-2.0 (github.com/gltn/stdm) **[V]**.

*Fit-for-Purpose Land Administration* (Enemark, Bell, Lemmen and McLaren; FIG Publication 60, FIG and World Bank, 2014) **[V]** argues for general rather than fixed boundaries, aerial imagery rather than field survey ("three to five times cheaper"), accuracy relative to use, and incremental upgrading, enshrined in law. For the loop, this is a stance on the *collection* block: the resolution of evidence should be chosen for the decision it serves.

---

### B.3. The Indexes and Their Open Terms

Each index below is given with its definition, unit, scale type and direction, the open terms that need a declared reading, and common standards. Scale types follow Stevens: nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio, absolute (counts). A proportion of people is a ratio-scale quantity on [0, 1] computed from absolute counts.

#### B.3.1 Population in Slums, Informal Settlements and Inadequate Housing (SDG 11.1.1)

UN-Habitat's metadata (2021-12-20) **[V]** compute two components: the percentage of the urban or city population living in slum or informal-settlement households (SISH), and the percentage in inadequate-housing households (IHH). A slum household lacks one or more of: an improved water source; improved sanitation; sufficient living area (not more than three people per habitable room); durable housing (permanent structure, not on a hazardous site: flood plain, steep slope, toxic waste, dangerous rights of way); security of tenure (documentation usable as proof, and de facto or perceived protection from eviction). Inadequate housing is measured by affordability: net monthly housing expenditure above 30 per cent of household income. The metadata's footnote adds the alternatives: house price-to-annual-income ratio of 3.0 or less and rent-to-monthly-income of 25 per cent or less count as affordable **[V]**. The metadata also state the indicator does not capture homelessness **[V]**.

*Open terms:* "improved" source and facility (JMP ladders); "habitable room"; "durable" and "hazardous location" (whose hazard map, which return period); "secure tenure" (which documents, whose perception); "household income" (gross, net, imputed rent); the unit (urban per Degree of Urbanisation, or the administrative city). Each is a point of interpretation with sources: UN-Habitat as *authority*, the national statistical office as *convention*, hazard maps as *data*.

*Scale type:* each deprivation is nominal (yes/no) per household; the index is a ratio over persons; direction: lower is better. The union rule ("one or more") makes the index non-decreasing in every deprivation, so the reading of each term can only move the index one way; this monotonicity is what lets a construal report its range under the admissible readings.

#### B.3.2 Housing Affordability

Three common forms: the share of households whose housing cost exceeds 30 per cent of income (11.1.1's IHH) **[V]**; the price-to-income ratio (median price over median annual household income; affordable at 3.0 or less in UN-Habitat's footnote) **[V]**; and the share of income spent on housing by the poorest quintile (GPSC/CRI) **[V]**. All are ratio scales; the thresholds are *normative standards* in Bradshaw's sense, held by UN-Habitat or a city. Open terms: the income concept, what counts as housing cost (utilities, service charges, informal rents), and the quintile or decile taken as "the poor". In informal markets neither prices nor rents are observed; they are estimated (§4.4).

#### B.3.3 Access to Basic Services (SDG 1.4.1 and Components)

SDG 1.4.1 (metadata 2023-07-18) **[V]** is the proportion of population in households with access to basic services, built from nine components (drinking water, sanitation, hygiene, energy, mobility, waste collection, health care, education, information technologies), each defined by its own SDG indicator. GPSC adds authorised electricity connections, sewer connections, and the share of income the poorest 20 per cent spend on fuel and electricity **[V]**. Open terms: the service ladder rung that counts ("basic" versus "safely managed"), legality of the connection, reliability (hours a day). Ratio scale; higher is better.

#### B.3.4 Convenient Access to Public Transport (SDG 11.2.1)

Metadata 2025-04-23 **[V]**: the share of population within walking distance along the street network of 500 m of a stop of a low-capacity system (bus, BRT) and/or 1 km of a high-capacity system (rail, metro, ferry), by sex, age and disability. The analysis area is to be delineated by the Degree of Urbanisation. Further criteria of convenience (access for people with disabilities; frequent service at peak; safe stops) are named but not part of the core computation; a secondary indicator recommends an average peak wait of 20 minutes, and affordability is suggested as the share of income spent on transport by the poorest quintile **[V]**. Stops come from operators, GTFS feeds, OpenStreetMap or field survey **[V]**. The metadata recognise that informal transport without fixed schedules escapes the frequency criterion **[V]**.

*Open terms:* "officially recognised stop" (informal minibus stops?), the capacity class of each mode, the reference point (home, school, workplace), "frequent", the network (pedestrian network from OSM, with or without informal paths). The thresholds of 500 m and 1 km are *normative*, held by UN-Habitat. Ratio scale; higher is better.

#### B.3.5 Accessibility to Jobs and Services

Cumulative opportunities, gravity and two-step floating catchment measures (Hansen 1959; Luo and Wang 2003; Geurs and van Wee 2004) **[V]** are already in the infrastructure (nomos's `measurement` accessibility; urban science's `access`). For inclusion, three refinements matter: accessibility *by group* (women's trip chains, people with reduced mobility at slower speeds), *with cost* (a fare can make a reachable job unreachable for the poorest), and the *distribution* of accessibility (its Gini, or the share below a threshold), which the infrastructure's unmet-need computation already supports. Ratio scale; direction higher is better; the threshold of "reachable" is a declared reading.

#### B.3.6 Intra-Urban Poverty and Inequality

Poverty headcount, gap and severity (FGT), Gini and Theil are standard and present in nomos. What is specific to the inclusive pillar is resolution: national surveys do not resolve neighbourhoods, so intra-urban poverty is estimated (§4.4). Open terms: the poverty line (national, international, relative), the welfare aggregate (consumption, income, assets), the equivalence scale, and the spatial unit.

#### B.3.7 Residential Segregation

Massey and Denton (1988, DOI 10.2307/2579183) **[V]** grouped twenty indices into five dimensions: evenness, exposure, concentration, centralisation and clustering. The index of dissimilarity (evenness) is already in nomos; isolation and interaction (exposure) are a few lines more. Reardon and O'Sullivan (2004, DOI 10.1111/j.0081-1750.2004.00150.x) **[V]** give spatial versions for several groups, where proximity is defined by a declared kernel, which removes the dependence on arbitrary tract boundaries (the modifiable areal unit problem). Mobility data allow *experienced* segregation, exposure in the places people visit rather than where they live: from 4.5 million phone users and 1.1 million places in 11 US cities, Moro, Calacci, Dong and Pentland (2021, DOI 10.1038/s41467-021-24899-8) **[V]** show that experienced income segregation can differ greatly within close spatial proximity and is associated with how people explore places. Scale: dissimilarity is ratio on [0, 1]; lower is less segregated. Open terms: the groups (income classes, ethnicity, tenure), the unit or kernel bandwidth, and whether segregation by choice is distinguished from segregation by constraint (an interpretation the index cannot make by itself).

#### B.3.8 Tenure Security

Documented (1.4.2 A), perceived (1.4.2 B; Prindex) and *de facto* (years of uncontested occupation, eviction history) tenure are three different states, the last rarely measured. Per-person indicators are nominal (secure/insecure) after a cut on an ordinal scale (Prindex's likelihood answers); the index is a ratio. The cut-off ("likely or very likely") is a reading **[V]**.

#### B.3.9 Participation in Planning (SDG 11.3.2)

Metadata 2025-04-23 **[V]**: the proportion of cities with a direct participation structure of civil society in urban planning and management that operates regularly and democratically. Option 1 is a scorecard: five local experts (government, academia, civil society, international organisations) score four questions on a four-point Likert scale (structures for participation in planning; in budget decisions; in evaluation and feedback on urban management; whether they promote the participation of women, youth and marginalised groups); each evaluator's scores are averaged, the evaluators' averages averaged, and a city counts if its mean exceeds 2.5 **[V]**.

*Remark.* The construction averages ordinal scores, which is not meaningful under monotone recodings of the Likert scale: two recodings that preserve the order of answers can put a city on either side of 2.5. A kybernesis evaluation should either treat the scorecard as ordinal (a median, or the share of "agree" and "strongly agree") or declare the interval reading explicitly as an *analyst* reading, so that the standing reports when the verdict turns on it. Arnstein's ladder (1969, DOI 10.1080/01944366908977225) **[V]** gives an ordinal scale of the depth of participation (from manipulation to citizen control) that complements 11.3.2's existence test.

#### B.3.10 Safety

SDG 11.7.2 (metadata 2024-03-28) **[V]**: the proportion of persons victim of non-sexual or sexual harassment in the previous 12 months, by sex, age, disability status and place of occurrence, with definitions from the International Classification of Crime for Statistical Purposes. SDG 16.1.4 (metadata 2024-07-29) **[V]**: the proportion of the adult population who feel safe walking alone in their neighbourhood after dark. Both are survey-based, ratio scales after an ordinal cut; "neighbourhood" and "after dark" are left to the respondent, which is a reading by the respondent and makes intra-urban comparison fragile.

#### B.3.11 Gender Gaps in Access and Mobility

A gap is a contrast between groups on any index above: a difference (interval) or a ratio (ratio scale) of women's to men's access, trips, travel time or harassment. Its open terms are the contrast form and the reference group. Women's trip chains (care trips) are poorly captured by commute-based accessibility; time-use data (already in the infrastructure for Europe) are the evidence that shows them.

#### B.3.12 Disability Access

Two families: the prevalence of disability by the Washington Group identifier **[V]**, and the accessibility of the built environment and transport (step-free stations, accessible stops, compliant buildings), which 11.2.1 names but does not compute **[V]**. The second is an audit of facilities against a code (in Japan, the Barrier-Free Act's standards, §5.2); the standard is held by a legislature or an operator.

---

### B.4. The Loop Block by Block

This section organises what the pillar needs by kybernesis's blocks. Each subsection states what the block receives, what methods and evidence serve it, and what is better estimated than observed.

#### B.4.1 Collection: Designs and Mechanisms of Observation

Every source is a design (who could be observed) and a mechanism (who was, and how). The inclusive pillar is dominated by mechanisms that *exclude the excluded*:

- **Censuses** enumerate dwellings and households; informal settlements are under-enumerated where enumeration areas follow formal addresses, and homeless people fall outside household frames (11.1.1 says it does not capture homelessness) **[V]**. Censuses rarely ask about tenure documents or perception.
- **Household surveys.** DHS, MICS and LSMS give asset, service and (LSMS) consumption data with known designs; DHS releases cluster coordinates displaced by up to 2 km (urban) and 5 km (rural, 1 per cent up to 10 km) to protect respondents **[S]**, which blurs neighbourhood-level analysis. The UN-Habitat Urban Inequity Surveys, cited in the 1.4.2 metadata, were designed to be intra-urban **[V]**. GPSC's guidance of at least 100 households per district for perception indicators is a minimum design **[V]**.
- **Perception surveys** (Prindex, 16.1.4, satisfaction with services) record felt need; national samples need a city boost.
- **Community enumeration and profiling.** Slum Dwellers International's *Know Your City* holds community-collected profiles, enumerations and maps; its portal lists 7,712 slums profiled in 224 cities **[V]**. The mechanism differs: the observers are residents, coverage follows federations' presence, and the data's use is negotiated with the community. The Inclusive Cities paper explicitly asks for collection "in partnership with informal dwellers and/or their organization" **[V]**.
- **Participatory appraisal and mapping** (Chambers 1994, DOI 10.1016/0305-750X(94)90141-4) **[V]**, gender-specific walks and safety audits (Her City **[V]**), and STDM for recording tenure claims in the field **[V]**.
- **Expert panels**: LGAF's nine panels **[V]**, 11.3.2's five evaluators **[V]**. Here the mechanism is selection of experts; the panel's composition is part of the evidence.
- **Administrative streams**: land registries (1.4.2 Part A), utility connection records, grievance logs (ESS10 requires a log "registered in writing and maintained as a database") **[V]**, participatory budgeting submissions.
- **Remote sensing and digital traces**: building footprints (Google Open Buildings v3: 1.8 billion detections across Africa, South and Southeast Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean, with confidence scores, licensed CC BY 4.0 or ODbL at the user's choice) **[V]**; settlement morphology classifiers (Kuffer, Pfeffer and Sliuzas 2016, DOI 10.3390/rs8060455) **[V]**; mobile phone metadata (Blumenstock, Cadamuro and On 2015, DOI 10.1126/science.aac4420) **[V]**.

*Design principle for the plug-in.* The collection block should carry, for each source, its frame and its known exclusions as data, so that the estimation block can weight or bound for them (peira's coarsening framework), and so that an *inquiry* (an enumeration, a boost sample) can be proposed where the exclusion is largest.

#### B.4.2 Evidence: Traces, Provenance, Licence, Resolution and Bias

| Source | Supports | Licence or terms | Resolution | Principal biases |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Census microdata or tables | counts, dwelling attributes, crowding, services | national; often tables open, microdata licensed **[M]** | enumeration area to block | under-enumeration of informal and homeless people; no tenure perception |
| DHS / MICS / LSMS | assets, services, consumption (LSMS) | registration required (DHS, MICS) **[S]**; varied (LSMS) **[M]** | cluster, displaced 2/5 km (DHS) **[S]** | urban clusters few; displacement; years apart |
| Prindex | perceived insecurity | non-commercial use **[V]** | national (≈1,000 interviews, 2020) **[V]** | not city-level; Gallup frame |
| SDI Know Your City | settlement profiles, enumerations, maps | not stated on portal **[V]** | settlement, household | coverage follows federations; consent and ownership terms |
| OSM | streets, services, stops | ODbL **[M]** | feature | under-mapping of informal areas, uneven by mappers |
| GTFS | scheduled stops and frequency | per feed **[M]** | stop, trip | informal transit absent (11.2.1 says so) **[V]** |
| Open Buildings | footprints, density, morphology | CC BY 4.0 or ODbL **[V]** | building | detection errors in dense informal fabric; a confidence threshold to declare **[V]** |
| Relative Wealth Index (Chi et al. 2022) | relative asset wealth | article CC BY-NC-ND; data on HDX **[V]** | 2.4 km grid **[V]** | relative within country; trained on DHS assets; urban/rural carries much of the signal **[V]** |
| Mobile phone data | mobility, experienced segregation, wealth proxies | proprietary, by agreement **[M]** | tower or device | ownership gaps by gender and income; privacy |
| Grievance logs | expressed need, process quality | project-internal | case | who complains is selected; fear of reprisal |
| Expert scorecards (LGAF, 11.3.2) | governance, participation structures | public reports **[M]** | country or city | panel composition; ordinal |

Provenance should be kept as the infrastructure keeps it elsewhere: each trace with its digest and custody (archeion), its licence, and its codebook (archeion's `datasets`, after DDI). Community data need one more field: the *terms agreed with the community* (who may see it, for what), which access rules in archeion can enforce.

#### B.4.3 Interpretation: Open Terms and Their Admissible Readings

Every index above has open terms. A reading carries its source (authority, convention, analyst, data, proposal). A first catalogue for the pillar:

| Open term | Admissible readings | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Slum household | UN-Habitat's five deprivations, any one **[V]**; national definitions (e.g. notified slums) **[M]**; settlement-level morphology (remote sensing) | authority; convention; data |
| Sufficient living area | ≤ 3 persons per habitable room **[V]**; floor area per person thresholds **[V]** (named as alternatives) | authority |
| Secure tenure | documented (country list of documents) **[V]**; perceived (likely/very likely cut) **[V]**; de facto (years without challenge) | authority; convention; analyst |
| Affordable | cost ≤ 30% of income; price/income ≤ 3.0; rent/income ≤ 25% **[V]**; residual income after housing | authority; analyst |
| Convenient access | 500 m / 1 km on the network **[V]**; plus 20-minute peak wait **[V]**; plus fare ≤ x% of income | authority; analyst |
| The poor | national poverty line; poorest 20% **[V]**; relative line | authority; convention |
| Disabled | WG "a lot of difficulty" or worse on one domain **[V]**; "some difficulty" | authority; convention |
| Participation structure | 11.3.2 scorecard above 2.5 **[V]**; Arnstein rung ≥ "partnership" **[V]** | authority; analyst |
| Vulnerable group | ESS10's project-specific identification **[V]** | convention (project) |
| City | Degree of Urbanisation **[V]**; administrative boundary | authority; convention |

tekmerion's `construal` already reads a finding under every combination of readings and reports its standing (clear, arguable, untenable) with its pivots. The needs domain already declares such points of interpretation (income lines, reference groups) for its census tables, so the slum and affordability terms fit the same form.

#### B.4.4 Estimation: The State of Inclusion and What Is Better Estimated than Observed

The state variables of the pillar are, per small area *a* and group *g*: the population and its composition; the joint distribution of the five housing deprivations; welfare (consumption or assets) and poverty; housing costs and incomes; service access by component; accessibility to jobs and services; tenure (documented, perceived, de facto); disability prevalence; safety experienced and felt; and the existence and depth of participation structures.

Some are **better computed than surveyed**: network distance to stops and services (11.2.1, accessibility) from OSM and GTFS, given a population surface; the computation needs only the declared readings of §4.3.

Some are **better estimated by models** because direct survey estimates are too imprecise at the scale of decision:

- *Poverty and welfare by small area.* The World Bank's *Guidelines to Small Area Estimation for Poverty Mapping* (Corral, Molina, Cojocaru and Segovia 2022; CC BY 3.0 IGO) **[V]** review direct estimates, area-level Fay–Herriot models (Fay and Herriot 1979, DOI 10.1080/01621459.1979.10482505) **[V]**, unit-level models following Elbers, Lanjouw and Lanjouw (2003, DOI 10.1111/1468-0262.00399) **[V]** and the empirical best predictor of Molina and Rao (2010, DOI 10.1002/cjs.10051) **[V]**, and alternatives for years between censuses, including gradient boosting; they give a decision tree for choosing among them by data constraints **[V]**. peira's `small_areas` holds Fay–Herriot's EBLUP; its README lists the unit-level nested-error model and ELL as planned (checked in the repository this session).
- *Wealth from imagery and traces.* Jean et al. (2016, DOI 10.1126/science.aaf7894) **[V]** and Chi, Fang, Chatterjee and Blumenstock (2022, DOI 10.1073/pnas.2113658119) **[V]** estimate asset wealth from satellite imagery and connectivity data; the latter report R² ≈ 0.56 against DHS wealth under spatially stratified cross-validation, larger prediction error far from training data, and an index relative within each country **[V]**. These are proxies whose area of applicability must be declared; tyche's prediction with an area of applicability (its decision 0028) already withholds estimates outside it.
- *Slum and deprived-area extent.* Morphology seen from space is not deprivation. A review of slum mapping by remote sensing over 2000–2015 (Kuffer et al. 2016) **[V]** finds contextual knowledge of slums' diversity limited, slum dynamics poorly captured, robust image-based proxies still to be developed, and slums' complex morphology hard to extract by standard methods even at sub-metre resolution. The "domains of deprivation" framework (Abascal et al. 2022, DOI 10.1016/j.compenvurbsys.2022.101770) **[V]** organises deprived-area mapping by domains of deprivation rather than by appearance (its content beyond the title read only in summary **[S]**). The estimand is the *household* count of 11.1.1; an image classifier gives settlement extent, which a model must carry to households, with the five deprivations not all visible (tenure never is).
- *Rents and prices in informal markets.* Unobserved in registries; estimated from surveys and listings by hedonic models (the infrastructure has hedonic prices with spatial dependence), with the income denominator also estimated, so the affordability ratio carries the uncertainty of both.
- *Tenure perception by area.* Prindex is national; a city's distribution needs its own survey or small-area estimation with covariates (documentation from the registry, settlement type, eviction history).
- *Participation structures.* Not estimated but assessed; the uncertainty is inter-rater (five evaluators), measurable by agreement statistics (nomos holds Krippendorff's α and κ).

Uncertainty should be carried in kybernesis's *measure* (intervals, posterior draws, MSE of the EBLUP), separately from the *standing* over readings.

#### B.4.5 Evaluation: Operators, Scale Types, Standards and Who Holds Them

Evaluation applies operators to the estimated state in parallel. For this pillar:

| Operator | Output space and scale | Standard and holder (Bradshaw's kind) |
|---|---|---|
| Deprivation union (11.1.1) | nominal per household → ratio over persons | UN-Habitat (normative) |
| Affordability exceedance | nominal per household → ratio | UN-Habitat 30%, 3.0, 25% (normative) |
| Access within threshold (11.2.1, 1.4.1) | nominal per person → ratio | UN-Habitat 500 m/1 km (normative) |
| Accessibility deficit | ratio (minutes, jobs reachable) | city plan or analyst (normative) |
| Group gap | interval (difference) or ratio | equality as norm (comparative) |
| Segregation index | ratio on [0, 1] | none universal; comparative across cities or years |
| Perceived insecurity | ordinal → nominal by cut → ratio | respondents (felt) |
| Grievances by type and area | absolute counts | complainants (expressed) |
| Participation scorecard | ordinal (treat as such) | UN-Habitat 11.3.2 (normative); communities (felt) |
| LGAF rating | ordinal A–D | World Bank good practice (normative) |
| Disability prevalence and facility compliance | ratio; nominal per facility | WG (convention); building code (normative) |

Two cautions. Composite inclusion indices (São Paulo's summed −1/0/+1 scores **[V]**) combine ordinal judgements additively; kybernesis's kernel should refuse such a composite unless an interval reading is declared, or compute it as a non-compensatory rule (nomos's appraisal holds non-compensatory screening). And the *comparative* standard needs the reference group declared: a neighbourhood's deficit relative to the city mean is not the same finding as one relative to the best-served quintile.

The products of evaluation are needs (gaps judged by a standard, as in the needs domain), problems (needs with a cause identified), risks (for inclusion: eviction risk, displacement risk after upgrading, exclusion from a programme's register), and assets (community organisations, savings groups, existing participation structures, which the Inclusive Cities paper lists among its measures **[V]**).

#### B.4.6 Policy Making: Options, Appraisal, Selection and Transfer

Options for this pillar come from three sources: the repertoire of §4.7 (each action a template with its mechanism); communities' own proposals (machizukuri proposals in Kobe, §5.1; participatory budgeting submissions); and transfer of a practice from another city. Appraisal can use the infrastructure's policy domain (a theory of change with its weakest link, evidence pooled and graded, transfer with support factors, net present value with the value of information) and nomos's appraisal (multi-criteria analysis with non-compensatory screening and weight stability, cost–benefit analysis).

*Transfer.* Cartwright and Hardie's argument, already in tekmerion and the policy domain, asks that a practice's *support factors* be shown present in the target city. For each action below, §4.7 names its support factors; §5 names those of Japanese practices. The question "will it work here" is then a construal over the support factors' readings, with standing.

*Who participates in selection.* Mansuri and Rao's Policy Research Report on participatory development (World Bank, DOI 10.1596/978-0-8213-8256-1) **[V]** reviews almost 500 studies, distinguishes *organic* from *induced* participation, and finds induced participation works best with a responsive state, while most Bank-funded participatory projects paid too little attention to context and to monitoring that allows learning **[S]**. Representative deliberation (citizens' panels chosen by lot) is documented by the OECD (2020, DOI 10.1787/339306da-en) **[V]** in 289 cases, about half of them at local level **[S]**. These are methods for the selection step, and their outputs (a panel's recommendations) enter the record as proposals with a source, not as verdicts.

#### B.4.7 Actions: The Repertoire of Interventions and Inquiries

Each action is given with its mechanism, the state variables it acts on, the evidence that would show it worked, typical risks, an example, and the support factors for transfer.

**(a) In-situ slum upgrading.** *Mechanism:* infrastructure (water, sanitation, drainage, paving, lighting) and sometimes housing improvement brought to an existing settlement, keeping residents in place. *Acts on:* service access, durability, health, safety, land values. *Evidence:* deprivations before and after against comparison settlements (difference in differences, peira's `quasi`), health outcomes, rents. Randomised or quasi-randomised evidence on housing components exists: replacing dirt floors with cement in Mexico improved child health and adult satisfaction and mental health (Cattaneo et al. 2009, DOI 10.1257/pol.1.1.75) **[V]**; prefabricated houses in Latin American slums (Galiani et al. 2017, DOI 10.1016/j.jue.2016.11.001) **[V]** (findings: improved well-being and perceived security, little effect on labour outcomes **[M]**); piped-water connections in urban Morocco (Devoto et al. 2012, DOI 10.1257/pol.4.4.68) **[V]**. *Risks:* rising rents displace tenants; elite capture of contracts; upgrades without tenure security. *Example:* Thailand's Baan Mankong, from 2003, giving infrastructure subsidies and housing loans to community organisations and their city-wide networks, upgrading in situ where possible (Boonyabancha 2005, DOI 10.1177/095624780501700104) **[V]**. *Support factors:* organised communities with savings groups; a national institution lending to communities (CODI in Thailand); land that can be leased or bought collectively; municipal willingness to recognise settlements.

**(b) Sites and services.** *Mechanism:* serviced plots on greenfield land on which households build incrementally. *Acts on:* future slum formation, housing quality, layout and access. *Evidence:* long-run comparison of serviced and unserviced areas; Michaels et al. (2021, DOI 10.1086/714119) **[V]** use a spatial regression discontinuity on Tanzanian projects of the 1970s and 1980s and find that by the 2010s the serviced greenfield areas had larger, more regular and better-quality buildings **[S]**; Owens, Gulyani and Rizvi (2018, DOI 10.1016/j.worlddev.2018.01.021) **[V]** revisit Indian projects once judged failures. *Risks:* peripheral location far from jobs; plots captured by the non-poor; slow occupation. *Support factors:* land on the urban fringe within commuting reach; trunk infrastructure finance; plot allocation rules that hold.

**(c) Tenure regularisation and titling.** *Mechanism:* recording rights (titles, certificates, occupancy rights, intermediate tenure) to reduce eviction risk and enable investment. *Acts on:* documented and perceived tenure; investment; labour allocation. *Evidence:* Field (2007, DOI 10.1162/qjec.2007.122.4.1561) **[V]** finds Peru's urban titling raised household labour supply outside the home and reduced child labour **[S]**; Galiani and Schargrodsky (2010, DOI 10.1016/j.jpubeco.2010.06.002) **[V]**, in a natural experiment in Buenos Aires, find more housing investment, smaller households and more schooling, not through credit **[S]**; Payne, Durand-Lasserve and Rakodi (2009, DOI 10.1177/0956247809344364) **[V]** review titling programmes and find the claimed gains in credit, investment and poverty generally absent, and that many non-title tenure forms already give high security. *Risks:* titles that trigger sales to wealthier buyers; disputes surfaced and decided against women or tenants; costly cadastral accuracy (the fit-for-purpose critique **[V]**). *Example:* fit-for-purpose systematic registration (Rwanda among FIG 60's case studies **[S]**); STDM in informal settlements **[V]**. *Support factors:* a recognised legal form for the rights recorded; affordable registration; a continuum-of-rights stance; joint titling for spouses.

**(d) Land readjustment.** *Mechanism:* landholders pool plots; the project re-plots them, contributing land for roads and parks and for reserve land sold to finance works; each receives a smaller but serviced and more valuable plot near the original. *Acts on:* layout, infrastructure, land values, tenure continuity, community cohesion. *Evidence and example:* §5.3. *Risks:* tenants and informal occupiers without registered rights outside the consent and compensation; dependence on rising land prices; long durations. *Support factors:* §5.3.

**(e) Housing finance and subsidies.** *Mechanism:* demand-side subsidies, mortgage guarantees, or state-built units allocated by lottery or register. *Acts on:* housing quality, affordability, location. *Evidence:* a housing lottery in Ahmedabad gave winners improved peripheral housing; fourteen years later winners reported no change in tenure security, income or human capital, more isolation from family and caste networks, and 34 per cent never took up the housing while 32 per cent eventually left (Barnhardt, Field and Pande 2017, DOI 10.1257/app.20150397) **[V]**; South African state housing has been studied for its employment effects (Franklin 2020, DOI 10.1016/j.jue.2020.103265) **[V]** (finding: housing enabled work, per its title; details **[M]**). *Risks:* relocation to the periphery that severs networks and jobs; subsidies captured by the non-poor. *Support factors:* well-located land; targeting registers; mortgage markets reaching informal incomes.

**(f) Rental policy.** *Mechanism:* recognising and regulating rental housing (security of occupancy, small-landlord finance, rental subsidies). *Acts on:* affordability and tenure security of tenants, who are a large share of the urban poor and invisible to ownership-centred programmes. *Evidence:* Gilbert (2016, DOI 10.1016/j.habitatint.2015.11.025) **[V]** reviews international experience. *Risks:* controls that shrink supply; informality of landlords. *Support factors:* rental contracts enforceable at low cost; a census or survey that records tenants.

**(g) Transport fares and accessible transit.** *Mechanism:* targeted fare discounts or integrated fares; stops and vehicles made accessible. *Acts on:* accessibility with cost; gender and disability gaps in mobility. *Evidence:* Bogotá's targeted ("pro-poor") fare subsidy assessed for accessibility, affordability and equity (Guzman and Oviedo 2018, DOI 10.1016/j.tranpol.2018.04.012) **[V]**; accessibility recomputed with the fare in the cost. *Risks:* targeting errors; subsidies flowing to those already served. *Support factors:* a targeting register; smart-card fare systems; operators compensated for discounts.

**(h) Universal design and barrier-free environments.** *Mechanism:* standards for new buildings, stations, vehicles and streets, plus area plans for existing ones. *Acts on:* disability and age access; 11.2.1's convenience criteria. *Evidence:* audits of facilities against the standard; travel of people with disabilities. *Example:* Japan's Barrier-Free Act, §5.2. *Risks:* standards applied only to new construction; compliance unchecked. *Support factors:* an enforcing building authority; operators able to finance retrofits; organisations of persons with disabilities to sit on councils.

**(i) Participatory budgeting.** *Mechanism:* residents decide part of the capital budget in assemblies. *Acts on:* allocation toward local priorities; participation. *Evidence:* Gonçalves (2014, DOI 10.1016/j.worlddev.2013.01.009) **[V]**, on Brazilian municipalities, finds those with participatory budgeting spent a larger share on sanitation and health, associated with lower infant mortality **[S]**. *Risks:* capture by organised groups; small budget shares; participation fatigue. *Support factors:* discretionary municipal capital budgets; a city government that implements the choices (Mansuri and Rao's responsive state **[V]**).

**(j) Grievance and feedback mechanisms.** *Mechanism:* a channel for project-affected people to raise concerns, with logged cases, published timelines, transparency, an appeal (including the courts) and optional mediation (ESS10 Guidance Note, Annex A) **[V]**. *Acts on:* the state's knowledge of harms; accountability; legitimacy. *Evidence:* resolution rates and times, repeat grievances, comparison of grievances with independent surveys. *Risks:* fear of reprisal; logs used as a performance metric and so suppressed. *Support factors:* a party that answers grievances with authority to act; anonymity.

**(k) Links to social protection.** *Mechanism:* using a social registry to target housing, fare or service subsidies and to enrol excluded households. *Acts on:* targeting accuracy and coverage. *Evidence:* inclusion and exclusion errors; take-up. The World Bank's *Sourcebook on the Foundations of Social Protection Delivery Systems* (Lindert et al. 2020, DOI 10.1596/978-1-4648-1577-5) **[V]** describes registries and the delivery chain. *Risks:* registries built on formal addresses miss informal residents. *Support factors:* an ID system reaching informal residents; data sharing between agencies.

**(l) Community-driven development and machizukuri councils.** *Mechanism:* recognised neighbourhood bodies that plan, propose and sometimes implement. *Evidence and example:* §5.1. *Risks:* elite capture; councils that represent owners but not tenants. *Support factors:* §5.1.

**Inquiries.** An *enumeration* (SDI profiling, a census boost in informal areas) acts on the evidence state where exclusion from frames is largest; a *survey* with the 1.4.2/Prindex module, Washington Group questions and safety questions closes the gaps of §4.4; a *pilot* with a declared design (peira's designs, randomisation and pre-registered protocol) tests an action before scale. kybernesis treats these as decisions whose value is information; the policy domain's EVSI decides when an inquiry is worth its cost before acting.

#### B.4.8 Results Capture: Showing What an Action Did

A result is a change in the estimated state attributable to an action, with its standing and measure. Four kinds of evidence carry it:

1. **Impact evaluation** with a declared design: randomisation where feasible, otherwise differences in differences, regression discontinuity (as in Michaels et al. **[V]**), synthetic control (peira's `quasi`, `interference` for spillovers such as displacement to neighbouring areas). *Impact Evaluation in Practice* (Gertler et al., 2nd ed., World Bank and IDB 2016, DOI 10.1596/978-1-4648-0779-4) **[V]** is the operational reference.
2. **Monitoring of the indexes** of §3 in treated and comparison areas, disaggregated by sex, disability and tenure; the World Bank Group Scorecard (FY24–FY30) asks for disaggregation by sex, youth and disability where applicable **[S]**.
3. **Process evidence**: grievance logs and their resolution; participation records (who attended, who spoke, which proposals were adopted); coded qualitative evidence from community engagement (zetesis's `coding` with coder agreement, its process tracing with Van Evera's tests for whether the mechanism operated).
4. **Displacement checks**: who left the treated area, measured by panel tracking (Barnhardt et al.'s take-up and exit figures are the model **[V]**), because an area's improvement can be the replacement of its residents.

TDLC's mission is to identify Japanese and global practices and share actionable solutions to increase the impact of World Bank Group-financed urban projects **[V]**; its "results capture" step is where the last kind of evidence matters most, since transfer claims rest on what an action did to the people it was for, not to the place.

---

### B.5. Japanese Practice and the Support Factors for Its Transfer

#### B.5.1 Machizukuri and Its Councils

*Machizukuri* (community-led town making) is formalised in municipal ordinances. Kobe's Ordinance on District Plans and Machizukuri Agreements (December 1981, created alongside the national district-plan system of 1980) **[V]** lets the city certify a *machizukuri kyōgikai* (council) that is formed by a majority of the district's residents, has residents, businesses and property owners (and experts) as members, and has the support of most residents. A certified council may submit a machizukuri proposal, which the city must endeavour to take into account, and may conclude an agreement with the mayor that builders must take into account; the city dispatches experts and subsidises activities. Kobe had 56 certified councils and 20 districts with agreements as of 1 June 2026 **[V]**. After the 1995 earthquake, councils were the vehicle of reconstruction planning: in the Shin-nagata Ekikita land readjustment project, Kobe set up twenty-one Residential Town Planning Committees, supported by dispatched experts, each drafting a land-use plan for its block, which the city adopted into the project, the zoning and a district plan **[V]** (World Bank case study, §5.3). Scholarship notes that engagement is uneven and residents' influence often limited (Sorensen and Funck, eds., *Living Cities in Japan*, 2007) **[S]**.

*Support factors for transfer:* a legal instrument that recognises councils and obliges the city to consider their proposals; a planning tool (district plans) that can encode their agreements; funded expert dispatch; neighbourhood associations with standing; a rule for whom a council represents (owners, tenants, businesses), which a transfer to settlements with many tenants must make explicit.

#### B.5.2 Barrier-Free and Universal Design Law

The Act on Promotion of Smooth Transportation, etc. of Elderly Persons, Disabled Persons, etc. (Act No. 91 of 2006) **[V]** integrated the 1994 "Heart Building" Act and the 2000 Transportation Barrier-Free Act **[V]**. It requires barrier-free standards for new passenger facilities, vehicles, roads, parking, parks and buildings; municipalities endeavour to designate promotion areas with a Master Plan and intensive areas with an Action Plan; these are made with **councils that include elderly persons and persons with disabilities**, and residents may propose plans; the State runs a "spiral-up" council of stakeholders, including persons with disabilities, to evaluate progress regularly. The 2018 amendment (Act No. 32 of 2018) made contributing to an inclusive society and removing social barriers a basic principle and required transport operators to publish annual hard and soft plans; a 2020 amendment (Act No. 28 of 2020) strengthened soft measures (Cabinet Office annual report on persons with disabilities, 2020) **[V]**. TDLC and the QII Partnership held a seminar in Tokyo on financing universal design in World Bank urban projects in October 2023 **[S]**.

*Support factors:* an enforcing building authority and operators able to finance retrofits; an organised disability movement to sit on councils; a monitoring loop (the spiral-up) that publishes progress. Universal design's seven principles (Center for Universal Design, North Carolina State University, 1997) **[M]** and ISO 21542 on the accessibility of the built environment **[M]** are the international references a city would cite.

#### B.5.3 Land Readjustment: Consent, Councils and Continuity of Tenure

The World Bank's case study *Land Readjustment in Japan* (Matsui; ed. Chen, Sakaki and Baranson; disclosed 2019) **[V]** and JICA-RI's *Land Readjustment: Solving Urban Problems Through Innovative Approach* (De Souza, Ochi and Hosono, eds., 2018) **[V]** give the consent process:

- an association (cooperative) project needs the agreement of at least two-thirds of landowners and two-thirds of leaseholders, counted both by number and by area (Land Readjustment Act, art. 18); an individual project with several holders needs all of them; public projects need no consent but are city-planning projects **[V]**;
- local governments, as approvers, generally ask for 80–90 per cent agreement in practice, because opposition raises the project's risk **[V]**;
- objections may be filed with the governor or the minister, who must examine them **[V]**;
- each public project is supervised by a land readjustment council of landowners' and leaseholders' representatives elected by the rights holders, and valuation advisers appointed with the council's consent are consulted on values, reserve land and compensation **[V]**;
- in projects subsidised by the central government, more than half of the land value increase must be recouped through the sale of reserve land **[V]**.

JICA-RI's volume argues that land readjustment keeps "all dwellers (landowners and tenants)" in place and so preserves community, and distributes costs and benefits more equitably than expropriation **[V]**. The consent rule as reported, however, counts owners and *leaseholders of land*; occupiers who rent buildings are not among the counted rights holders in either account. This is the support factor a transfer most needs: in a city where most poor residents are building tenants or informal occupiers, the two-thirds rule does not secure their participation, and their continuity must be provided for by other rules (rehousing in joint apartments, as at Shin-nagata, where eight joint apartment projects housed rights holders **[V]**).

TDLC's Technical Deep Dive on Urban Planning and Land for Growth (Tokyo, Kyoto, Osaka, 27–31 October 2025; about 40 practitioners from 10 client countries) covered land readjustment, digital land management, land value capture and flexible zoning, and named "insecure land tenure, and limited access to essential services for low-income communities" among the challenges **[V]**.

*Support factors for transfer:* a cadastre and registry complete enough to know who holds what; credible valuation; rising land values to finance reserve land; a legal consent threshold; elected councils and valuation advisers; a rule for tenants and informal occupiers; municipal technical capacity (experts dispatched).

#### B.5.4 Public Housing

The Public Housing Act (Act No. 193 of 1951) set up local public rental housing for low-income households; a 1996 revision linked rents to household income and dwelling conditions **[S]**. For transfer, the lesson is the income-linked rent and the register of eligibility, not the scale of construction, which depended on a period of public land and finance unlikely to recur.

---

### B.6. Mapping to kybernesis and the Infrastructure

What the infrastructure can compute now, with modules checked in this session's reading of the repository or named in its CLAUDE.md:

- **Accessibility and unmet need**: nomos `measurement` (cumulative, gravity, 2SFCA, Rural Access Index, Gini of access) and urban science `access` (catchments on a network). 11.2.1 is computable once stops are read; there is no GTFS reader yet (no GTFS code found in the repository), so stops and frequency need a data-library product.
- **Inequality and poverty measures**, **dissimilarity**: nomos `measurement` (the index of dissimilarity is in `specialisation.hpp`). Isolation, exposure and spatial multigroup indices (Reardon–O'Sullivan) are a small addition to nomos.
- **Small-area estimation**: peira `small_areas` (Fay–Herriot EBLUP); the unit-level nested-error model, ELL and the empirical best predictor are planned there.
- **Surveys, weighting, disclosure control**: peira; **coder agreement**, **appraisal**: nomos; **qualitative coding, process tracing, QCA**: zetesis.
- **Readings and standing**: tekmerion `construal`; **needs as gaps judged by standards**: the needs domain.
- **Evidence with custody, codebooks, access rules**: archeion; **claims about rights and their proof**: the law domain's `facts` and `proof`.
- **Policy appraisal, transfer, value of information**: the policy domain; **prediction with an area of applicability**, **change of support**: tyche `spatial`.

New modules the pillar needs: (1) a household deprivation classifier implementing 11.1.1's union rule over declared readings, with its population weights (a module of urban science, or of a planned settlements module); (2) unit-level SAE in peira (already planned); (3) a GTFS product and frequency-aware access in urban science; (4) spatial segregation indices in nomos; (5) a tenure-record model on the LADM/STDM structure (Party – Relationship – Spatial Unit) over archeion's custody and the law domain's facts, whose placement (law, a planned land domain, or archeion) is a decision to propose; (6) ordinal scorecard aggregation respecting scale type (kybernesis's kernel already holds scales and meaningfulness; LGAF and 11.3.2 are its first instances); (7) grievance logs as an evidence stream (tekmerion items sealed in archeion's journal); (8) Washington Group tabulation in peira's surveys (trivial).
