# Chapter C. Resilient Cities

## Part C. Resilient Cities: Disaster Risk Management, Climate Adaptation and Recovery after Conflict

*One part of the survey behind the Urban Analyst's tools for the Urban Studio, built on kybernesis. Written 2026-11-19.*

**Markers.** **[V]** verified this session against a primary record (the issuing body's page, the report itself, a DOI record or a bibliographic index of the publisher); **[S]** confirmed only from secondary sources (search snippets, syndicated copies, news reports); **[M]** from memory, not verified this session. A number carries the marker of the record it came from.

### C.1. Scope, Sources and Method

This part covers the pillar the Tokyo Development Learning Center (TDLC) calls *resilient*: disaster risk management (risk assessment and mapping, risk reduction, preparedness, risk financing), climate adaptation, and recovery after disasters and conflicts. It is organised in three movements. Sections 2 to 4 give the concepts, the international frameworks and the Japanese practice that the World Bank's two Tokyo units share. Section 5, at the coordinator's instruction, restates everything by the blocks of kybernesis, from collection to the capture of results, with a repertoire of actions. Sections 6 to 8 close with three tables: the indexes, the blocks, and the actions.

Two neighbouring parts of the survey cover what this part treats briefly: the methods of estimating risk (probabilistic risk assessment, cascades, criticality of dynamical systems, deep uncertainty), and economic evaluation and allocation, including the design of a risk-finance strategy. Here those methods appear only where a block needs to name them.

*Caveat on verification.* Several issuing bodies refused automated reading this session: UNDRR and PreventionWeb, GFDRR's own pages and the World Bank's Open Knowledge Repository. Where the report itself could be read through a copy, it was read and is marked [V]: the Scorecard's preliminary level, *Lifelines*, the PDNA Guidelines' Volume A and the Cat DDO product note. Otherwise the World Bank's programme pages, its blogs, RePEc's records of World Bank publications and the Japanese ministries' pages served as primary records. What rests on search snippets alone is marked [S].

### C.2. The Concepts beneath Disaster Risk Management

#### C.2.1 Risk as a Function of Hazard, Exposure and Vulnerability

Disaster risk is never observed. It is a distribution of losses implied by three things: a hazard (a law of intensities over places and return periods), an exposure (what stands and who lives where the hazard reaches), and a vulnerability (how what is exposed responds to an intensity), offset by capacity. INFORM states the composition at its most explicit. Risk is the product of the three dimensions (hazard and exposure, vulnerability, lack of coping capacity), each raised to one third, so that "the risk equals zero if one of the three dimensions above is zero" [V: INFORM methodology]. Probabilistic assessments compose the same three factors as a loss distribution. The expected annual loss (EAL) is the integral of the loss-exceedance curve, and the probable maximum loss (PML) at a return period is a quantile of the same curve. Because none of these quantities is ever measured, the *estimation* block of kybernesis carries most of the weight in this pillar. The *evaluation* block then applies operators to an estimated state, never to an observed one.

#### C.2.2 Losses to Assets, to Services and to Well-being

Three evaluation spaces compete, and a tool must declare which it uses.

- **Assets.** The PDNA's *damage* is the "value of total/partial destruction in infrastructure and assets", valued at replacement cost, while *loss* is the "value of changes" in the production and delivery of goods and services, governance and risks [V: PDNA Guidelines Vol. A, 2013].
- **Services.** *Lifelines* estimates that disruptions of power, water and transport cost households and firms in low- and middle-income countries between $391 billion and $647 billion a year [V: Hallegatte, Rentschler and Rozenberg 2019]. Firms' utilisation losses alone come to $151 billion a year, and the direct impacts on people to "at least $90 billion per year" [V].
- **Well-being.** *Unbreakable* measures losses in consumption rather than assets. On average, the poorest 20% bear about 11% of asset losses but 47% of well-being losses [V: World Bank blog, Hallegatte, Walsh and Rentschler 2018]. Socio-economic resilience is "the ratio (0–100%) of expected asset losses to expected well-being losses". At 50%, a dollar of asset loss costs as much well-being as a two-dollar drop in national income [V: Erman and Su 2022, World Bank blog]. The headline estimate of well-being losses, about $520 billion a year across 117 countries, is [S].

The three spaces rank the same city differently: a flood in a wealthy district has large asset losses and small well-being losses, and one in an informal settlement the reverse. kybernesis should therefore hold them as parallel evaluation spaces of one estimated state, not as one index.

#### C.2.3 Resilience as Capacity and as Trajectory

The Sendai Framework defines resilience as "the ability of a system, community or society exposed to hazards to resist, absorb, accommodate, adapt to, transform and recover" [V: quoted in the Scorecard, 2017]. That definition admits two operationalisations:

- **Capacity, judged ex ante.** Scorecards and indices score the institutions, plans and assets that should produce resilience. Their scale is ordinal.
- **Trajectory, measured ex post.** The depth of a loss of function, the time to recovery and the well-being loss integrated over that time are measured after an event, on ratio scales.

*Lifelines* gives a usable trajectory measure for networks: the resilience of a transport network is "the ratio of the loss of functionality to the loss of assets" [V]. In kybernesis, capacities are evaluations of institutions; trajectories are estimated states over time, best handled by survival analysis (peira `survival`) and by kinesis's dynamics.

#### C.2.4 Ordinal Scorecards and What They Do Not License

The Scorecard says plainly what its numbers are:

- the preliminary questions are scored by a scheme "intentionally simple and crude";
- "the scores are not normative and therefore not comparable across different cities";
- cities "are free to apply their own weighting" [V: Scorecard 2017].

The admissible operators follow from that. Within one city, a criterion's level may be compared over time; across criteria, one may count the criteria at each level or take a median. Summing levels across essentials requires a declared weighting, and ranking cities is inadmissible. INFORM, by contrast, normalises its indicators to a common range and takes geometric means [V], which treats them as ratio quantities, a reading the analyst should state as such. This is exactly the role kybernesis gives to a scale type on each evaluation space.

### C.3. Frameworks and Diagnostics

#### C.3.1 The Sendai Framework and Its Thirty-Eight Indicators

The Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030 sets seven global targets, monitored by 38 indicators through the Sendai Framework Monitor, launched in 2018 [S: PreventionWeb, UNDRR].

- **Target A, mortality:** A-1 deaths and missing persons per 100,000 (compound of A-2 and A-3), A-2 deaths, A-3 missing.
- **Target B, people affected:** B-1 directly affected per 100,000 (compound of B-2 to B-5), B-2 injured or ill, B-3 people whose dwellings were damaged, B-4 whose dwellings were destroyed, B-5 whose livelihoods were disrupted or destroyed.
- **Target C, direct economic loss:** C-1 loss in relation to GDP (compound of C-2 to C-6), in agriculture, productive assets, housing, critical infrastructure and cultural heritage.
- **Target D, critical infrastructure and basic services:** D-1 damage to critical infrastructure (compound of D-2 to D-4: health facilities, educational facilities, other units), D-5 disruptions to basic services (compound of D-6 to D-8).
- **Target E, strategies:** E-1 national strategies, E-2 the share of local governments with local strategies.
- **Target F, international cooperation:** F-1 to F-8.
- **Target G, early warning and risk information:** G-1 (compound of G-2 to G-5) multi-hazard early-warning systems; G-2 monitoring and forecasting; G-3 people per 100,000 covered by early-warning information; G-4 the share of local governments with a plan to act on early warnings; G-5 accessible risk information; G-6 the share of people exposed or at risk protected by pre-emptive evacuation following early warning.

The structure is [M]; the wordings of G-1 to G-4, G-6, D-5 and B-3 are [S].

Definitions are fixed in the custodian's metadata [V: World Bank SDG metadata 1.5.1].

- **Death:** "the number of people who died during the disaster, or directly after, as a direct result of the hazardous event".
- **Missing:** people whose whereabouts are unknown since the event.
- **Directly affected:** people injured or ill, evacuated, displaced or relocated, or who suffered direct damage to livelihoods or assets.
- **Indirectly affected:** people who suffer consequences over time from disrupted economies, infrastructure or services.

SDG 1.5.1 is computed as (A2 + A3 + B1) / population × 100,000 [V]. The methodology is the *Technical guidance for monitoring and reporting on progress in achieving the global targets of the Sendai Framework* (UNISDR 2017), and the terminology is that of the intergovernmental expert working group's report A/71/644, endorsed by the General Assembly [V: cited in the same metadata].

For a city, the local quantities are B-3 to B-5, D-2 to D-8, E-2, G-3, G-4 and G-6. Several of them are measured only after an event.

#### C.3.2 The Disaster Resilience Scorecard for Cities and MCR2030

The Scorecard (UNISDR, May 2017, developed with USAID, the European Commission, IBM and AECOM) is built on the Ten Essentials for Making Cities Resilient [V]:

1. organise for resilience;
2. identify, understand and use current and future risk scenarios;
3. strengthen financial capacity for resilience;
4. pursue resilient urban development;
5. safeguard natural buffers to enhance the protective functions offered by natural ecosystems;
6. strengthen institutional capacity for resilience;
7. understand and strengthen societal capacity for resilience;
8. increase infrastructure resilience;
9. ensure effective disaster response;
10. expedite recovery and build back better.

The Scorecard asks the city to identify a "most probable" and a "most severe" scenario for each hazard, and many criteria are scored against those scenarios. It is assessed at two levels [V]:

- **Preliminary level:** "47 questions / indicators, each with a 0–3 score", for a workshop of one or two days.
- **Detailed level:** "117 indicator criteria, each with a score of 0–5", taking one to four months.

Its level descriptors are measurable thresholds dressed as ordinal levels [V]:

- **P9.1 (early warning):** level 3 when over 90% of the population is estimated reachable by the early-warning system, 2 over 75%, 1 over half, 0 under half.
- **P8.7 (health care):** levels by the share of major injuries in the "most severe" scenario treatable within given hours, 0 being longer than 36 hours.
- **P9.7 (drills):** 3 for an annual suite of drills validated as realistic for both scenarios, down to 0 for no exercises.
- **P3.3 (insurance):** 3 when uptake is high across sectors, 2 when it varies but the city promotes it, 1 when it varies and the city does not.

A tool can therefore estimate the quantity beneath a level (the share reachable by warnings) and only then apply the Scorecard's cut-points.

*Making Cities Resilient 2030* (MCR2030) is the partnership that succeeds the 2010–2020 campaign. It was launched in October 2020 and runs from January 2021 to the end of 2030, along a roadmap of three stages [S: mcr2030.undrr.org]:

- **Stage A:** cities know better;
- **Stage B:** cities plan better, using the Scorecard among other tools;
- **Stage C:** cities implement better.

The Scorecard has addenda for extreme heat and for chemicals and waste [S]. An online version has been retired, and the downloadable files remain [S].

#### C.3.3 The City Resilience Index

Arup, with the Rockefeller Foundation, built the City Resilience Index: 52 indicators assessed through 156 questions, qualitative and quantitative, aggregated against 12 goals, and piloted in Shimla, Concepción, Arusha, Hong Kong and Liverpool [V: Arup, updated April 2019]. Its four dimensions (health and well-being; economy and society; infrastructure and ecosystems; leadership and strategy) are [S]. Its 1–5 scoring scale and its seven qualities of resilient systems (reflective, robust, redundant, flexible, resourceful, inclusive, integrated) are [M]. Like the Scorecard, it is a self-assessment whose questions mix quantities and judgements, so it belongs to evaluation, with ordinal scales.

#### C.3.4 The World Bank's City Resilience Program and City Scan

The City Resilience Program (CRP), housed in GFDRR, works through capacity-building workshops on resilience planning. In them, cities learn to prioritise and design resilience investments tied to World Bank operations [V: World Bank Academy]. Its central tool is the *City Scan*, which "combines spatial and socio-economic data so decision-makers can discuss natural hazards and disaster risks and identify priority areas for action" [V]. Secondary accounts describe it as "a conversation starter", a rapid remote assessment before investment planning. They also report that it informed more than US$2.2 billion of resilience investment, and that the programme has worked in some 360 cities [S]. The City Scan's methodology document was not found. Its shape (a remote compilation of open hazard, exposure and facility layers for one city) is essentially what the Urban Studio can already assemble from GHS-POP, Overture, OSM and the hazard layers it reads.

#### C.3.5 Open Risk Information: ThinkHazard!, the Risk Data Library and OpenDRI

- **ThinkHazard!** (GFDRR) screens a project area for eleven hazards: river flood, urban flood, coastal flood, earthquake, landslide, tsunami, volcano, cyclone, water scarcity, extreme heat and wildfire [V: thinkhazard.org]. Its four levels (high, medium, low, very low) come from intensity thresholds at return periods per hazard [M]. The infrastructure already reads these levels.
- **The Risk Data Library Standard (RDLS)**, led by GFDRR with a steering committee that includes the JRC, gives "a common description of the data used and produced in risk assessments", in four components: hazard, exposure, vulnerability, and loss and damage [V: riskdatalibrary.org]. Version 1.0 was under review when the page was read [V]. RDLS is the natural schema for the evidence block's metadata (§5.2).
- **The Open Data for Resilience Initiative (OpenDRI)** and its GeoNode deployments supported community mapping, notably in OpenStreetMap [M].

#### C.3.6 Lifelines and Unbreakable

*Lifelines: The Resilient Infrastructure Opportunity* (Hallegatte, Rentschler and Rozenberg, World Bank 2019, doi:10.1596/978-1-4648-1430-3, CC BY 3.0 IGO) [V] gives this pillar its service-based view.

- **Cost and benefit.** Making exposed assets resilient would add $11–65 billion a year, "only 3 percent of infrastructure investment needs". Over 3,000 scenarios the benefit–cost ratio exceeds 1 in 96% of them, exceeds 2 in 77% and exceeds 6 in 25%. The net present value exceeds $4.2 trillion in half the scenarios, and the median cost of a decade's delay is $1 trillion [V].
- **Networks.** Working at the level of networks (criticality, redundancy, diversification and nature-based solutions) reduces the cost of resilience further [V].
- **Japan.** Many high-income countries "like Japan" implement technical solutions beyond, and more expensive than, the set assessed [V]. This bears directly on transfer (§5.6).

*Unbreakable: Building the Resilience of the Poor in the Face of Natural Disasters* (Hallegatte, Vogt-Schilb, Bangalore and Rozenberg, World Bank 2017) [V: RePEc record] argues that assessments counting only damage miss how disasters affect well-being. It recommends financial inclusion, insurance, social protection, contingent finance and early warning [V]. Its Resilience Indicator covers 117 countries on data of 2015 [S: GFDRR toolbox]. Its follow-up, *Building Back Better* (Hallegatte, Rentschler and Walsh 2018), is cited in §5.7.

#### C.3.7 Disaster Risk Finance

This part only outlines the instruments; their design belongs to the economic part. The World Bank frames finance as **risk layering**: "no single financial instrument can provide suitable and affordable financial protection against all possible risks" [V: DRF Analytics 101, Note 1].

- **Retention:** reserve funds, contingency budgets and contingent credit cover frequent, low-severity events.
- **Transfer:** insurance, parametric insurance and catastrophe bonds or swaps cover rare, severe ones.

The IBRD's **DPF Cat DDO** is a contingent line [V: product note]:

- **Access:** an adequate macroeconomic framework and a satisfactory programme for managing disaster and public-health risks.
- **Size:** a country limit of US$1 billion or 0.5% of GDP, whichever is less.
- **Trigger:** "typically the member country's declaration of a state of emergency".
- **Drawdown period:** three years, renewable up to four times, to a maximum of 15 years.

**CCRIF SPC**, formed in 2007, is "the world's first multi-country, multi-peril risk pool based on parametric insurance" [V: ccrif.org]. It has eight products (tropical cyclone, earthquake, excess rainfall, fluvial flood, utilities, fisheries, livelihood microinsurance), 40 members, and payouts of about US$500 million from 2007 to October 2026 [V]. **Adaptive social protection** is the household layer. Its four building blocks are programmes, information, finance, and institutional arrangements and partnerships, designed so that transfers scale up after shocks [V: Bowen et al. 2020]. Japan's household layer is earthquake insurance, reinsured by the state (§4.8).

#### C.3.8 Early Warning: Early Warnings for All and CREWS

*Early Warnings for All* aims at "every person ... protected by early warning systems by the end of 2027". It is co-led by WMO and UNDRR, supported by ITU and IFRC, and reports that "only half of the countries worldwide report having adequate multi-hazard early warning systems" [V: WMO]. Its four pillars (disaster risk knowledge; observation, monitoring and forecasting; dissemination and communication; preparedness and response) are [S]. The **Climate Risk and Early Warning Systems (CREWS)** initiative is a pooled financing mechanism of WMO, UNDRR and World Bank/GFDRR for the Least Developed Countries and Small Island Developing States [V: WMO 2025]. Since 2015 it has reached over 300 million people [V]. Its 2030 strategy, launched at COP30 on 10 November 2025, aims at essential early-warning and climate services in all of those countries by 2030 [V]. The pillars map onto Sendai's G-indicators and the Scorecard's P9.1.

#### C.3.9 Assessments after Disasters and after Conflicts

The EU, the UN and the World Bank cooperate under a Joint Declaration on Post-Crisis Assessments and Recovery Planning, which aims to avoid duplicated assessments [V: EU FPI]. Its annex was updated by an operational note in 2026 [V].

- **The Post-Disaster Needs Assessment (PDNA)** is country-led. It analyses the context before the disaster, estimates damage and losses, and produces a costed recovery strategy [V]. Volume A (2013) draws on the Damage and Loss Assessment (DaLA) and the Human Recovery Needs Assessment (HRNA) [V]. It defines damage, losses, recovery needs and the human-development impact, and it builds "build back better" into needs [V]. A Disaster Recovery Framework then sets out how the strategy is carried out [V].
- **GRADE**, the World Bank's Global Rapid post-disaster Damage Estimation, is a remote, desk-based first estimate, delivered within two to three weeks [S]. It combines hazard modelling with capital-stock values and does not substitute for a PDNA [S]. Its estimate after Hurricane Dorian in The Bahamas took 13 days [S].
- **The Recovery and Peacebuilding Assessment (RPBA)**, formerly the Post-Conflict Needs Assessment, has three phases: pre-assessment; assessment, giving a costed, prioritised plan; and validation, with financing arrangements [V: EU FPI]. Its reference is *Joint Recovery and Peacebuilding Assessments: A Practical Note to Assessment and Planning* (2017), and it typically includes a Risk and Resilience Assessment [S].
- **Ukraine's Rapid Damage and Needs Assessment (RDNA4)** was released on 25 February 2025 by the Government of Ukraine, the World Bank Group, the European Commission and the UN [V: Government of Ukraine]. It put direct damage at US$176 billion and needs over ten years at US$524 billion, as of 31 December 2024, with housing the largest need at almost US$84 billion [V]. It "incorporates the build back better approach" and excludes over US$13 billion of needs already met [V].
- **TDLC's Technical Deep Dive** on *Integrated Urban Recovery and Transformation in Ukraine* ("Reconstructing Cities Amid Uncertainty"), illustrated by Hiroshima [V: TDLC page], is the unit's own bridge between Japanese reconstruction and recovery after conflict.

#### C.3.10 Composite Risk Indices: INFORM

INFORM (European Commission JRC and partners) has three dimensions, each of two categories combined by geometric means [V]:

- **hazard and exposure:** natural and human-induced hazards;
- **vulnerability:** socio-economic vulnerability and vulnerable groups;
- **lack of coping capacity:** institutional and infrastructure capacity.

Its 0–10 scale and subnational models are [M]. Being multiplicative, it makes a city with no exposure riskless whatever its vulnerability: right as arithmetic, and a reading to declare as policy.

#### C.3.11 Loss Databases

- **EM-DAT** (CRED, UCLouvain; created in 1988 with WHO) records a disaster that meets at least one of four criteria: 10 deaths, 100 people affected, a declared state of emergency, or a call for international assistance [V: emdat.be]. It covers 1900 onwards and is open for non-commercial use [V].
- **DesInventar Sendai** (UNDRR) is the loss database aligned with the Sendai Framework Monitor [V]. Its 65 datasets of 82 countries were updated for GAR 2015 [V], and its server software is Apache-2.0 [V]. That it records small, local events, disaggregated by municipality, is [M].

The two databases are biased in opposite directions (§5.2).

### C.4. Japanese Practice Shared by the Tokyo Hubs

#### C.4.1 The Two Hubs and Their Knowledge Products

The **Tokyo Disaster Risk Management Hub** was established in 2014 "in acknowledgement of the learning emerging from the 2011 Great East Japan Earthquake and Tsunami" [V: World Bank]. It is managed by GFDRR and runs the Japan–World Bank Program for Mainstreaming DRM in Developing Countries: over 300 projects in more than 110 countries, with over $190 million committed as of January 2026 [V]. Its themes are policy and institutions, resilient infrastructure, risk finance, hydromet and early warning, and urban flood management [V]. Its knowledge products include [V: titles from the Hub's page]:

- *Learning from Japan's Experience in Integrated Urban Flood Risk Management* (2020), with four knowledge notes: assessing and communicating risk; planning and prioritising investments; designing and implementing them; operating and maintaining them;
- *Building Regulation for Resilience* (2016) and *Converting Disaster Experience into a Safer Built Environment: The Case of Japan* (2018);
- *Modernization of Japan's Hydromet Services* (2016) and *Making Schools Resilient at Scale: the Case of Japan* (2016);
- *Road Geohazard Risk Management Handbook* (2020) and *Dam Safety Management in Japan* (2025);
- *Seismic Resilience: Experience and Lessons Learned from Japan* (2025);
- *Driving Policy Reform for Disaster Resilience: Lessons from World Bank DPF with a Cat DDO* (2025).

*Learning from Megadisasters: Lessons from the Great East Japan Earthquake* (Ranghieri and Ishiwatari, eds., World Bank 2014) consolidates 36 Knowledge Notes of a joint study by the Government of Japan and the World Bank [V: RePEc]. They are arranged in seven clusters:

1. structural measures;
2. non-structural measures;
3. emergency response;
4. reconstruction planning;
5. hazard and risk information and decision making;
6. economics of disaster risk, risk management and risk financing;
7. recovery and relocation.

**TDLC** runs four activities: Technical Deep Dives (one-week programmes for officials and World Bank teams), operational support, partnership and collaboration, and outreach [V: World Bank]. It has long-standing partnerships with seven Japanese cities [V; their names are not on the page]. Its resilience offer includes a 2026 Deep Dive on *Strengthening Disaster and Climate Resilience in Cities* (urban flooding) and a brief, *Building Urban Flood Resilience: Lessons from Shizuoka City* [V]. It has also launched Practitioner Programs [V; details truncated].

#### C.4.2 Two Levels of Design Event, the Five-Minute Town and Evacuation on Foot

After 2011, the Central Disaster Management Council's expert committee (report of 28 September 2011) found three faults: earlier scenarios far below what happened, over-reliance on coastal structures, and warnings below the real event [V: Cabinet Office summary]. It required that tsunami countermeasures assume **two levels** [V]:

- **The largest class.** For the tsunami of very low frequency and enormous damage, the aim is protecting lives first, "with residents' evacuation as the axis".
- **The frequent class.** For the tsunami of higher frequency and lower height, coastal protection facilities continue to be built, to protect lives, property and economic activity.

The same report set out four further principles [V]:

- where the tsunami arrives quickly, towns should aim for evacuation "in roughly five minutes" (概ね５分程度で避難できるようなまちづくり);
- evacuation on foot remains the principle, with safe ways of evacuating by car to be studied;
- multiple lines of defence (多重防護) include raised roads as second embankments;
- public, welfare and health facilities should be sited where the largest tsunami does not reach.

This is the clearest Japanese example of a *declared reading* of an open term: "design event" is read twice, and each reading has its own instrument. It also gives the Urban Studio's evacuation-on-foot bounds a standard to test against.

#### C.4.3 Building Codes and Seismic Retrofit

Japan's seismic code was revised in 1981, the "new seismic standard" (新耐震) [M]. MLIT estimates the seismic adequacy rate of housing in 2023 at about 90%: about 50.0 of 55.7 million dwellings, about 85% for detached houses and 96% for apartments [V: MLIT 2025]. The rate rose from about 75% in 2003 [V]. On 17 July 2025 the basic policy under the retrofit promotion act moved the target, "largely eliminate housing with insufficient seismic performance", from 2030 to 2035 [V]. Buildings along designated evacuation routes stood at about 46% adequacy against a target of 60% by 2030 [V]. A dwelling counts as adequate if built after 1981 or found adequate by diagnosis [V: the table splits stock at S55/S56]. That is a declared reading of "seismically safe".

#### C.4.4 Hazard Information and the Five Alert Levels

JMA and the municipalities align their information on five alert levels (警戒レベル) [V: JMA]:

- **Level 5:** emergency safety measures (緊急安全確保), JMA's special warnings;
- **Level 4:** evacuation instruction (避難指示), JMA's danger warnings (危険警報);
- **Level 3:** evacuation of the elderly and others (高齢者等避難), warnings;
- **Level 2:** advisories, and checking the hazard map;
- **Level 1:** early attention information.

JMA's page now refers to the Disaster Management Agency's guideline [V]. The revision of May 2021 abolished the evacuation advisory (避難勧告) and unified level 4 as the evacuation instruction [S: prefectural pages]. The national Hazard Map Portal, run by MLIT and the Geospatial Information Authority [M], is already read by the infrastructure for Miyako.

#### C.4.5 Community Disaster Prevention Organisations

Voluntary disaster prevention organisations (自主防災組織) are "voluntary organisations based on residents' sense of solidarity" [V: FDMA White Paper 2024]. On 1 April 2024 there were 167,233 of them in 1,697 of Japan's 1,741 municipalities [V]. Their *activity coverage rate* (活動カバー率) is the share of all households within the activity area of such an organisation [V]. The national average was 85.4%, with Hyogo highest at 98.4% [S: Shizuoka Prefecture, citing the White Paper]. In 2023, 1,024 municipalities subsidised them, ¥3.61 billion in total [V]. Coverage is a household-weighted share, so it says nothing about how active an organisation is. An evaluation must keep the two apart.

#### C.4.6 River and Urban Flood Management

The **Metropolitan Area Outer Underground Discharge Channel** (首都圏外郭放水路, MLIT Edogawa River Office) reduces inundation in the Naka and Ayase basins. It was built from March 1993 to June 2006 and operated provisionally from June 2002 [V: MLIT]. Its tunnel of about 6.3 km at some 50 m depth, and its pumping capacity of 200 m³/s, are [S]. Japan's turn to **river-basin flood control** (流域治水), which shares flood management among the river, the basin's land and its users, and the 2021 laws that support it, are [M]. The urban flood knowledge notes (§4.1) are the Hub's codified version.

#### C.4.7 Land Use in Hazard Zones

The 2020 amendments to the Act on Special Measures concerning Urban Reconstruction and related acts did four things [S: MLIT summaries]:

- they excluded "disaster red zones" (disaster hazard areas, landslide special warning areas, landslide prevention areas, steep-slope collapse hazard areas) from residential inducement areas in principle;
- they required a "disaster prevention guideline" (防災指針) in location normalisation plans (立地適正化計画);
- they prohibited development of housing and of one's own business premises in red zones throughout city planning areas;
- they tightened permission for housing in inundation hazard areas of urbanisation control areas.

These are the Japanese instruments closest to the "relocation" and "land-use regulation" actions of §5.7.

#### C.4.8 Earthquake Insurance

Japanese earthquake insurance is attached to fire insurance and reinsured by the government [M for the reinsurance]. MOF reports two indexes with different denominators [V: MOF 2025]:

- **Attachment rate** (付帯率), the share of new housing fire policies with earthquake cover: 69.7% in FY2023;
- **Household penetration** (世帯加入率), policies in force over households in the resident register: 35.1% in 2023.

MOF warns that the first measures uptake among fire-policy holders only, and that outside the large cities many households rely on mutual-aid schemes (共済) [V]. The FY2024 attachment rate of 70.4% is [S]. This is a model case of one open term ("insured") with two admissible readings, each with its source.

#### C.4.9 Institutions

Japan's **Disaster Management Agency** (防災庁) was launched on 2 November 2026 under the Prime Minister, with a dedicated minister [V: Cabinet Office]. Its role is to be "the command centre of disaster response", from prevention through first response, shelters and supplies, to recovery and reconstruction, under five principles of conduct [V]. Its staff of 352, its power to make recommendations to ministries, and regional bureaux for the Nankai Trough and the Japan and Chishima Trenches from FY2027 are [S: news reports]. The Disaster Countermeasures Basic Act (1961) and the Reconstruction Agency for Tōhoku are [M].

#### C.4.10 Reconstruction after War

Hiroshima's reconstruction under the Hiroshima Peace Memorial City Construction Law (1949) [M] is the Japanese case TDLC uses for Ukraine (§3.9). Its transfer to a city under continuing attack would need support factors that differ from those of disaster recovery (§5.6).

### C.5. The Survey by Block of kybernesis

kybernesis steers a system known through evidence:

1. collection;
2. evidence;
3. interpretation;
4. estimation;
5. evaluation;
6. policy making;
7. policy and decision, an intervention or an inquiry.

The sections below take the blocks in order and add the capture of results, the last step of TDLC's Practitioner Programs.

#### C.5.1 Collection

A design and its observation mechanism come first. In this pillar the mechanisms are of five kinds.

- **Hazard models and maps.** Each is computed by a model whose design (return periods, defended or undefended, fluvial or pluvial, resolution) is part of the observation.
  - JRC's global river flood hazard maps give depths for seven return periods from 10 to 500 years at about 90 m, from LISFLOOD and LISFLOOD-FP, without restriction on use [S: JRC README via search].
  - Fathom's global flood maps are commercial [M]. UNDRR's Global Assessment Report (GAR) risk data are [M].
  - GEM's OpenQuake engine runs probabilistic seismic hazard and risk under AGPL [S].
  - National portals (Japan's Hazard Map Portal) give legally designated zones [M].
- **Exposure inventories.** Census and grid population (GHS-POP, already read), building footprints and heights (Overture, OSM), facilities and networks (OSM), and asset values from national accounts or capital-stock models (GRADE's method [S]).
- **Loss records after events.** EM-DAT, DesInventar, national damage reports, PDNA and GRADE estimates, damage surveys by building, post-event imagery (Copernicus EMS rapid mapping [M]), and insurers' claims.
- **Household surveys.** Savings, insurance, access to transfers and recovery after shocks, as *Unbreakable* requires. This is peira's ground: a design (strata, clusters), a weighting, and a disclosure control before release.
- **Institutional assessments.** The Scorecard in workshop (multi-stakeholder, "the conversation is often as important as the score" [V]), the CRI, building regulatory capacity assessments [V: Hub titles], and community risk mapping.

The analyst should record the mechanism as a design. A workshop score, a satellite damage map and a census count are different coarsenings of one state, and peira's theory (full data coarsened by a mechanism) gives the common language. *Drills* are a collection design too: an evacuation drill is an experiment on the evacuation system (§5.7.6).

#### C.5.2 Evidence: Traces, Provenance, Licence, Resolution and Biases

Each trace should carry its provenance (archeion), its licence, its resolution in space and time, and its known biases.

- **Hazard maps.**
  - *Model and scenario.* A map is a model output for a scenario, with an epistemic error that is not usually published.
  - *Defences.* Whether flood defences are represented changes everything behind a levee. Undefended maps overstate risk there [M for JRC's treatment].
  - *Resolution.* At ~90 m a global map cannot resolve streets, so pluvial (surface-water) flooding, the urban flood of TDLC's deep dive, is mostly missing.
  - *Licence.* JRC's maps carry no restriction on use [S]; Fathom's are commercial [M]; Japanese portals are public [M].
- **Exposure.**
  - *Population.* GHS-POP is a residential distribution, not the population present at the hour of a flood [M]. OSM and Overture are incomplete in informal settlements, exactly where vulnerability is greatest.
  - *Buildings.* Heights are often defaults, and the infrastructure already marks them paler.
- **Loss databases.**
  - *Thresholds.* EM-DAT's threshold (10 deaths, 100 affected, a declaration or an appeal [V]) drops small, frequent events.
  - *Reporting.* Its counts of "affected" depend on national reporting. Losses are reported more fully where insured.
  - *Scale.* DesInventar records small events municipality by municipality [M] and so captures the "extensive risk" EM-DAT misses. The two should be read together, never summed.
- **Post-event estimates.** GRADE is a first-order remote figure [S]. PDNA values damage at replacement cost and losses as changed flows [V]. RDNA4 values needs, not damage alone, and excludes needs already met [V]. Each answers a different question.
- **Scorecards.** Self-assessed and "unavoidably subjective". The Scorecard asks that each score's justification be recorded so that it can be validated [V], which makes the justification the trace and the score its reading.
- **Conflict settings.** Access is partial, data contested and attribution political. Evidence may be withheld for safety. Here disclosure control (peira `disclosure`) and custody (archeion) are matters of protecting people, not of formality.

The Risk Data Library Standard's four components [V] give the metadata schema. tekmerion's `located` evidence (an item's force diluted by its disc and span within a claim's) is the right model for a damage report placed approximately in space and time.

#### C.5.3 Interpretation: Open Terms and Their Admissible Readings

Every index in this pillar contains an open term. Each needs declared readings, each reading with its source, so that a conclusion's standing (clear, arguable or untenable) can be computed over them on tekmerion's `construal`.

- **Affected.** Directly or indirectly affected (Sendai's metadata [V]), or EM-DAT's "affected", requiring immediate assistance [M]. B-1 counts only the direct.
- **Disaster.** EM-DAT's four criteria [V]; a national declaration (which also triggers a Cat DDO [V]); a parametric trigger (CCRIF [V]). One event may be a disaster under one reading and not under another, with money depending on it.
- **Critical infrastructure.** Sendai D-2 to D-4 [S] name health, education and "other" units. The Scorecard's Essential 8 lists water, energy, transport, communications, health care and education [V]. A city must declare its list.
- **Design event / return period.**
  - Japan's tsunami levels L1 and L2 [V].
  - A river plan's design flood [M].
  - The Scorecard's "most probable" and "most severe" scenarios [V].
  - Sendai's targets fix none.
- **Exposed / in the hazard zone.** Any depth > 0, depth above a threshold (JRC's classes <1, 1–3, 3–10, >10 m [S]), or a legally designated zone such as a red zone [S]. A building's exposure also depends on its footprint versus centroid.
- **Reachable by early warning.** The Scorecard's "estimated reachable" [V]; Sendai G-3, covered "through local governments or national dissemination mechanisms" [S]. One is a capacity, the other a count.
- **Evacuated in time.** Arrival within the warning lead time; within five minutes where tsunamis arrive quickly [V]; on foot [V]. The walking speed is a reading too, with the elderly slower.
- **Seismically adequate.** Built after 1981 or diagnosed adequate [V], or meeting the current code.
- **Insured.** MOF's two readings: attachment rate or household penetration [V].
- **Covered by a community organisation.** Households within an activity area [V], or organisations shown active by drills.
- **Damage, loss, need.** PDNA's three [V]; RDNA4 adds needs already met [V].
- **Recovered.** A return to the pre-event level; to the counterfactual trend; or *Unbreakable*'s consumption-equivalent loss made good.
- **Build back better.** Stronger, faster, more inclusive [V: Wahba and Hallegatte 2018], or PDNA's risk-reduction share of needs [V].
- **In conflict settings.** "Displaced", "returned", "destroyed by" (attribution), "safe to return" (unexploded ordnance), and property rights. Readings may come from parties to the conflict, so the source of each reading matters more than anywhere else.

#### C.5.4 Estimation: The State Variables

The city's state, as this pillar needs it, has six parts. Most are poorly observed and better estimated, as the list states.

1. **Hazard fields** at return periods and their change with climate. Never observed as a law, they are estimated by hazard models, with tyche's spatial change of support between grids and kinesis for dynamics.
2. **Exposure by place and hour.** Observed in part (census, footprints). Better estimated:
   - daytime population;
   - informal settlements;
   - building type and value, by tyche's dasymetric carriage and peira's small areas.
3. **Vulnerability.**
   - *Physical.* Fragility and vulnerability curves by building type, the type itself rarely recorded.
   - *Social.* A latent quantity. Cutter's SoVI reduced 42 variables to 11 factors explaining about 76% of the variance [V], with tyche's `latent` the method.
4. **Service networks.** Their topology is observed (OSM, utilities), but the state after a shock is estimated, along with the cascade between networks and the time to restoration. Already partly in the infrastructure: road criticality to care, station coverage under floods, water-network criticality.
5. **Household finances.** Savings, insurance, transfers and their scalability are observed only by surveys. Well-being losses are a model's output: *Unbreakable*'s is a household model, and nomos's `microsimulation` is the host.
6. **Institutional capacity.** Observed only as scorecard judgements, so it is better treated as a latent trait measured by ordinal items. nomos's `psychometrics` (item response theory) could estimate it with an error, rather than summing levels.

After an event the state is re-estimated. GRADE's remote estimate comes first, then PDNA's [S/V]. Recovery is a trajectory, best estimated by survival models (peira `survival`) on records of reopenings, rebuildings and returns. Bayesian networks (tyche `graphical`) can join imagery, reports and models into a damage posterior.

The probabilistic assessment of risk itself (EAL, PML, cascades, deep uncertainty) belongs to the neighbouring part on methods.

#### C.5.5 Evaluation: Operators, Scale Types and Standards

Operators applied in parallel to the estimated state:

- **Loss operators:** EAL as the integral of the exceedance curve; PML at a return period as a quantile; *Lifelines*' service loss in person-days or currency; *Unbreakable*'s well-being loss in consumption-equivalent currency; socio-economic resilience as a ratio in [0, 1] [V]. All on ratio scales.
- **Rates:** Sendai's per 100,000 and per GDP [V]. Ratio scales. Their standard is relative ("substantially reduce"), so they are judged against the city's own baseline (2005–2015 for Sendai [M]).
- **Coverage shares:**
  - people reachable by warnings;
  - people within an evacuation time of a designated site;
  - shelter places over people needing them;
  - households covered by community organisations;
  - insured dwellings.

  Ratio scales, often cut into ordinal levels by the Scorecard's thresholds [V].
- **Scorecard levels:** ordinal 0–3 or 0–5 [V]. Within-city change only, with no cross-city comparison [V].
- **Composite indices:** INFORM's geometric means [V]; the CRI's goals [V]. Their scale type is an aggregation choice that must be declared, and nomos's composite indicators hold the method.
- **Problems and needs:** gaps judged by standards (Bradshaw's normative, felt, expressed, comparative, as in the needs domain). Examples: housing below the seismic standard; schools in the "most severe" scenario's zone (Scorecard P8.8 [V]); people more than five minutes from height [V].

Who holds the standards:

- national law: Japan's building code and retrofit targets [V], red zones [S];
- national agencies: JMA's levels [V], the new Disaster Management Agency [V];
- international frameworks: Sendai, relative targets; the Scorecard, levels;
- humanitarian standards: the Sphere Handbook's living space per person, 3.5 m² [M];
- financiers: Cat DDO access criteria [V], insurers' terms;
- the city itself, through its plan.

A judgement should always say whose standard it applies.

#### C.5.6 Policy Making: Options, Appraisal, Selection and Transfer

- **Options** are drawn from the repertoire (§5.7) and composed into portfolios. Risk layering pairs reduction with retention and transfer [V].
- **Appraisal.** Benefit–cost analysis under uncertainty (*Lifelines*' 3,000 scenarios [V]), multi-criteria appraisal with non-compensatory screening (nomos `appraisal`), and methods for deep uncertainty: real options and robust decision making (Hallegatte et al. 2012 [V]), and dynamic adaptive policy pathways (Haasnoot et al. 2013 [V]; Marchau et al. 2019 [S]). These are treated in the neighbouring parts.
- **Selection** under a budget with the value of information: tyche's decision, and the policy domain's adopt, reject or study first.

**Transfer** is a lens valid when its support factors hold: Cartwright and Hardie's argument, already in tekmerion's `warrant` and the policy domain's `transfer`. For each Japanese practice, the support factors a transfer needs:

- **Hazard maps and risk communication.** They need hazard data at street resolution, a legal mandate to publish them, and a channel by which residents read them. The Cabinet Office notes that distributing a map is not enough [V].
- **Two-level design events.** These need a credible estimate of the largest event (Japan used historical documents and tsunami deposits [V]) and the capacity to build for the frequent one.
- **The five-minute town.** It needs vertical evacuation structures or high ground within reach, a walking population, and warnings with lead time. A car-dependent or mobility-impaired population breaks it.
- **Community disaster organisations.** These need neighbourhood associations or equivalent social fabric, municipal subsidy [V], and drills. They transfer poorly where tenure is insecure or residents move often.
- **Building codes and retrofit.** These need enforcement capacity, which the Hub's building regulatory capacity assessments diagnose [V], a construction industry able to comply, and finance for retrofit. Japan took four decades from 1981 to reach about 90% adequacy [V].
- **Large flood infrastructure** like the outer discharge channel. It needs a basin's hydrology, capital, operation and maintenance budgets, and an institution to run it, and it invites the levee effect (§5.7.1).
- **Earthquake insurance.** It needs government reinsurance [M], affordable premiums and trust in payouts. Even in Japan, household penetration is about a third [V].
- **Early warning.** It needs dense observation networks, a mandated national service, and dissemination channels. Japan's hydromet modernisation is documented for transfer [V: Hub titles].
- **Reconstruction after war.** Hiroshima's path [M] presumed peace, secure tenure and national finance. Transfer to Ukraine needs readings of uncertainty that Hiroshima's planners did not face, which is what TDLC's "Reconstructing Cities Amid Uncertainty" names [V].

#### C.5.7 Actions: The Repertoire of Interventions

Each action is stated by its mechanism, the states it acts on, the evidence that would show it worked, its typical risks, an example, and the support factors for its transfer. Table 8 summarises them.

##### C.5.7.1 Risk Reduction: Structural and Non-structural

- **Building codes and seismic retrofit.**
  - *Mechanism:* lowers vulnerability curves of new and existing stock.
  - *Acts on:* vulnerability.
  - *Evidence it worked:* adequacy rate rising, and damage ratios in later events lower by vintage (e.g. post-1981 versus earlier).
  - *Risks:* non-enforcement; retrofit reaching the wealthy first.
  - *Example:* Japan, ~90% adequacy of housing, target moved to 2035 [V].
  - *Support:* enforcement capacity and finance.
- **Land-use regulation in hazard zones.**
  - *Mechanism:* lowers exposure growth by excluding or conditioning development.
  - *Acts on:* exposure.
  - *Evidence:* new buildings in zones before and after, by difference in differences on peira's `quasi`.
  - *Risks:* displacement of development to unmapped hazards; informal building; inequity.
  - *Example:* Japan's 2020 exclusion of red zones [S].
  - *Support:* hazard zoning with legal force and enforcement.
- **Drainage and flood defences.**
  - *Mechanism:* lowers hazard intensity for events up to the design level.
  - *Acts on:* hazard as experienced.
  - *Evidence:* flooded area and damage for comparable rainfall before and after.
  - *Risks:* the **levee effect**, where flood protection that suppresses frequent floods "is often associated to increasing vulnerability" [S: Di Baldassarre et al. 2015]; residual risk above the design event.
  - *Example:* the outer discharge channel [V/S].
  - *Support:* capital, operation and maintenance, basin governance.
- **Nature-based solutions.**
  - *Mechanism:* wetlands, mangroves and green infrastructure attenuate hazards.
  - *Acts on:* hazard and the city's natural buffers, the Scorecard's Essential 5 [V].
  - *Evidence:* attenuation measured; maintenance of the ecosystem.
  - *Risks:* uncertain performance at extremes; land competition.
  - *Example:* the Scorecard's P5.1–5.3 [V]; *Lifelines* names nature-based options among network solutions [V].
  - *Support:* land, ecology, and monitoring.
- **Relocation.**
  - *Mechanism:* removes exposure permanently.
  - *Acts on:* exposure.
  - *Evidence:* people and assets out of the zone, and livelihoods kept.
  - *Risks:* livelihood loss, refusal, the new site's own hazards, and **maladaptation**, a response that increases vulnerability for other systems, sectors or groups [S: Barnett and O'Neill 2010].
  - *Example:* Tōhoku's group relocation to higher ground [M]; *Learning from Megadisasters*' cluster 7 [V].
  - *Support:* land, consent and finance.

##### C.5.7.2 Preparedness

- **Early warning.**
  - *Mechanism:* lead time converts exposure into evacuation.
  - *Acts on:* exposure at the moment of impact.
  - *Evidence:* Sendai G-3 and G-6 [S], warning lead times, false-alarm rates.
  - *Risks:* cry-wolf erosion; warnings below the real event, the 2011 lesson [V].
  - *Example:* JMA's levels [V]; CREWS [V].
  - *Support:* observation, a mandated service, dissemination channels.
- **Evacuation planning and drills.**
  - *Mechanism:* routes, sites and practised behaviour shorten the time to safety.
  - *Acts on:* evacuation time and its distribution over people.
  - *Evidence:* drill times against the bound; the share evacuated in real events.
  - *Risks:* drills unrepresentative of night, rain or crowds; dependence on cars.
  - *Example:* the five-minute town and evacuation on foot [V]; the Scorecard's P9.7 [V].
  - *Support:* reachable high ground or structures, and community organisation.
- **Shelters.**
  - *Mechanism:* capacity for those displaced.
  - *Acts on:* the welfare of the displaced.
  - *Evidence:* places over need in the "most severe" scenario (Scorecard P9.5 [V]).
  - *Risks:* shelters within hazard zones; inaccessibility for disabled people.
  - *Example:* Japan's designated sites and shelters, read by the infrastructure for Miyako.
  - *Support:* buildings and supplies.
- **Community organisations.**
  - *Mechanism:* neighbours help in the first hours, before official response.
  - *Acts on:* response capacity.
  - *Evidence:* coverage [V] and drill participation.
  - *Risks:* coverage without activity.
  - *Example:* 自主防災組織, 85.4% coverage [S].
  - *Support:* social fabric.
- **Contingency planning and business continuity.**
  - *Mechanism:* pre-agreed roles and supplies.
  - *Acts on:* the time to response.
  - *Evidence:* exercises and after-action reviews.
  - *Risks:* plans unused.
  - *Example:* the Disaster Management Agency's first request, to check business continuity plans and seismic adequacy [S].

##### C.5.7.3 Response and Recovery

- **Build back better.**
  - *Mechanism:* reconstruction stronger, faster, more inclusive.
  - *Acts on:* future vulnerability and the duration of present losses.
  - *Evidence:* recovery time; vulnerability of the rebuilt stock.
  - *Risks:* slower recovery if "better" delays; exclusion of renters and the informal.
  - *Example:* globally about $173 billion a year, or 31% of well-being losses. Building stronger alone gives 12%; reconstruction faster by two thirds gives 14% [V: Wahba and Hallegatte 2018].
- **PDNA-based recovery frameworks.**
  - *Mechanism:* a costed, prioritised strategy agreed by government and partners.
  - *Acts on:* the allocation of recovery finance.
  - *Evidence:* needs met over time.
  - *Risks:* assessment fatigue; needs inflated or omitted.
  - *Example:* PDNA and its Disaster Recovery Framework [V].
- **Post-conflict reconstruction.**
  - *Mechanism:* RPBA's prioritised plan with political dialogue.
  - *Acts on:* services, housing, institutions and trust.
  - *Evidence:* returns, services restored, conflict risk.
  - *Risks:* rebuilding into contested property; renewed attack.
  - *Example:* RDNA4's US$524 billion over ten years [V]; TDLC's Ukraine deep dive [V].
  - *Support:* security, tenure, finance.

##### C.5.7.4 Risk Financing

The instruments are named here only; their design is the economic part's.

- **Reserves and contingency budgets** for frequent events [V].
- **Contingent credit** (Cat DDO: liquidity on a declaration, conditioned on a reform programme [V]). Its risk is a trigger that comes late.
- **Indemnity insurance**, with moral hazard and adverse selection [M].
- **Parametric insurance and CAT bonds**, which pay fast on an index (CCRIF [V]). Their risk is **basis risk**, a payout that does not match the loss [M].
- **Shock-responsive social protection** (adaptive social protection's four blocks [V]). Its risk is targeting error.

The evidence each would show working: time to liquidity, the share of losses financed, and households' consumption smoothed. The Lifelines plug-in already composes the layers of budget, contingent credit and insurance for Freetown.

##### C.5.7.5 Institutions

- **DRM agencies with authority over ministries:** Japan's agency of 2 November 2026 [V].
- **Legal frameworks:** a basic act [M]; hazard-zone law [S]; Sendai's E-1 and E-2 [S].
- **Risk information systems:** RDLS [V]; open data; national portals.
- **Hydromet services:** the Hub's modernisation reports [V].

The evidence they worked is slow: Scorecard levels rising and Sendai reporting complete. Their typical risk is a mandate without a budget.

##### C.5.7.6 Inquiries

kybernesis's decision is an intervention or an inquiry. The inquiries of this pillar:

- **Risk assessments.** A City Scan [V], a probabilistic assessment, a Scorecard workshop [V].
- **Studies whose value of information is computed first.** tyche's `decision` gives the tests worth running within a budget.
- **Drills treated as experiments.** An evacuation drill tests the bound the model predicts. A drill's times are the only direct evidence of evacuation capacity before an event.
- **Post-event reviews.** GRADE, then PDNA, then process tracing of what failed (zetesis `tracing`).

#### C.5.8 Results Capture

TDLC's programme ends with results capture. In this pillar, results are slow to arrive and rarely attributable, so capture must be designed in advance.

- **Outputs and capacities.** Scorecard levels re-scored on the same criteria with recorded justifications [V]; the MCR2030 stage reached [S]; plans adopted (Sendai E-2).
- **Exposure and vulnerability.** Adequacy rates [V], zone occupancy, insurance indexes [V] and community coverage [V], tracked as time series with their readings fixed.
- **Event outcomes.** Sendai's A to D after each event, against the city's baseline. Since events are rare and differ, compare like events (by intensity) before and after an intervention, or estimate avoided losses by modelling the counterfactual. Both are estimates with their own standing.
- **Drills and exercises.** Timed results against the evacuation bound; the Scorecard's P9.7 requires annual validation [V].
- **Finance.** Days to liquidity, and payouts against losses.

The record should be sealed (archeion's journal) so that a later claim of success can be checked against what was declared before the event.

### C.6. Table of the Indexes

| Index | Definition | Unit and scale type | Open terms needing readings | Evidence (source, licence) | Method | kybernesis block | In the infrastructure |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Hazard intensity at return period | Depth, velocity or acceleration exceeded with annual probability 1/T | m, m/s or g; ratio | Defended or undefended; fluvial or pluvial; design T | JRC flood maps, no restriction [S]; Fathom, commercial [M]; national portals [M]; OpenQuake models, AGPL [S] | Hazard modelling; change of support | Collection → Estimation | Reads JRC-type layers, Freetown's maps and Japan's portal; tyche `spatial` change of support |
| Exposed population | People within a hazard zone | Persons, share; ratio | "Exposed" threshold; residential versus daytime population | GHS-POP [M licence]; census | Overlay; dasymetric carriage | Estimation | urban-science `exposure`; tyche `spatial` |
| Exposed buildings and assets | Buildings or value within a zone | Count, m², currency; ratio | Footprint or centroid; value basis | Overture, OSM (ODbL) [M]; capital stock | Overlay; valuation | Estimation | `exposure` (Freetown's 153,435 buildings) |
| Physical vulnerability | Damage ratio as a function of intensity | [0, 1] per intensity; ratio | Building typology | Fragility libraries; damage surveys | Curve fitting; Bayesian updating | Estimation | New (vulnerability curves module) |
| Social vulnerability | Latent susceptibility of a population | Score; interval at best | Variable set; weighting | Census (Cutter 2003: 42 variables to 11 factors [V]) | Factor analysis | Estimation → Evaluation | tyche `latent`; peira `small_areas`; new composition |
| Expected annual loss (EAL) | Integral of the loss-exceedance curve | Currency/yr; ratio | Loss definition (damage or loss); T range | Event sets; loss tables | Probabilistic risk assessment | Evaluation | tyche `exceedance`; `exposure` (Freetown's EAL to the cent) |
| Probable maximum loss (PML) | Loss at return period T | Currency; ratio | T chosen | As EAL | Quantile of exceedance curve | Evaluation | tyche `exceedance` |
| Service disruption | Users × duration without a service | Person-days or currency; ratio | "Critical"; outage threshold | Utilities' logs; network models | Network criticality; *Lifelines* [V] | Estimation → Evaluation | Road criticality to care; water `criticality`; station coverage |
| Network resilience | Loss of functionality / loss of assets | Ratio | Functionality metric | Network and hazard | Simulation [V: *Lifelines*] | Evaluation | Partly (road ranking); new ratio |
| Well-being loss and socio-economic resilience | Consumption-equivalent loss; asset loss / well-being loss | Currency; ratio in [0, 1] [V] | Discounting; consumption floor | Household surveys | Household model [V/S] | Estimation → Evaluation | New, on nomos `microsimulation` |
| Recovery time | Time to return to a reference level | Days; ratio | "Recovered" | Reopening and rebuilding records | Survival analysis | Estimation | peira `survival`; new composition |
| Early-warning coverage | Share reachable (Scorecard) or covered per 100,000 (G-3) | Share; ratio, cut ordinal [V] | "Reachable"; channel | Telecom and broadcast coverage, sirens, surveys | Coverage modelling | Evaluation | Partly (station coverage); new |
| Evacuation time and its feasibility | Time to a safe site per person; share within the bound | Minutes; ratio | Walking speed; "safe site"; the 5-minute standard [V] | Designated sites; roads; drills | Shortest paths, prisms, crowd simulation | Estimation → Evaluation | Lifelines plug-in (Miyako); urban `movement`; kinesis `prisms` |
| Shelter capacity | Places over people needing shelter | Ratio | Space per person (Sphere 3.5 m² [M]); scenario | Shelter registers | Demand by scenario | Evaluation | New |
| Insurance penetration and protection gap | Insured over total (two readings [V]); uninsured share of losses | Share; ratio | Denominator; mutual aid included | Insurers; MOF [V] | Ratios; loss models | Evaluation | New (readings declared) |
| Contingent finance | Pre-arranged funds over expected need | Currency, ratio | Layer boundaries | Budgets; Cat DDO terms [V] | Risk layering | Evaluation → PolicyMaking | Lifelines plug-in's layers (Freetown) |
| Seismic adequacy rate | Adequate dwellings over all | Share; ratio | "Adequate" (post-1981 or diagnosed [V]) | Housing statistics, MLIT [V] | Ratio by vintage | Evaluation | New, simple |
| Community organisation coverage | Households within activity areas | Share; ratio [V] | "Covered" versus "active" | FDMA [V] | Ratio | Evaluation | New, simple |
| Sendai A–D outcomes | Deaths, affected, losses, disruption | Per 100,000, per GDP; ratio | "Affected", "disaster" [V] | EM-DAT [V], DesInventar [V], national reports | Rates; baselines | Results capture | New (reader); demography for denominators |
| Institutional capacity | Scorecard levels | 0–3 or 0–5; ordinal [V] | Scenario; evidence of each level | Workshop with justifications | Ordinal comparison; IRT | Evaluation | nomos `measurement`, `psychometrics`; ordinal models not built |
| INFORM risk | Geometric mean of three dimensions [V] | 0–10 [M]; treated as ratio | Normalisation | JRC (subnational models [M]) | Composite indicator | Evaluation | nomos composite indicators |

### C.9. What the Infrastructure Holds and What It Lacks

**Already able to compute.** The Lifelines and evacuation plug-in, on the new disaster-risk domains (uncommitted when this was written), computes:

- road links ranked by flood risk and criticality to care;
- coverage by stations under floods;
- evacuation on foot with bounds, and a crowd simulation on Miyako with Japan's designated sites;
- the financing layers for Freetown.

Beneath it sit urban-science `exposure`, tyche `exceedance`, urban `access` and nomos accessibility, urban `movement` with physis's kinematic waves, civil engineering's water criticality, tyche `decision` and `graphical`, kinesis's criticality signs and `prisms`, tekmerion `construal` and `located`, peira's designs, survival, small areas and quasi-experiments, nomos's appraisal, microsimulation and composite indicators, and the policy domain's transfer.

**New modules this pillar needs:**

- vulnerability curves by building type, and their Bayesian updating from damage surveys;
- a well-being loss model on households (after *Unbreakable*), on nomos `microsimulation`;
- recovery trajectories as survival on records;
- readers for EM-DAT and DesInventar, with Sendai's indicators computed and their readings declared;
- scorecard workshops held as records with justifications, and ordinal measurement models for institutional capacity (not built, as ADR 0128 records);
- shelter demand by scenario;
- insurance indexes under their two readings;
- a post-event pipeline (rapid estimate, then needs) and RPBA-shaped records for post-conflict settings, with disclosure control.

# Chapter D. Productive Cities

## Part D. The Productive City: Economies, Municipal Finance, Land Value, Territory, Tourism and Spatial Data

*Status: draft. Activity: research and use. Written 2026-11-19 for the TDLC Urban Analyst toolset on kybernesis.*

**Scope.** This part covers the productive pillar with municipal finance, land value, territorial development, tourism statistics and spatial data infrastructure, organised finally by kybernesis block (§7) with three tables (§8). Tourism is kept to its statistics and economic weight; its promotion, development and management are another part's subject. General economic appraisal (CBA, MCA, the prioritisation of capital investment) is another part's subject and is mentioned here only where the pillar adds to it.

**Markers.** Each bibliographic item carries one marker. **[V]**: verified this session against a primary record (the issuing body's page or repository record, a DOI resolved through Crossref, or the report's own text). **[S]**: confirmed only through secondary sources. **[M]**: from memory, not verified this session. A number is given only where its source was seen; where a figure is recalled it is marked [M] beside it.

### D.1. The Concept of a Productive City

#### D.1.1 Productivity as a Relation between Density, Access and Institutions

A city is productive when the people and firms within it produce more, per worker and per unit of land, than they would elsewhere, and when that surplus is converted into wages, revenue and further investment rather than dissipated in congestion, land scarcity or rents captured by a few. The empirical literature identifies three sources of the urban premium: *sorting* (more productive workers and firms locate in larger cities), *agglomeration* (proximity raises productivity through sharing, matching and learning) and *selection* (competition removes the least productive firms). Only the second is a property of the place that policy can act on, and separating it from the first is the central identification problem of the field (§5.1).

Density alone does not produce the premium. The World Bank's diagnosis of African cities as "crowded, disconnected and costly" (Lall, Henderson and Venables 2017) makes the point: people may be concentrated without capital or effective access, and then density yields crowding, not agglomeration. The operative variable is therefore *effective density*, the number of jobs, firms or consumers reachable within a given travel cost. It joins the productive pillar to transport and land use, which the infrastructure already models in its LUTI and accessibility modules.

#### D.1.2 The Fiscal Loop

The productive pillar is closed by a fiscal loop. Public investment in access raises land values. Part of that rise can be recovered through property taxes, betterment levies, development charges or land sales, and the revenue recovered finances further investment and services. A city's ability to run the loop depends on its own-source revenue, its creditworthiness and its capacity to plan and execute capital investment. The municipal finance diagnostics (§2.4) measure this capacity, and the land value capture literature (§2.5) measures the coupling between access and revenue. Japan's railway-led development (§6.1) is the most cited instance of the loop run by private actors.

#### D.1.3 Territory, Tourism and Spatial Data as Enabling Layers

Three further subjects belong to the pillar because the loop cannot be measured without them. *Territory*: an index per capita or per km² depends on the unit chosen, and the Degree of Urbanisation and the functional urban area give units comparable across countries (§2.6). *Tourism*: a sector whose demand originates outside the city, whose output is not a national-accounts industry but a demand-side aggregate, and whose costs fall on residents (§2.8). *Spatial data infrastructure*: land values, cadastral coverage, travel times and the location of firms are all geospatial evidence, and a city's capacity to produce them is itself an index (§2.7).

#### D.1.4 What Kybernesis Asks of the Pillar

In kybernesis terms the pillar's state is a belief about (i) the spatial distribution of output, employment and firms, (ii) the transport cost matrix and the access it yields, (iii) the land and property value surface, (iv) the municipal balance sheet and cash flows, and (v) the visitor flows and their spending. Evaluation operators map that belief to features (output per worker), needs (an infrastructure gap), problems (a debt service ratio over threshold), risks (dependence on a single tourism market) and assets (latent land value near a planned station). Nearly every index below has open terms whose reading changes the number, and the interpretation block must record which reading was taken and on whose authority.

### D.2. Frameworks and Diagnostics

#### D.2.1 Competitive Cities

*Competitive Cities for Jobs and Growth: What, Who, and How* (World Bank Group 2015) **[V]** (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/23227; CC BY 3.0 IGO) analyses 750 cities and reports that about three-quarters grew faster than their national economies from the early 2000s. It finds that the private sector accounts for roughly 75 percent of job creation. It groups city interventions under four levers: institutions and regulations, infrastructure and land, skills and innovation, and enterprise support and finance. Its argument for practice is that successful cities adapted interventions to their political economy and to the needs of their own firms rather than importing a template. That is the same condition kybernesis states as support factors of transfer. Companion papers cover city analytics, case studies, a user's guide and city strategy **[V]** (listed in the same repository record).

#### D.2.2 Urbanisation Reviews and Spatially Targeted Policy

The World Bank's urbanisation reviews were synthesised in *Planning, Connecting, and Financing Cities—Now: Priorities for City Leaders* (Lall et al., ed., 2013) **[V]** (http://hdl.handle.net/10986/12238). Its three pillars are *planning* (land use and basic services, which it ranks first), *connecting* (links between a city's markets and other places) and *financing* (up-front capital, beginning with the city's creditworthiness). Chapter 4 draws lessons from the country reviews. The framework maps cleanly onto the pillar: planning governs land value, connecting governs market access, and financing governs the fiscal loop.

*Africa's Cities: Opening Doors to the World* (Lall, Henderson and Venables 2017) **[V]** (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/25896) supplies the diagnostic triple *crowded, disconnected, costly*. In a comparison of 125 cities it reports African cities about 31 percent more costly for households than cities elsewhere in low- and middle-income countries. Its prescriptions are formal land markets, clear property rights, effective planning and early, coordinated infrastructure.

*Place, Productivity, and Prosperity: Revisiting Spatially Targeted Policies for Regional Development* (Grover, Lall and Maloney 2022) **[V]** (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/entities/publication/10f32dab-3e71-5d4f-a7d4-47b7c35e0a86; ISBN 978-1-4648-1670-3) is the reference for lagging regions. It argues that urbanisation in developing countries is often not accompanied by structural transformation. It offers a framework for judging place-based proposals by whether the place has the agglomeration, connectivity and human capital for an intervention to work. For the TDLC remit on rural–urban linkages and lagging regions, this book gives the evaluation questions; the Degree of Urbanisation (§2.6) gives the units.

#### D.2.3 The GPSC Urban Sustainability Framework

The Global Platform for Sustainable Cities' *Urban Sustainability Framework* (1st ed., 2018) **[V]** (https://www.thegpsc.org/node/13878; repository handle 10986/29364; indicator lists read from the report's text) has two enabling dimensions, governance and integrated planning, and fiscal sustainability. It has four outcome dimensions: urban economies; natural environment and resources; climate action and resilience; and inclusivity and quality of life.

- **Fiscal sustainability** has five focus areas: accountability and transparency; creditworthiness; revenue and financial autonomy; expenditure management; and management of debt and other obligations. Its indicators include:
  - a credit rating or shadow rating on the *national* scale;
  - own-source revenue as a percentage of total revenue (citing ISO 37120);
  - utility cost recovery;
  - taxes collected as a percentage of taxes billed;
  - the operating margin;
  - three debt service ratios: to operating revenue, to own-source revenue (the ISO 37120 definition) and to the operating margin before debt service;
  - the share of foreign-currency debt;
  - debt growth against the growth of revenue available for debt service;
  - contingent liabilities due within five years.
- **Urban economies** lists:
  - GDP per capita and its growth;
  - GDP density per km² of built-up area;
  - GDP per person employed and its growth;
  - employment change;
  - manufacturing employment share;
  - location quotients of the top three subsectors;
  - a Herfindahl index;
  - R&D intensity and FDI;
  - businesses per 100,000 people aged 16–64;
  - days to obtain business and construction licences;
  - visitors from abroad and domestically.

The USF is the closest existing catalogue to this pillar's indexes. Its weakness for kybernesis is that it names indicators without declaring the readings of their open terms, for example whether "own-source" includes shared taxes, or the unit over which GDP density is computed.

#### D.2.4 Municipal Finance Diagnostics

**The Municipal Finances Self-Assessment (MFSA).** *Municipal Finances: A Handbook for Local Governments* (Farvacque-Vitkovic and Kopanyi, eds., 2014) **[V]** (doi:10.1596/978-0-8213-9830-2; handle 10986/18725) is the World Bank's reference text. Its sequel, *Better Cities, Better World: A Handbook on Local Governments Self-Assessments* (Farvacque-Vitkovic and Kopanyi 2019) **[V]** (doi:10.1596/978-1-4648-1336-8; handle 10986/32120), pairs two tools: the Urban Audit, for investment programming, and the MFSA, for financing. The MFSA proceeds through core databases, historical analysis, ratio analysis, financial projections, a financial management assessment and an action plan. Its appendices include a light version, the calculation methodology, an online application and a self-assessed *shadow credit rating*. The sequence is almost exactly the kybernesis chain:

1. the databases are evidence;
2. the ratios are evaluations;
3. the projections are estimations forward in time;
4. the action plan is policy.

**PEFA at the subnational level.** The Public Expenditure and Financial Accountability programme's *Guidance for Subnational Government PEFA Assessments* (May 2022) **[V]** (https://www.pefa.org/resources/guidance-subnational-government-pefa-assessments) replaced the December 2016 supplementary guidance. It applies the national PEFA framework's indicators, adjusted for subnational governments, after a 2020 consultation and pilot that proposed modules on service delivery and on a full overview of subnational public financial management **[V]**. PEFA scores are ordinal (A to D per dimension, aggregated by declared methods) **[M]**. They are therefore an evaluation space of ordinal scale type, and they must not be averaged as if cardinal.

**The City Creditworthiness Initiative.** Launched within the Low-Carbon, Livable Cities initiative in 2013, its academies began in Nairobi in October 2013 **[V]** (World Bank feature, https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/feature/2013/10/24/financing-sustainable-cities-africa-creditworthy). The World Bank's framing figure, that about 4 percent of the 500 largest cities in developing countries are deemed creditworthy in international markets and 20 percent in local markets, dates from 2013 **[V]** and should be cited with its date. The Rockefeller Foundation granted US$1 million in 2015 as seed funding for a multi-donor trust fund **[V]** (World Bank press release, 18 February 2015).

**The World Observatory on Subnational Government Finance and Investment (SNG-WOFI).** This OECD and UCLG observatory gives country-level structure. Its 2022 synthesis covers 135 country profiles **[V]** (https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/2022-synthesis-report-world-observatory-on-subnational-government-finance-and-investment_b80a8cdb-en.html; https://www.sng-wofi.org). It reports that in 2020 grants and subsidies made up about 51.5 percent of subnational revenue across 116 countries, taxes about 31 percent and tariffs and fees about 10 percent **[V]** (observatory presentations). Its caution that tax figures merge shared and own-source taxes, and so do not measure discretion, is the open term of every fiscal autonomy index below.

#### D.2.5 Land Value Capture

*Financing Transit-Oriented Development with Land Values: Adapting Land Value Capture in Developing Countries* (Suzuki, Murakami, Hong and Tamayose 2015) **[V]** (doi:10.1596/978-1-4648-0149-5; CC BY 3.0 IGO) is the World Bank's guidance on *development-based* land value capture: land sales and leases, air rights, joint development and land readjustment tied to transit. Its cases are Tokyo and Hong Kong's Rail plus Property, with development-rights financing in Nanchang, air-rights sales in São Paulo and examples from New York and Washington. It argues that the conditions for capture exist wherever rising incomes and motorisation raise land values near stations.

The *Global Compendium of Land Value Capture Policies* (OECD and Lincoln Institute of Land Policy 2022) **[V]** (https://www.oecd.org/urban-rural-and-regional-development/global-compendium-of-land-value-capture-policies_4f9559ee-en) profiles 60 countries (all 38 OECD members and 22 others). It classifies instruments into five types: infrastructure levies, developer obligations, charges for development rights, land readjustment and strategic land management. That classification is the natural codebook for the open term "land value captured" (§3.7). It also matches the TDLC Technical Deep Dives' recurring finding that participants know the tools but rarely apply them, for want of capacity, trust and transparency **[V]** (TDLC report on the TOD Deep Dive, 2024; §6).

#### D.2.6 Territorial Units: The Degree of Urbanisation, Functional Urban Areas and Rural–Urban Linkages

*Applying the Degree of Urbanisation: A Methodological Manual to Define Cities, Towns and Rural Areas for International Comparisons* (European Commission, FAO, UN-Habitat, OECD and World Bank 2021) **[V]** (https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/en/web/products-manuals-and-guidelines/-/ks-02-20-499) sets out the method endorsed by the UN Statistical Commission at its 51st session (3–6 March 2020). The manual complements national definitions rather than replacing them; Eurostat notes an update in progress, with an online edition carrying changes to July 2024.

The method classifies 1 km² grid cells **[V]** (GHSL definitions, https://human-settlement.emergency.copernicus.eu/degurbaDefinitions.php):

- an *urban centre* is contiguous cells of at least 1,500 inhabitants per km² with a collective population of at least 50,000;
- an *urban cluster* is contiguous cells of at least 300 per km² with at least 5,000 people;
- *rural cells* are the rest.

Local units are then classified as cities, towns and semi-dense areas, or rural areas. Versions differ in the finer second-level classes, so the version used is itself a reading to declare.

The *functional urban area* (FUA) adds the labour market. Dijkstra, Poelman and Veneri, *The EU-OECD Definition of a Functional Urban Area*, OECD Regional Development Working Papers 2019/11 **[V]** (doi:10.1787/d58cb34d-en), define it as a city plus its commuting zone: contiguous local units with at least 15 percent of their employed residents working in the city. Some countries deviate (Latvia uses 20 percent), and the definition depends on local commuting data and administrative boundaries **[V]**. Where commuting data are absent, as in most TDLC client countries, the commuting zone must be estimated, for instance from travel-time catchments or mobile data, and that estimate is an estimation-block product with its own uncertainty.
