UN-Habitat's *Urban-Rural Linkages: Guiding Principles and Framework for Action to Advance Integrated Territorial Development* (2019) **[V]** (https://unhabitat.org/urban-rural-linkages-guiding-principles) gives ten principles and eleven entry points. It is a policy framework, not a measurement one; its measurable content is the flows (people, goods, food, finance, information) across the urban–rural gradient that the Degree of Urbanisation defines.

#### D.2.7 Land Administration and Spatial Data Infrastructure

The *Integrated Geospatial Information Framework* (UN-GGIM and World Bank) has three parts **[V]** (https://ggim.un.org/documents/Part%201-IGIF-Overarching-Strategic-Framework.pdf):

- Part 1, the overarching strategic framework, endorsed at UN-GGIM's eighth session in August 2018;
- Part 2, an implementation guide with one chapter per strategic pathway, consulted on in 2018–2019 and embraced at the tenth session;
- Part 3, country-level action plans.

Its nine strategic pathways are Governance and Institutions; Policy and Legal; Financial; Data; Innovation; Standards; Partnerships; Capacity and Education; and Communication and Engagement. They are grouped under governance, technology and people **[V]** (UN-GGIM decision-maker brochure, https://ggim.un.org/UN-IGIF/documents/un-igif-Decision-Maker-brochure.pdf). An SDI maturity index for a city or country is a score per pathway, ordinal, against declared statements. It belongs with LGAF-style assessments, not with ratio-scale indicators.

The *Land Governance Assessment Framework* (Deininger, Selod and Burns 2012) **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/land-governance-assessment-framework) assesses five areas: the legal and institutional framework; land use planning, management and taxation; management of public land; public provision of land information; and dispute resolution. Each dimension is rated on a four-grade scale, from very good to very weak practice. Later rounds use 27 indicators and 116 dimensions, so the version must be declared. *Fit-for-Purpose Land Administration* (FIG and World Bank 2014, FIG Publication 60; Enemark, Bell, Lemmen and McLaren) **[V]** (https://www.fig.net/pub/figpub/pub60/figpub60.htm) argues for general boundaries and aerial imagery over field survey where that serves current land issues. It changes what "cadastral coverage" should count (§3.9).

#### D.2.8 Tourism Measurement

The statistical standards are the *International Recommendations for Tourism Statistics 2008* (IRTS 2008) **[M]** and the *Tourism Satellite Account: Recommended Methodological Framework 2008* (TSA:RMF 2008) **[V]** (https://www.untourism.int/standards/on-economic-contribution-of-tourism-tsa-2008). The UN Statistical Commission took note of the TSA:RMF at its 39th session in 2008. It aligns with the SNA 2008 and is organised as ten summary tables, from inbound, domestic and outbound expenditure through tourism supply, employment, gross fixed capital formation and collective consumption, to non-monetary indicators **[V]** (table count; table contents partly [M]). The TSA's headline aggregates are tourism direct gross value added and tourism direct GDP: demand-side measures, because tourism is defined by the visitor, not by the product.

The *Statistical Framework for Measuring the Sustainability of Tourism* (SF-MST) was endorsed by the UN Statistical Commission at its 55th session (27 February to 1 March 2024) **[V]** (https://unwto.org/tourism-statistics/statistical-framework-for-measuring-the-sustainability-of-tourism). It extends the TSA to the environmental and social dimensions by linking it to the SEEA and to social statistics **[M]** for the detail of the linkage, and an implementation programme and compilation guide are to follow **[V]**.

Two further sources are needed for the pillar. UN Tourism's *'Overtourism'? Understanding and Managing Urban Tourism Growth beyond Perceptions* (2018) **[V]** (https://www.unwto.org/global/publication/overtourism-understanding-and-managing-urban-tourism-growth-beyond-perceptions) reports residents' perceptions in eight European cities and proposes 11 strategies and 68 measures, among them dispersal in time and space, regulation, local benefit and monitoring. The World Travel & Tourism Council's estimates place travel and tourism at 9.8 percent of global GDP and 366 million jobs in 2025 **[S]**.

*Caveat.* The "over 10 percent of global GDP" figure in TDLC's remit is the WTTC's *total* contribution, which adds indirect and induced effects modelled from input–output tables. It is not the TSA's tourism direct GDP, which is several times smaller in most countries **[M]** for the ratio. The two must never be compared as if they measured one quantity, and the interpretation block must record which is meant.

### D.3. The Indexes

Each family below gives the definition, the unit and scale type, the direction, the open terms that need declared readings, and the standards or targets found. Scale types follow Stevens: nominal, ordinal, interval, ratio. "Band" means that a value is judged within a target range rather than higher-is-better.

#### D.3.1 Economic Density and Output per Worker

*Gross product per km²* (GPSC's "GDP density per km² of urban built-up area"), *gross product per capita* and *output per worker* (GVA per person employed) are ratio-scale. They are higher-is-better when read as features, though density judged against infrastructure capacity becomes a band. Their open terms:

- the denominator unit (administrative area, DEGURBA urban centre, FUA, or built-up area from GHSL or Overture footprints);
- the output concept (GDP at market prices or GVA at basic prices; nominal or real; local currency or PPP);
- the employment concept (by place of work or of residence; formal only or including informal).

Subnational gross product is rarely published below the province in TDLC client countries. It is estimated by disaggregating regional accounts with night lights, built-up area and employment as covariates (§5.6). An estimate of this kind carries model uncertainty, which kybernesis must propagate into every derived density.

#### D.3.2 Jobs and Firm Dynamics

These indexes are:

- employment and its growth (ratio; higher better within the bounds of the labour supply);
- the employment rate and labour force participation (ratio, bounded proportions);
- firm births, deaths and their rates (ratio);
- survival curves of cohorts of firms (peira's `survival`);
- the share of employment in firms above a size;
- sectoral structure: location quotients (ratio, read against 1), Herfindahl concentration (ratio on [0, 1]; GPSC's bands of 0.15 and 0.25 for moderate and high concentration **[V]**, from the USF text) and shift-share components.

The open terms are "job" (ILO's 19th ICLS definitions of work and employment **[M]**; formal against informal), "firm" (establishment against enterprise; registered against observed), the industry classification and its revision, and whether a firm's location is its registered seat or its place of operation.

#### D.3.3 Wage Premia and Agglomeration Elasticities

The *agglomeration elasticity* is the percentage change in productivity (wages, TFP or output per worker) for a percentage change in density or effective density. It is ratio-scale and dimensionless. It is a parameter of the state, so it belongs to estimation, not evaluation. A meta-analysis of 729 estimates from 34 studies **[M]** for the counts (Melo, Graham and Noland 2009) **[V]** (doi:10.1016/j.regsciurbeco.2008.12.002) finds wide dispersion explained by the measure of productivity, the industry, the country and the treatment of unobserved worker quality. Combes, Duranton and Gobillon (2008) **[V]** (doi:10.1016/j.jue.2007.04.004) show on French matched data that sorting accounts for a large share of spatial wage disparities. Their method of estimating a worker fixed effect first and then regressing area effects on density is the standard. Combes and Gobillon's handbook chapter (2015) **[V]** ("The Empirics of Agglomeration Economies", *Handbook of Regional and Urban Economics* 5, pp. 247–348; doi:10.1016/B978-0-444-59517-1.00005-2) surveys the identification strategies.

For developing countries, Chauvin, Glaeser, Ma and Tobio (2017) **[V]** (doi:10.1016/j.jue.2016.05.003) compare Brazil, China, India and the United States. They find the wage–density relation stronger in China and India than in the United States, and they caution that spatial equilibrium holds less well there **[M]** for the comparative magnitudes.

The open terms are the productivity measure, the density measure (resident, employment or effective), the spatial unit, the sorting control and the instrument. A city's own elasticity is rarely identifiable from its own data. In practice it is *transferred* from a national or meta-analytic estimate, which is precisely a kybernesis transfer with support factors to state: a similar industrial mix, labour mobility and informality share.

#### D.3.4 Market Access, Job Accessibility, Travel Times and Congestion

*Market access* in the Donaldson and Hornbeck (2016) **[V]** (doi:10.1093/qje/qjw002) sense is MA_i = Σ_j τ_ij^(−θ) N_j: the sum of other places' population or income, discounted by trade cost τ raised to the trade elasticity θ. It is ratio-scale and higher-is-better; θ and the cost matrix are its open terms. The older Harris (1954) potential **[M]** uses distance decay without the general-equilibrium derivation.

*Job accessibility* is the number or share of jobs reachable within a travel-time threshold by a mode (cumulative opportunities), or a gravity-weighted sum. The World Bank's first benchmark for eleven African cities (Peralta-Quirós, Kerzhner and Avner 2019, Policy Research Working Paper 8971) **[V]** (doi:10.1596/1813-9450-8971) estimates employment locations as "employment opportunity areas" and maps informal transit, because neither job locations nor transit schedules were available. The open terms are the threshold (30, 45 or 60 minutes), the mode, the time of day, whether waiting and walking are included, and whether jobs are counted at all or matched to workers' skills.

*Travel speed and congestion.* Akbar, Couture, Duranton and Storeygard (2023) **[V]** (*American Economic Review* 113(4): 1083–1111; doi:10.1257/aer.20181662) build city speed indices from 57 million trips simulated through a web mapping service across 180 Indian cities. Each index decomposes exactly into uncongested speed and a congestion factor. They find that differences in uncongested speed, not congestion, drive most variation between cities. This is a decisive caution against reading "congestion" as the single transport problem. The congestion index is a ratio (free-flow time over peak time, or its inverse). Its open terms are the sample of trips, the hour, and the routing service's opaque model.

#### D.3.5 Land and Property Values

Land value per m², property price per m², rent per m², the price-to-income ratio, and the capitalisation of an amenity (the hedonic implicit price) are ratio-scale. Their open terms:

- transaction price against assessed value against asking price;
- land against land with buildings;
- formal against informal transactions;
- the quality adjustment (hedonic or repeat-sales);
- the date and deflator.

Rosen (1974) **[V]** (doi:10.1086/260169) is the theoretical basis of the hedonic method. Ahlfeldt, Redding, Sturm and Wolf (2015) **[V]** (doi:10.3982/ECTA10876) show, from the division and reunification of Berlin, that land prices and employment respond to access in the way a quantitative spatial model with agglomeration predicts. Their model is the structural counterpart of the reduced-form hedonic. For transit uplift, the meta-analysis of Debrezion, Pels and Rietveld (2007) **[V]** (*Journal of Real Estate Finance and Economics* 35: 161–180) reports a larger premium for commercial than for residential property near stations. Commuter rail shows larger effects than light rail or metro, and the measured effect falls once other accessibility variables enter. The rule follows: an uplift estimate without controls for access by road overstates the capture base.

#### D.3.6 Municipal Finance Indexes

The core ratios, in the forms the GPSC USF and ISO 37120 give **[V]** (USF text) and in Japan's statutory forms (§6.3), are:

- **Own-source revenue per capita** (ratio, local currency per person, higher better). The open terms are whether shared taxes count as own-source (SNG-WOFI's caution), whether user charges of utilities count, and population by census or by estimate.
- **Own-source revenue share** (ratio on [0, 1]; GPSC: own-source as a percentage of total revenue including formula transfers and grants). Higher means more autonomy, but a band is often appropriate, since equalisation transfers are a design choice, not a failure.
- **Fiscal autonomy** in the stronger sense: the share of revenue over whose base or rate the city has discretion. It is ordinal or ratio depending on construction. Japan's *fiscal capability index* is the ratio of basic fiscal revenue to basic fiscal demand, averaged over three years, where 1.0 or more means no ordinary local allocation tax **[V]** (MIC and prefectural statistics). It is a statutory and computable version of autonomy relative to a declared standard need.
- **Capital investment per capita** and **capital budget execution** (ratio; executed over budgeted). The open terms are budgeted against executed, and own capital against capital financed by transfers or loans.
- **Operating margin** (operating revenue less operating expenditure, ratio to revenue; positive required by GPSC).
- **Debt service ratios** (ratio, lower better, judged against thresholds): debt service over own-source revenue (ISO 37120), over operating revenue, or over the operating margin before debt service. The open terms are which liabilities count (leases, utilities, state enterprises, PPP commitments, arrears) and which revenue. Japan's *real debt service ratio* has statutory thresholds (§6.3).
- **Credit rating** (ordinal; GPSC asks for a national-scale rating or a shadow rating). The open terms are international against national scale, the agency, and whether a shadow rating by the MFSA method counts.
- **Cost recovery** of services (ratio; tariff revenue over operating cost, or over full cost with depreciation and capital). The open term, operating cost only or full cost, can move the value by a factor of two **[M]**.
- **Collection efficiency** (taxes collected over taxes billed) and **coverage** of the property tax roll (parcels or value on the roll over parcels or value existing).

#### D.3.7 Land Value Captured

*Land value captured* is the revenue recovered through capture instruments over a period, absolute or as a share of the estimated value uplift caused by a public action. It is ratio-scale. As a share it is bounded only by definition (shares above one indicate that the instrument also taxes pre-existing value). The open terms are the heaviest in the pillar:

- the codebook of instruments counted (the OECD and Lincoln five types, with or without the recurrent property tax);
- the counterfactual for uplift (hedonic difference-in-differences, a valuation roll against a control area, or a developer's pro forma);
- the attribution of uplift to the action rather than to the market;
- the period, and the discounting of capture that arrives over decades.

No international standard exists for this index **[M]**; the compendium describes instruments, not rates of capture.

#### D.3.8 Tourism Indexes

The indexes are:

- arrivals and overnight stays (ratio), domestic and inbound;
- visitor expenditure (ratio, TSA Tables 1–4);
- tourism direct GVA and its share of GVA (ratio, TSA Table 6) **[M]** for table numbers;
- tourism employment (TSA Table 7) **[M]**;
- occupancy rates of rooms and beds (ratio on [0, 1]);
- *tourism intensity*, overnight stays per resident, and *tourism density*, overnight stays or visitors per km² (ratio; judged as a band, the band being a declared social or physical limit);
- *seasonality*, the ratio of the peak month to the annual mean, or a Gini coefficient over the twelve months (ratio; lower usually better, though Japan's TDLC blog notes that a low season gives destinations time to recover **[V]**);
- market concentration of origins, a Herfindahl index over source markets (ratio; a risk);
- *carrying capacity* (physical, ecological or social, the last measured by resident perception surveys, which are ordinal).

The open terms are "visitor" against "tourist" against "same-day visitor" and the "usual environment" (IRTS **[M]**), which accommodation is counted (registered only, or short-term rentals too), and which carrying capacity is meant.

#### D.3.9 Spatial Data Infrastructure Maturity and Cadastral Coverage

*SDI maturity* is a score per IGIF pathway (ordinal; higher better). Its open terms are the statements that define each level and who assesses. *Cadastral coverage* is the share of parcels, or of area, surveyed and recorded with a defined boundary (ratio on [0, 1]). Japan reports it as the *progress rate of the cadastral survey*: 53 percent of the area subject to survey nationally and 81 percent of priority areas at the end of fiscal 2024. In densely inhabited districts (DID) the rate is 27 percent, and for residential land 52 percent **[V]** (MLIT press release; Saitama Prefecture). Japan is therefore itself an example of low urban cadastral coverage beside a highly functional land market, which matters for the reading of the index (§6.6). The open terms are parcels against area, surveyed against registered against both, the accuracy class (fit-for-purpose general boundaries or fixed surveyed boundaries) and currency, meaning whether transfers since the survey are recorded. SDG indicator 1.4.2, the share of adults with secure tenure rights **[M]**, is the person-based counterpart.

### D.4. The Evidence to Collect

| Evidence | Supports | Licence and access | Biases and cautions |
|---|---|---|---|
| Firm censuses and business registers (e.g. Japan's Economic Census **[M]**; Melbourne's CLUE, already read) | employment by place of work, firm births and deaths, sector, size | national statistical terms; often confidential microdata, published aggregates | registers miss informal firms; censuses are infrequent; registered seat against operating site |
| Labour force surveys | employment rates, wages, informality, sector by residence | microdata under licence; aggregates open | sample too small below region; residence-based, not workplace-based |
| National and regional accounts | GVA by sector and region, deflators | open (e.g. WDI, CC BY 4.0 **[M]**) | rarely below province; city figures modelled |
| Municipal budgets and financial statements; BOOST | revenue by source, expenditure by function and economic class, executed against budgeted | BOOST line-item data published where the country agrees **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/boost-portal/about-the-portal; subnational views where available) | cash against accrual bases; off-budget utilities and enterprises; transfers recorded inconsistently |
| PEFA reports, MFSA workbooks, credit rating reports | ordinal assessments, ratios, ratings | PEFA reports often public; ratings proprietary | assessments dated; shadow ratings self-assessed |
| Property transactions and valuation rolls (e.g. MLIT's Real Estate Information Library) | prices, land values, hedonic estimation, the capture base | Reinfolib offers transaction prices, published land prices, urban planning information and an API **[V]** (https://www.reinfolib.mlit.go.jp/) | informal markets unrecorded; declared prices under-reported where taxed; rolls stale |
| Night lights (Black Marble, already read) | proxy for output and its growth, electrification | NASA open data **[M]** | saturation in cores; blooming; weak in low-light and agricultural economies (Henderson, Storeygard and Weil 2012) |
| Mobile phone and card data | commuting zones, visitor flows, spending by origin, nowcasts | commercial agreements; privacy constraints | market share by operator or card scheme; foreign visitors' roaming; opaque processing |
| GTFS and travel-time services | job accessibility, speed indices | feeds licensed by agency; routing services under terms of use | informal transit absent from GTFS; routing models opaque (Akbar et al. 2023) |
| OpenStreetMap and Overture (already read) | road networks, buildings, places of business as firm proxies | ODbL for OSM; Overture by theme **[M]** | uneven completeness; places of business biased to the formal and the visible |
| Tourism statistics (accommodation surveys, border arrivals, TSA) | arrivals, nights, occupancy, origin, direct GVA | official; Japan's Overnight Travel Statistics Survey is monthly by prefecture, with foreign guest nights by nationality **[V]** (Statistics Bureau FAQ; Japan Tourism Agency releases) | excludes unregistered rentals; same-day visitors missing; stratification changed in January 2026 in Japan's survey **[V]** |
| Cadastral and land registry extents; LGAF reports | cadastral coverage, land governance scores | national; LGAF reports often public | area against parcel counts; currency of records |
| 3D city models (Japan's PLATEAU) | floor area by use, building age, the base for value and capacity | open data for about 250 cities through the G-Spatial Information Center, CityGML **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/plateau/en/); licence per dataset (CC BY 4.0 per secondary sources **[S]**) | coverage of cities uneven; attributes vary by year of survey |

### D.5. Methods

#### D.5.1 Agglomeration Estimation

The standard two-step estimator, after Combes, Duranton and Gobillon (2008), is:

1. regress log wages on worker characteristics, a worker fixed effect and area–year effects;
2. regress the area effects on log density, instrumented by deep lags of population or by geology.

Without matched employer–employee panels, which most TDLC clients lack, only the second step on area averages is available, and the estimate then includes sorting. It should be labelled as an upper bound **[M]** for the direction of bias, which the literature generally finds upward. The infrastructure's `tyche::panel` absorbs several fixed effects. With matched data it could run the first step; a dedicated two-way fixed-effects estimator for worker and firm (Abowd, Kramarz and Margolis 1999 **[M]**) is not yet written. Gibbons, Lyytikäinen, Overman and Sanchis-Guarner (2019) **[V]** (*Journal of Urban Economics* 110: 35–50; doi:10.1016/j.jue.2019.01.002) give the transport-appraisal version: changes in accessibility from new roads, measured from minimum journey times, applied to firm-level outcomes.

#### D.5.2 Shift-Share

The shift-share decomposition splits a city's employment change into a national component, an industry-mix component and a competitive (local) component. As an instrument (Bartik 1991 **[M]**), the predicted local change from national industry growth weighted by initial shares identifies local labour demand shocks. Goldsmith-Pinkham, Sorkin and Swift (2020) **[V]** (*American Economic Review* 110(8): 2586–2624) show that identification then rests on the exogeneity of the initial shares. Borusyak, Hull and Jaravel (2022) **[V]** (*Review of Economic Studies* 89(1): 181–213) show it can rest instead on many exogenous shocks, with exposure-robust standard errors. A plug-in should offer both the descriptive decomposition (nomos `measurement` holds specialisation measures, and a decomposition is arithmetic) and the instrument with the Borusyak, Hull and Jaravel inference, which the toolbox does not yet hold.

#### D.5.3 Market Access and Accessibility Models

Market access needs a cost matrix and θ. The cost matrix comes from shortest paths on the road network (morphe's Dijkstra, already used by urban science's `access`), from GTFS schedules, or from routing services. θ is transferred from the trade literature (economics' `trade` estimates gravity by PPML on nomos). Job accessibility uses nomos's cumulative-opportunity and gravity measures and the two-step floating catchment, all already written. The missing piece in TDLC contexts is job locations. They can be estimated by dasymetric allocation from Overture places and building floor space, with tyche's change of support carrying control totals down from regional employment.

#### D.5.4 Municipal Finance Diagnostics and Projections

The MFSA's ratio analysis is arithmetic on a declared chart of accounts. Its value lies in the codebook: mapping each country's budget classification (or a BOOST extract) onto a common one, with each mapping recorded as a reading. archeion's `datasets` (codebooks after DDI, harmonisation onto a finest common target) is the natural home for that mapping.

Projections are a cash-flow model: revenue by source driven by its base (population, property values, economic activity), expenditure by function, the capital programme and debt service. Debt capacity is the largest new borrowing that keeps the debt service ratio under a declared threshold over the horizon. Projections under uncertainty are tyche `uncertainty` (Latin hypercubes, Sobol' indices), and capital programming under a budget is a knapsack (calx). The shadow credit rating is an ordinal scorecard, a composite indicator with declared weights (nomos), and must be reported as ordinal.

#### D.5.5 Land Value Capture Appraisal and Hedonic Valuation

The appraisal has four steps:

1. estimate the uplift surface by hedonic regression with spatial dependence (urban science `demand`, tyche `spatial`), or by difference-in-differences around stations opened at different dates (peira `quasi`, staggered adoption);
2. multiply by the stock of land and floor space within the influence area (Overture buildings, PLATEAU, or the zoning envelope from architecture's `rules`, which states what could be built);
3. apply each instrument's capture rate under its legal design;
4. discount the stream (nomos `appraisal`, with declining rates).

The structural alternative, a quantitative spatial model of the Ahlfeldt et al. kind, is close to the existing LUTI module, whose land market already capitalises access. Debrezion et al.'s finding that omitted road access inflates uplift is a check the plug-in should run.

#### D.5.6 Tourism Satellite Accounts, Input–Output Impact and Nowcasting from Proxies

A city-level TSA combines visitor expenditure by product (from surveys) with a supply–use table to compute tourism ratios and tourism direct GVA. Input–output multipliers then give indirect and induced effects. economics' `production` (Leontief) and industrial ecology's `eeio` (multipliers, regional accounts) already hold the algebra. What is missing is the TSA's tables and tourism ratios as a module, and the discipline of reporting direct and total effects apart (§2.8).

Nowcasting from proxies uses night lights for output: Henderson, Storeygard and Weil (2012) **[V]** (*American Economic Review* 102(2): 994–1028) estimate an elasticity of measured GDP to lights and combine the two for a better estimate where accounts are poor. It also uses imagery with machine learning for poverty and wealth (Jean et al. 2016 **[V]**, *Science* 353: 790–794) and mobile phone metadata (Blumenstock, Cadamuro and On 2015 **[V]**, *Science* 350: 1073–1076). Each is an estimation-block model whose transfer from training to target place has support factors (similar economic structure, similar phone penetration). Kybernesis must declare these factors and tyche's area-of-applicability check must test them.

### D.6. Japanese Practice and the Support Factors of Its Transfer

#### D.6.1 Railway-Led Development and Land Value Capture

The model is the private railway that is also a developer. Tokyu's Tama Den-en-toshi is its largest instance. By Tokyu's own presentations it is a corridor of about 5,000 ha with roughly 620,000 residents (March 2017). The line opened in stages from 1966 to 1984, and the land readjustment ended in 2006. It was carried out through some 58 readjustment associations of landowners, in which the railway acquired land for its own development **[S]** (developer presentations found through GPSC and conference archives; the figures are the developer's). The World Bank's *Financing Transit-Oriented Development with Land Values* **[V]** treats Tokyo's railway development as a principal case of development-based capture.

The support factors a transfer would need:

- a railway operator permitted to hold and develop land;
- greenfield or low-density land at the urban edge;
- an institution for pooling land (§6.2);
- a growing population and rising incomes;
- long-term finance;
- a planning system that grants density near stations.

Tokyu's own material attributes part of the success to the high-growth period and the expansion of housing loans **[S]**. A plug-in should therefore present the case as conditional, not as a template.

In built-up areas the Japanese instruments are urban redevelopment, under which floor area is exchanged for land rights, and the special districts for urban renaissance, which grant floor area ratio exceptions and tax relief. The TDLC report on its 2024 TOD Deep Dive names the latter as the national instrument supporting value capture **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/tokyo-development-learning-center/tdd/event_implementation_report_technical_deep_dive_on_transit-oriented_development; held in Osaka, 29 January to 2 February 2024, with Umekita, Namba and Senri-Chuo as cases). The 2026 municipal finance Deep Dive's site visit to Shinagawa Season Terrace showed air rights and PPP used to finance infrastructure **[V]** (§6.3).

#### D.6.2 Land Readjustment (Kukaku Seiri)

Land readjustment pools scattered plots, contributes a share of each to roads, parks and a reserve plot sold to finance works, and returns re-plotted parcels of higher value. *Land Readjustment in Japan: Case Study* (Matsui 2019, World Bank East Asia and Pacific) **[V]** (https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/481571569562840686/land-readjustment-in-japan-case-study) traces more than a century of practice. TDLC's October 2025 Deep Dive on *Urban Planning and Land for Growth* **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/tokyo-development-learning-center/tdd/tdd_on_urban_planning_and_land_for_growth; Tokyo, Kyoto and Osaka, 27–31 October 2025) included:

- about 65 participants from nine countries (Bangladesh, Indonesia, Nigeria, Pakistan, Senegal, Tanzania, Thailand, Uzbekistan and Vietnam);
- site visits to the Fushimi West project in Kyoto and the Umekita project in Osaka;
- country action plans to digitise land records, review planning law and pilot readjustment.

TDLC also presents readjustment as central to Hiroshima's reconstruction **[V]** (TDLC feature on Hiroshima's recovery).

The support factors: secure and recorded title to the land pooled; landowners' trust in the readjusting body and a rule for consent (a supermajority in Japan **[M]**); valuation of plots before and after that owners accept; a market that will buy the reserve plots; and courts that settle disputes. JICA and the World Bank have promoted the method abroad since the 1970s with mixed success, Thailand's experience being cited as difficult **[S]**. The low urban cadastral coverage in Japan itself (§3.9) shows that the method needs agreed boundaries within the scheme, not a complete national cadastre.

#### D.6.3 The Local Allocation Tax and Municipal Fiscal Discipline

The *local allocation tax* equalises fiscal capacity. Its pool is fixed shares of national taxes; the shares change by statute, and are quoted in municipal materials as 33.1 percent of income and corporation taxes, 50 percent of the liquor tax and 19.5 percent of the consumption tax, plus all of the local corporation tax. Of the pool, 94 percent is ordinary allocation and 6 percent special. Each authority's ordinary allocation is its basic fiscal demand less its basic fiscal revenue, where revenue counts 75 percent of standard local tax revenue. The remaining 25 percent stays with the authority as an incentive to raise its own base **[V]** (MIC and prefectural materials, e.g. https://www.soumu.go.jp/main_content/000958552.pdf). The fiscal capability index (§3.6) follows from the same quantities.

The *Act on Assurance of Sound Financial Status of Local Governments* sets statutory ratios with thresholds **[V]** (MIC White Paper on Local Public Finance and prefectural materials):

- the real debt service ratio: early-warning standard 25 percent, rehabilitation standard 35 percent;
- the future burden ratio: early-warning standard 350 percent for municipalities, 400 percent for prefectures and designated cities;
- borrowing: since fiscal 2006 local bonds have been issued by consultation rather than permission. An authority whose real debt service ratio, averaged over three years, is 18 percent or more still needs permission to borrow. Below 18 percent a sound authority may merely notify **[V]** (https://www.soumu.go.jp/menu_seisaku/hakusyo/chihou/18data/18czb3-2.html).

The ladder of notification at under 18 percent, permission at 18 or more, a mandatory soundness plan at 25 or more and rehabilitation at 35 or more is the clearest example available of an evaluation space with declared thresholds held by an authority. It maps directly onto kybernesis's judgement against standards.

The municipal revenue base is the fixed asset tax, at a standard rate of 1.4 percent of assessed value, with a city planning tax of at most 0.3 percent in urbanisation promotion areas **[V]** (MLIT, https://www.mlit.go.jp/totikensangyo/totikensangyo_tk5_000073.html). The city planning tax is a broad-based, earmarked levy on land that benefits from planning, closer to value capture than its name suggests.

TDLC's March 2026 Deep Dive on municipal finance **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/tokyo-development-learning-center/tdd/strengthening_municipal_finance_advancing_sustainable_urban_development_through_a_technical_deep_dive; Tokyo, 9–13 March 2026, with IFC; 68 participants from ten countries) presented:

- the national framework of assigned functions and rules-based transfers;
- Toyama's compact city strategy, which joins land use, transit and capital planning to reduce long-term costs and broaden the tax base;
- Fukuoka's debt management;
- a design–build–operate waste plant;
- Shinagawa's air rights.

The support factors for transfer of the Japanese fiscal model: a stable national tax base large enough to fund equalisation; a formula for standard need that is accepted as fair; accurate local accounts; and a central authority able to enforce thresholds. Without these, the ratios can be computed but not enforced.

#### D.6.4 Kitakyushu's Industrial Transformation

Kitakyushu was selected by the OECD in 2011 as the first "Green Growth City" in Asia **[V]** (TDLC partnership page, https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/tokyo-development-learning-center/partnership/kitakyushu). It is studied in *Green Growth in Kitakyushu, Japan* (OECD 2013) **[V]** (doi:10.1787/9789264195134-en), which covers land use, transport, buildings, waste, energy, water, industry, innovation assets and institutions. TDLC shares its Eco-Town and the "Kitakyushu Model" of waste management and circular economy **[V]**. The productive lesson is the conversion of a polluting heavy-industry base into environmental industries, which the plug-in should read through shift-share (§5.2) and location quotients rather than by narrative. The decline of steel employment and the rise of environmental services are measurable components **[M]** for the trajectory's magnitudes.

#### D.6.5 Kyoto's Accommodation Tax as a Fiscal Instrument

This section keeps to tourism's fiscal and statistical side; the promotion, development and management of destinations are another part's subject. Kyoto has been a TDLC partner city since 2018 **[V]** (TDLC partnership page). It is the subject of *Cultural Heritage, Sustainable Tourism and Urban Regeneration: Capturing Lessons and Experience from Japan with a Focus on Kyoto* (World Bank 2018) **[V]** (http://hdl.handle.net/10986/30204). Its accommodation tax was revised from 1 March 2026 into five tiers by room charge per person per night: ¥200 under ¥6,000; ¥400 from ¥6,000; ¥1,000 from ¥20,000; ¥4,000 from ¥50,000; and ¥10,000 from ¥100,000. The city expects about ¥12.6 billion a year after the revision **[V]** (Kyoto City, https://www.city.kyoto.lg.jp/gyozai/page/0000236942.html).

The tax is an own-source revenue instrument whose base is the visitor rather than the resident. It is progressive in price and earmarked for heritage and tourism, and a TDLC client could compare it with flat per-night levies. For this pillar its interest is threefold:

- it converts the tourism flow measured by the accommodation survey (§3.8) into municipal revenue (§3.6);
- its yield is an estimate that depends on the price distribution of rooms;
- its effect on demand is an elasticity that the revision will allow to be measured.

TDLC's December 2025 Deep Dive on *Cities for Tourism and Jobs* **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/tokyo-development-learning-center/tdd/cities_for_tourism_and_jobs_tdd1; Kyoto, Sapporo, Tokyo and Otaru, 1–5 December 2025, nine countries) framed tourism as a matter of jobs. The support factors for the fiscal instrument are a legal power to levy and to earmark, a register of accommodation that includes short-term rentals, and a visitor flow large enough that the yield justifies administration.

#### D.6.6 Japan's Spatial Information

The *Basic Act on the Advancement of Utilizing Geospatial Information* (Act No. 63 of 2007) **[V]** (https://www.japaneselawtranslation.go.jp/ja/laws/view/4053/en) defines fundamental geospatial data as the positional reference for all other geospatial information and requires a basic plan. The Geospatial Information Authority of Japan (GSI) integrates large-scale map data from many bodies into that data and provides it free of charge **[V]** (UN-GGIM and GSI papers). PLATEAU (MLIT) provides CityGML 3D city models with building use, construction year and planning attributes for about 250 cities **[V]**. The Real Estate Information Library joins transaction prices, published land prices, hazard and planning information with an API **[V]**. RESAS (Regional Economy Society Analyzing System), provided by METI and the Cabinet Secretariat since 2015, visualises regional economic data for local governments' revitalisation strategies **[V]** (https://resas-portal.go.jp).

Read against the IGIF pathways, Japan scores highly on Data, Standards and Policy and Legal. Its cadastral coverage in dense districts is low (27 percent, §3.9). The SDI maturity index and the cadastral index must therefore be kept apart: a country can have mature spatial information and an incomplete cadastre.

The support factors for transfer of PLATEAU-like models: aerial survey and building data already held by municipalities; a national data model (CityGML profiles); and a use case that justifies the cost, such as disaster risk, which PLATEAU's own uses emphasise **[M]**.

### D.7. The Pillar by Kybernesis Block

This section restates the pillar block by block, so that each block of kybernesis finds what it needs: collection, evidence, interpretation, estimation, evaluation, policy making, actions and inquiries, and results capture. It refers back to §2–§6 rather than repeating them. Economic appraisal (CBA, MCA, the prioritisation of capital investment) and the promotion and management of tourism are other parts' subjects; they appear here only where the productive pillar adds something specific.

#### D.7.1 Collection

**The minimum dataset.** A productive-city diagnostic for a TDLC client city needs seven groups of data, listed in the order in which each conditions the next.

1. *The territorial base*: a population grid (GHS-POP, already read), administrative boundaries, the Degree of Urbanisation classes computed from the grid (§2.6), and a commuting zone, estimated where commuting data do not exist.
2. *The economy*: regional accounts, the labour force survey, the business register or economic census, night lights (Black Marble, already read), and places of business (Overture, already read).
3. *Access*: the road network (OSM), transit schedules (GTFS) or a mapping of informal transit, and speeds from a routing service.
4. *Land*: transactions, the valuation roll, the extent of the cadastre, and the zoning in force.
5. *Municipal finance*: at least five years of budgets and executed accounts. The MFSA's first step is a core database and a historical analysis **[V]** (§2.4), and five years is the customary span **[M]**. Also the debt register, the transfer formula, and any PEFA or rating reports.
6. *Tourism statistics*: the accommodation survey, arrivals, and a TSA where one exists.
7. *Spatial data infrastructure*: an IGIF self-assessment and an LGAF report, where available.

**Modes of collection.**

- Administrative extraction under a data-sharing agreement: budgets, rolls, registers.
- Sample surveys: enterprise surveys, such as the World Bank Enterprise Surveys **[M]**; household and labour force surveys; visitor expenditure surveys; resident perception surveys.
- Remote sensing: lights, built-up area, building footprints.
- Crowd and open mapping: OSM.
- Purchase or partnership: mobile phone and card data.

**Scoping as a decision.** TDLC's *needs identification* phase is the collection block's scoping. Which data to collect first is a decision of the value of information. The infrastructure already holds the method: tyche chooses the tests worth running within a budget, on calx's knapsack (ADR 0123). The plug-in should use it to rank collection tasks by how much each would change a decision. A city whose question is whether to borrow for a station needs its debt register and transaction prices before its firm census.

**Rights at collection.** The licence and privacy terms are settled at collection, not afterwards. Mobile data and microdata from registers come with use restrictions that must travel with every trace (§7.2).

#### D.7.2 Evidence

Each trace enters with five attributes: provenance, licence, resolution, custody and known biases. §4 lists the sources; this section states what the evidence block must record about them.

**Provenance.** The evidence block distinguishes:

- an *official record* (a budget, a roll, a census);
- an *official estimate* (regional GVA, a population projection);
- a *modelled product* (a population grid, Space2Stats's regional cells, a GDP downscaling);
- an *observation by a third party* (OSM, Overture, a routing service's speed);
- a *survey* (labour force, enterprise, visitor).

A modelled product is evidence of a model applied to the city, not direct evidence of the city. If it is then used to estimate the same quantity, the model's error is counted twice unless the dependence is declared. tekmerion counts reports that share an origin once (ADR 0123), which is the right rule here.

**Custody and audit.** Municipal accounts come audited or unaudited. GPSC's indicator "percentage of municipal government accounts audited" **[V]** is in effect a custody grade. archeion holds digests and custody chains, so an executed budget received as a spreadsheet can be kept with its digest and the authority that released it.

**Licence.** OSM is ODbL **[M]**. WDI and most World Bank data are CC BY 4.0 **[M]**. Overture's themes carry their own licences **[M]**. BOOST data are published where the country agrees **[V]**. Japan's Real Estate Information Library requires attribution and disclaims accuracy **[S]**. Mobile and card data are under contract.

**Resolution.**

- *Spatial*: grids at 1 km (GHS-POP, the Degree of Urbanisation); lights at about 500 m **[M]**; parcels for rolls and transactions; administrative units for budgets.
- *Temporal*: monthly for tourism; annual for budgets and accounts; five- or ten-yearly for censuses.

A question asked at a resolution finer than its evidence must be answered by estimation (§7.4), and the plug-in should say so.

**Biases.**

- Informality is missing from registers and rolls.
- Declared transaction prices are understated where transfers are taxed **[M]**.
- Registers suffer survivorship (dead firms not removed).
- Lights saturate in cores and underweight agriculture (Henderson, Storeygard and Weil 2012 **[V]**).
- OSM and Overture are more complete in rich and touristed places **[M]**.
- GTFS omits informal transit (Peralta-Quirós et al. 2019 **[V]**).
- Accommodation surveys omit unregistered rentals and same-day visitors.
- Routing services' models are opaque (Akbar et al. 2023 **[V]**).

#### D.7.3 Interpretation

Each open term below is a point of interpretation, read under every admissible reading on tekmerion's `construal`. Each reading carries its source, graded as authority (a statute or standard), convention, analyst or data.

| Open term | Admissible readings | Sources |
|---|---|---|
| The city (unit) | administrative boundary; DEGURBA urban centre; FUA; built-up extent | national law; UN Statistical Commission 2020 **[V]**; EU–OECD 2019 **[V]**; GHSL |
| Output | GDP at market prices or GVA at basic prices; nominal or real; local currency or PPP | SNA 2008 **[M]**; ICP **[M]** |
| Employment, job | work for pay or profit; formal or including informal; by place of work or of residence | 19th ICLS (2013) **[M]**; 17th ICLS guidelines on informal employment (2003) **[M]** |
| Firm | enterprise or establishment; registered or observed | ISIC Rev. 4 statistical units **[M]** |
| Own-source revenue | local taxes, fees and charges (ISO 37120 as quoted by GPSC); with or without shared taxes; with or without utility charges | GPSC USF **[V]**; SNG-WOFI's caution **[V]**; IMF GFSM 2014 **[M]**; OECD tax autonomy taxonomy **[M]** |
| Debt and obligations | direct debt; plus leases, utilities and enterprises; plus PPP commitments and contingent liabilities | GPSC USF (contingent liabilities due within five years) **[V]**; Japan's future burden ratio **[V]** for its existence, coverage **[M]** |
| Cost recovery | operating cost only; full cost with depreciation | GPSC (utility cost recovery) **[V]**, basis unstated |
| Creditworthy | international-scale rating; national-scale rating; shadow rating | GPSC (national scale) **[V]**; MFSA shadow rating **[V]** |
| Land value captured | instruments counted among the five OECD–Lincoln types; with or without the recurrent property tax | OECD and Lincoln 2022 **[V]** |
| Value uplift | hedonic difference-in-differences; roll against a control area; pro forma | analyst; Debrezion et al. 2007 on omitted access **[V]** |
| Visitor, tourist, same-day visitor; usual environment | IRTS 2008 definitions | UN 2008 **[M]** |
| Tourism's contribution | TSA direct GVA or GDP; total including indirect and induced effects | TSA:RMF 2008 **[V]**; WTTC **[S]** |
| Cadastral coverage | parcels or area; surveyed or registered; general or fixed boundaries | FIG and World Bank 2014 **[V]**; Japan's progress rate **[V]** |
| Lagging region | income per capita below a share of the national mean; low growth; both | Grover, Lall and Maloney 2022 **[V]** for the framework; thresholds **[M]** |
| Carrying capacity | physical; ecological; social (perceived) | UN Tourism 2018 **[V]** |

**Readings change rankings.** A city's own-source revenue share can change its rank among peers when shared taxes are moved from "own" to "transfer". By kybernesis's rule, a conclusion that holds under some readings and fails under others is *arguable*, and the plug-in should show the pivot. SNG-WOFI's caution that its tax figures merge shared and own-source taxes **[V]** is an authority's own statement that a reading is open.

#### D.7.4 Estimation

The state of the productive city has the variables below. For each, the table states whether it is observed or better estimated by a model or proxy, by what method, and with what uncertainty.

| State variable | Observed directly? | Better estimated by | Uncertainty and its source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Population by grid cell | census at areal units; grids are modelled | dasymetric grids (GHS-POP), change of support with control totals (tyche) | allocation error within units; validated by blocked cross-validation (tyche, ADR 0128) |
| Output by place | rarely below province | downscaling of regional accounts with lights, built-up area and employment (Henderson et al. 2012 **[V]**); imagery models (Jean et al. 2016 **[V]**) | model error; lights' saturation; transfer outside the area of applicability (tyche, ADR 0128) |
| Employment by place of work | only where an economic census exists | dasymetric allocation from Overture places and floor area; small-area estimation from the LFS (peira's Fay–Herriot) | sampling error (EBLUP's MSE); proxy completeness |
| Informal employment share | survey only | small-area estimation | sampling and model error |
| Number of firms | registers biased both ways | capture–recapture across two lists, e.g. a register and Overture places, on peira's closed capture–recapture (a proposal here, not a published method for firms **[M]**) | dependence between lists; heterogeneous detectability |
| Commuting flows and the FUA | rarely, outside OECD countries | Wilson's models calibrated to totals (nomos), mobile phone origin–destination | calibration of the decay; operator share |
| Cost matrix | partly (routing services, GTFS) | network models with mapped informal transit | hour and mode; opacity of routing services |
| Market and job access | computed, never observed | from the cost matrix and the population or job surfaces | inherits both |
| Land value surface | at sparse transactions | hedonic regression with spatial dependence; mass appraisal | thin markets; declared prices understated |
| Agglomeration elasticity | not identifiable from one city's data | transfer from a meta-analytic prior (Melo et al. 2009 **[V]**), updated by Bayes where local data exist (tyche `bayes`) | wide prior; sorting bias of area-level estimates |
| Property tax potential and gap | potential is never observed | building stock × value surface × statutory rate, less collection | compounding of the stock and value errors |
| Municipal cash flows ahead | no (projection) | the cash-flow model of §5.4 under declared scenarios; tyche `uncertainty` | scenario and parameter uncertainty; Sobol' indices name the drivers |
| Debt capacity | no | the largest borrowing keeping the declared ratio under threshold over the horizon | inherits the projection |
| Visitors, including unregistered stays and same-day visitors | registered nights only | mobile data; card spending; listings of short-term rentals **[M]** | operator and scheme shares; foreign roaming |
| Tourism direct GVA where no TSA | no | visitor spending survey × tourism ratios; input–output (economics `production`, industrial ecology `eeio`) | survey error; tourism ratios borrowed from elsewhere |

Two rules follow. First, each estimate enters evaluation with its interval, and evaluation reports the standing of a judgement under that interval, not only at the point estimate. Second, every estimate that was trained elsewhere is a transfer, with support factors to be checked before the estimate is used (§7.6).

#### D.7.5 Evaluation

The operators are ratios, shares, densities, elasticities, concentrations, access measures and gaps against standards (§3). Each evaluation space has a scale type; ordinal spaces (PEFA, ratings, IGIF and LGAF levels, resident perceptions) are never averaged as if cardinal.

Evaluation sorts its outputs into five kinds:

- **features**: output per worker, market access;
- **needs**: the gap between capital investment and the declared standard, and the property tax gap;
- **problems**: a debt service ratio over its threshold, low capital budget execution, low collection efficiency;
- **risks**: revenue concentrated in one source, a tourism market concentrated in few origins, contingent liabilities, foreign-currency debt (GPSC **[V]**);
- **assets**: latent land value near a planned station, underused public land (the LGAF's "management of public land" **[V]**), heritage that already draws visitors.

Standards and who holds them:

| Standard | Held by | Scale | Marker |
|---|---|---|---|
| Real debt service ratio: notification below 18%, permission at 18% or more, soundness plan at 25% or more, rehabilitation at 35% or more | MIC under the Act on Assurance of Sound Financial Status of Local Governments | ratio, thresholds | **[V]** |
| Future burden ratio: 350% (municipalities), 400% (prefectures, designated cities) | MIC, same Act | ratio, thresholds | **[V]** |
| Fiscal capability index of 1.0 | MIC, local allocation tax rules | ratio, threshold | **[V]** |
| Herfindahl bands 0.15 and 0.25; positive operating margin; national-scale investment-grade rating | GPSC USF (World Bank) | ratio and ordinal | **[V]** |
| PEFA scores A–D | PEFA Secretariat | ordinal | **[M]** for scoring rules |
| LGAF grades A–D (very good to very weak practice) | World Bank | ordinal | **[V]** |
| IGIF pathways and actions | UN-GGIM | ordinal (levels declared by the assessor) | **[V]** for the pathways |
| Degree of Urbanisation thresholds | UN Statistical Commission | nominal classes from ratio thresholds | **[V]** |
| ISO 37120 indicator definitions | ISO | definitions, no targets | **[M]** |
| Tourism carrying limits | the city itself; no international standard | ratio, band | **[M]** |

A judgement is the triple (operator value with interval, standard, holder) with a standing (clear, arguable or untenable under the readings of §7.3) and a measure.

#### D.7.6 Policy Making

**Options** come from the repertoire of §7.7, filtered by what the evaluation found: a problem or need selects the actions whose mechanism acts on its state variable.

**Appraisal specific to the pillar.** General CBA, MCA and the prioritisation of capital investment belong to another part and are held by nomos's `appraisal`. Four appraisals belong to this pillar.

- *Wider economic benefits*: the productivity gain from a change in effective density, computed with a transferred agglomeration elasticity (§3.3). Transport appraisal guidance such as the UK's TAG unit on wider impacts is the model **[M]**.
- *Fiscal impact*: the change in own-source revenue through the fiscal loop. An intervention raises land values; the property tax and capture instruments recover a share, with a lag that depends on revaluation.
- *Value capture feasibility*: §5.5.
- *Debt sustainability*: whether financing the option keeps the debt service ratio under its threshold across the projection's uncertainty.

**Selection** under a debt ceiling and a capital budget is a constrained choice. The knapsack exists in calx and the decision rules (expected value, robustness) in tyche.

**Transfer.** A practice from elsewhere is an option only through a lens whose support factors hold. Cartwright and Hardie's argument, that what worked there will work here if its support factors are present here, is implemented in tekmerion's `warrant` and the policy domain's `transfer`. The support factors of each Japanese practice are listed in §6, and those of each action in §7.7. Kybernesis should refuse a transfer whose factors are unestablished, or carry it as arguable.

#### D.7.7 Actions and Inquiries

Each action below is given by its mechanism, the states it acts on, the evidence that would show it worked, its typical risks, an example and its support factors. The table in §8.3 condenses them.

**A1. Transport investment and transit-oriented development.**

- *Mechanism*: lowers travel costs and so raises effective density and market access, hence productivity and land values. TOD concentrates floor space where access is highest.
- *States*: the cost matrix, access, land values, the distribution of employment and firms.
- *Evidence it worked*: access and employment, firm entry or land values rising near the new line against places not yet reached (staggered difference-in-differences, peira `quasi`). Gibbons et al. (2019) **[V]** find that new roads attract transport-intensive establishments and reorganise existing firms, which is partly relocation; Donaldson and Hornbeck (2016) **[V]** value market access in land.
- *Risks*: relocation counted as creation; cost overruns; low ridership; induced sprawl.
- *Examples*: Tama Den-en-toshi **[S]**; Hong Kong's Rail plus Property (Suzuki et al. 2015 **[V]**); Senri-Chuo in Osaka **[V]**.
- *Support factors*: §6.1.

**A2. Land readjustment and urban redevelopment.**

- *Mechanism*: pools parcels, takes contributions of land for infrastructure and reserve plots sold to pay for works, and returns re-plotted parcels of higher value. Redevelopment exchanges land rights for floor area in a denser building.
- *States*: parcel structure, infrastructure stock, land values, tenure.
- *Evidence it worked*: infrastructure delivered per hectare; reserve-land sales covering costs; value before and after; owners' consent; time to completion.
- *Risks*: holdouts; decades-long schemes; tenants displaced; valuations disputed; failure in transfer (Thailand **[S]**).
- *Examples*: Japan (Matsui 2019 **[V]**); Fushimi West and Umekita **[V]**; Hiroshima's reconstruction **[V]**; the town planning schemes of Gujarat **[M]**.
- *Support factors*: §6.2.

**A3. Zoning and land-use reform.**

- *Mechanism*: raising permitted density relaxes the constraint on floor space, which brings construction, slower price growth and agglomeration.
- *States*: the zoning envelope, the supply of floor space, prices.
- *Evidence it worked*: construction in upzoned against other areas, allowing for displacement between them. Greenaway-McGrevy and Phillips (2023) **[V]** (*Journal of Urban Economics* 136; doi:10.1016/j.jue.2023.103555) find that Auckland's 2016 upzoning of about three-quarters of residential land stimulated construction, robust to large displacement effects.
- *Risks*: infrastructure capacity exceeded; windfalls to owners left uncaptured; heritage lost.
- *Examples*: Auckland **[V]**; Japan's floor area exceptions in priority zones for urban renaissance **[V]** (TDLC 2024).
- *Support factors*: demand for floor space; infrastructure headroom; the capacity to enforce.
- *In the infrastructure*: architecture's `rules` and `form` compute what a lot may hold, and urban science's `luti` runs the response.

**A4. Land value capture instruments.**

- *Mechanism*: recovers part of the uplift that public action creates.
- *States*: municipal revenue; land values, since levies are capitalised; the timing of development.
- *Evidence it worked*: revenue collected against projection; share of uplift captured; no deterrence of development (permits before and after).
- *Risks*: uplift overestimated; development deterred; capture by insiders; legal challenge; volatile yields from land sales.
- *Examples*: Hong Kong and São Paulo's air rights (Suzuki et al. 2015 **[V]**); Japan's city planning tax **[V]**; the 60 country profiles of the OECD–Lincoln compendium **[V]**.
- *Support factors*: a legal basis; valuation capacity; transparent accounts; trust. Distrust is the barrier TDLC's participants named **[V]**.

**A5. Property tax reform and own-source revenue.**

- *Mechanism*: expands the roll's coverage, updates valuations, and improves collection and enforcement.
- *States*: the roll, valuations, collection efficiency, own-source revenue.
- *Evidence it worked*: growth in own-source revenue, coverage and collection efficiency against comparable cities or randomised assignment. Balán, Bergeron, Tourek and Weigel (2022) **[V]** (*American Economic Review* 112(3): 762–797) show in a field experiment in Kananga, DRC, that city chiefs' local information raised property tax compliance over collection by state agents.
- *Risks*: regressive valuations; political backlash; shocks at revaluation.
- *Examples*: Kananga **[V]**; Japan's fixed asset tax **[V]**; the priority actions of TDLC's 2026 Deep Dive **[V]**.
- *Support factors*: a roll and a cadastre to build on; valuation capacity; legitimacy of enforcement; local power over the rate.

**A6. Intergovernmental transfers.**

- *Mechanism*: equalising, conditional or performance-based grants. A predictable formula supports planning and borrowing.
- *States*: revenue, fiscal capacity gaps, incentives for own effort.
- *Evidence it worked*: predictability (deviation from formula); equalisation (dispersion of fiscal capacity after transfers); no crowding-out of own effort.
- *Risks*: dependence; soft budget constraints; crowding-out.
- *Examples*: Japan's local allocation tax, whose 25 percent retention preserves the incentive to raise own revenue **[V]**; performance-based grants in World Bank operations **[M]**.
- *Support factors*: national fiscal space; data for a need formula; credible commitment.

**A7. Municipal borrowing and creditworthiness.**

- *Mechanism*: long-term finance brings infrastructure forward and matches its cost to its life; it requires creditworthiness.
- *States*: the debt stock, debt service, capital investment, the rating.
- *Evidence it worked*: borrowing obtained at falling spreads; rating upgrades; debt service within threshold; capital executed.
- *Risks*: unsustainable debt; foreign-currency mismatch (GPSC **[V]**); contingent liabilities; expectations of bail-out.
- *Examples*: Japan's consultation system with permission at 18 percent **[V]**. The Japan Finance Organization for Municipalities, capitalised by all prefectures and municipalities, issues bonds and lends long-term to local governments and their enterprises **[V]** (Ministry of Finance debt management reports); its predecessor was reorganised in 2008 **[M]** for the exact date. The City Creditworthiness Academy **[V]**.
- *Support factors*: a legal framework for subnational borrowing; a local capital market; accounting and audit; autonomy over revenue.

**A8. Public–private partnerships.**

- *Mechanism*: private finance and efficiency under a contract that allocates risk.
- *States*: capital stock, service quality, contingent liabilities.
- *Evidence it worked*: value for money against a public comparator; availability and performance; fiscal commitments recorded.
- *Risks*: hidden liabilities; renegotiation; weak management of contracts.
- *Examples*: Clean Plaza Fujimi's design–build–operate contract; Shinagawa Season Terrace's air rights **[V]** (TDLC 2026).
- *Support factors*: a PPP law and unit; project preparation; accounting for fiscal commitments (the IMF and World Bank's PFRAM **[M]**).

**A9. Economic zones and cluster policy.**

- *Mechanism*: concentrated infrastructure and incentives attract firms and may seed agglomeration.
- *States*: firm location, employment, exports, land.
- *Evidence it worked*: growth in zones beyond relocation from elsewhere, and spillovers. Kline and Moretti (2014) **[V]** (*Quarterly Journal of Economics* 129(1): 275–331) find that the Tennessee Valley Authority's gains in manufacturing persisted after the subsidies ended while those in agriculture did not **[M]** for the detail. The World Bank's *Special Economic Zones: An Operational Review of Their Impacts* (2017) **[V]** (handle 10986/29054) reviews zone performance and the Bank's portfolio.
- *Risks*: relocation rather than creation; costly incentives; enclaves; dispossession from land.
- *Examples*: the TVA **[V]**; Kitakyushu's Eco-Town **[V]**.
- *Support factors*: market access, a labour pool, governance of the zone, and fit with existing industry (Grover et al. 2022 **[V]**).

**A10. Rural–urban connectivity.**

- *Mechanism*: roads and market links lower the costs of trade between towns and their hinterland and let labour move out of agriculture.
- *States*: the market access of rural places, employment structure, prices.
- *Evidence it worked*: Asher and Novosad (2020) **[V]** (*American Economic Review* 110(3): 797–823) find that India's rural roads programme mainly moved workers out of agriculture, with little change in agricultural output or consumption **[M]** for the detail.
- *Risks*: low traffic; unfunded maintenance; hollowing-out of lagging places.
- *Examples*: India's PMGSY, the programme Asher and Novosad study **[M]** for the name; Japan's roadside stations (michi-no-eki) **[M]**.
- *Support factors*: demand in the towns; funded maintenance; targeting by the Degree of Urbanisation's classes.

**A11. Cadastre and SDI investment.**

- *Mechanism*: recorded boundaries and rights lower transaction costs and enable the property tax, capture and readjustment. The SDI underlies every other action.
- *States*: cadastral coverage, roll coverage, the availability of data.
- *Evidence it worked*: coverage growing; transactions recorded; the roll expanding; IGIF levels rising.
- *Risks*: costly field survey stalls, which is the fit-for-purpose argument **[V]**; disputes; data not maintained.
- *Examples*: Japan's cadastral survey (53 percent overall, 27 percent in dense districts **[V]**); PLATEAU **[V]**; Rwanda's land tenure regularisation **[M]**.
- *Support factors*: legal recognition of general boundaries; funded maintenance; an institutional mandate (the IGIF's Governance and Institutions pathway).

**Inquiries.** An inquiry is a decision to observe, worth taking when it can change a decision by more than it costs.

- **I1. Diagnostics.** The MFSA and Urban Audit **[V]** observe the fiscal state and the investment programme. A subnational PEFA **[V]** observes public financial management. The LGAF **[V]** observes land governance. An IGIF action plan **[V]** observes the SDI. A competitive-cities diagnostic **[V]** observes the economy and its levers. Their outputs are mostly ordinal; their cost runs from weeks of self-assessment to months of expert work **[M]**.
- **I2. Valuations.** Mass appraisal for the roll (IAAO standards **[M]**); a study of value uplift around a planned line (§5.5); a shadow credit rating **[V]**. Each produces an estimate with an interval that enters §7.4.
- **I3. Studies.** Enterprise surveys **[M]**; origin–destination and commuting surveys; a visitor expenditure survey for a TSA; a study of the property tax gap; the feasibility of a capture scheme; an appraisal of wider economic benefits.

#### D.7.8 Results Capture

TDLC's practitioner programmes end in *results capture* (the brief). For kybernesis, results capture is the return path from decision to evidence.

**What to record.** Outcome indicators are state variables from §3; output indicators record whether an action was delivered. Each carries a baseline, a target and successive measurements, with the reading of every open term fixed at baseline. A changed reading at follow-up breaks comparability, which archeion's `datasets` reports between versions of a codebook.

**Attribution chosen in advance.** Staggered roll-out (stations, readjustment schemes, tax reforms by district) permits staggered difference-in-differences against units not yet reached (peira `quasi`). The design should be declared before results arrive, using peira's `protocol` for plans and deviations and archeion's sealed journal, so that results cannot be fitted afterwards. Where spillovers are expected (TOD, zones), peira's `interference` estimates direct and spillover effects by rings of distance.

**Lags.** Land values respond within a year or two; employment and firm location over several years; the fiscal loop only after revaluation **[M]**. Results capture at a fixed horizon must say which effects it can have seen.

**Return to estimation.** Measured effects update the priors of §7.4. A locally measured uplift or elasticity becomes data for the next city's transfer, with its support factors recorded.

**Public record as results capture.** Japan publishes each authority's fiscal soundness ratios every year, with counts of authorities above each threshold in the White Paper on Local Public Finance **[V]**. A public, comparable, annual record of this kind is the strongest form of results capture for fiscal reforms.

#### D.8.2 The Pillar by Block

| Block | Methods | Evidence | What is better estimated than observed | Open terms | In the infrastructure |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 Collection | scoping by value of information; data-sharing agreements; sample design for enterprise, visitor and resident surveys | registers, budgets, rolls, surveys, lights, OSM/Overture, GTFS, routing speeds, mobile and card data | which data to collect first is itself a decision under uncertainty | which units and periods; what "core database" contains | tyche tests within a budget on calx's knapsack (ADR 0123); data library registry; peira survey designs |
| 2 Evidence | provenance grading; custody and digests; licence carried with each trace; counting shared origins once | as above, each with provenance, licence, resolution, custody (audited or not), biases | none; this block records, it does not estimate | official record against estimate against modelled product | archeion (holdings, custody, `datasets` codebooks); tekmerion (grades, shared origins) |
| 3 Interpretation | every open term read under every admissible reading, each with its source; pivots shown | standards and statutes (ISO 37120, PEFA, TSA:RMF, IRTS, DEGURBA, Japan's Act), conventions (GPSC, World Bank) | none; readings are declared, not estimated | §7.3: unit, output, job, firm, own-source revenue, debt, cost recovery, creditworthy, captured value, uplift, visitor, contribution, cadastral coverage, lagging region, carrying capacity | tekmerion `construal`; apodeixis (derivability under readings) |
| 4 Estimation | downscaling and change of support; dasymetric allocation; small-area estimation; hedonic and spatial models; gravity calibration; capture–recapture (proposal); cash-flow projection under uncertainty; Bayesian transfer of elasticities | accounts, lights, buildings, places, LFS, transactions, budgets, mobile data | output and employment by place; commuting; land values between transactions; agglomeration elasticity; tax potential; future cash flows and debt capacity; unregistered visitors; tourism GVA without a TSA | the reading of each estimated quantity, inherited from block 3 | tyche (`spatial`, `bayes`, `uncertainty`, area of applicability), peira (`small_areas`, imperfect detection), nomos (`interaction`), urban science (`demand`, `luti`, `access`), kinesis `scales`; cash-flow model new |
| 5 Evaluation | ratios, shares, densities, elasticities, concentrations, access measures, gaps against standards; ordinal scorecards kept ordinal | estimated state with intervals; standards with their holders | the standing of a judgement under the estimate's interval | the standard chosen and its holder; which threshold applies | nomos `measurement` and composite indicators; urban `indicators` catalogue; kybernesis evaluation spaces; municipal finance ratios and scorecards new |
| 6 Policy making | options from the repertoire; wider-benefits, fiscal-impact, capture-feasibility and debt-sustainability appraisals; selection under debt and budget limits; transfer through support factors | evaluation outputs; transferred effects and their support factors | effects of options (ex ante), with intervals | which effects count (direct only or wider); discount rates | nomos `appraisal` (brief here); calx knapsack; tyche decision rules; tekmerion `warrant`; policy domain `transfer` |
| 7 Actions and inquiries | the eleven actions and three kinds of inquiry of §7.7 | the evidence each action's success would leave (§8.3) | the value of an inquiry (value of information) | what counts as delivery | tyche `decision` (value of information); kybernesis decision block |
| 8 Results capture | baselines and targets with fixed readings; attribution declared in advance; staggered difference-in-differences; spillovers by rings; sealed record; priors updated | follow-up measurements of §3's indexes; delivery records | effects net of trend and spillover | readings frozen at baseline | peira (`quasi`, `interference`, `protocol`); archeion sealed journal and `datasets` versions |

The infrastructure already holds most of the computation:

- density, per-capita and concentration measures (nomos `measurement`);
- accessibility and market access (nomos, urban science `access`, economics `trade`);
- hedonic valuation and spatial dependence (urban science `demand`, tyche `spatial`);
- uplift and results by staggered adoption, with spillovers (peira `quasi`, `interference`);
- appraisal and discounting (nomos `appraisal`);
- uncertainty (tyche `uncertainty`);
- input–output multipliers (economics `production`, industrial ecology `eeio`);
- aggregation across levels (kinesis `scales`);
- change of support (tyche `spatial`);
- small-area estimation (peira `small_areas`);
- readings (tekmerion `construal`);
- transfer (tekmerion `warrant`);
- custody and codebooks (archeion).

The new pieces needed are:

1. a **municipal finance** module: chart of accounts and its codebooks, the MFSA's ratios, cash-flow projection, debt capacity, a shadow rating as an ordinal scorecard, and statutory threshold schemes such as Japan's as declared standards;
2. a **land value capture** module: the OECD–Lincoln instrument codebook, capture rates and the appraisal of §5.5, composed from urban science, peira and nomos;
3. a **tourism accounts** module: TSA tables and tourism ratios, direct and total effects kept apart, intensity, seasonality and origin concentration;
4. a **shift-share** tool with exposure-robust inference;
5. an **ordinal scorecard** form for IGIF, LGAF and PEFA, each a declared scheme of statements;
6. a **matched-panel agglomeration** estimator, needed only where matched data exist;
7. **data edges** for routing-service speeds, budget extracts (BOOST) and accommodation statistics.

| A1 Transport investment and TOD | lower travel cost raises effective density and access; floor space concentrated at stations | cost matrix, access, land values, employment and firm location | access, firm entry, employment and values rising near the line against places not yet reached; relocation separated (Gibbons et al. 2019 [V]) | relocation counted as creation; overruns; low ridership; sprawl | Tama Den-en-toshi [S]; Hong Kong Rail plus Property [V]; Senri-Chuo [V] | ridership potential; land near stations; operator's right to develop; transport and planning integrated |

| A8 PPPs | private finance and efficiency under a risk-allocating contract | capital stock, service quality, contingent liabilities | value for money against a public comparator; performance; commitments recorded | hidden liabilities; renegotiation; weak contract management | Clean Plaza Fujimi (DBO), Shinagawa Season Terrace [V] | PPP law and unit; project preparation; fiscal commitment accounting [M] |
| A9 Economic zones and cluster policy | concentrated infrastructure and incentives attract firms; possible agglomeration | firm location, employment, exports, land | growth beyond relocation; spillovers; persistence after incentives (Kline and Moretti 2014 [V]) | relocation not creation; costly incentives; enclaves; land dispossession | TVA [V]; Kitakyushu Eco-Town [V]; World Bank SEZ review 2017 [V] | market access; labour pool; zone governance; fit with existing industry (Grover et al. 2022 [V]) |
| A10 Rural–urban connectivity | lower trade costs between towns and hinterland; labour leaves agriculture | rural market access, employment structure, prices | sectoral reallocation and prices against unconnected places (Asher and Novosad 2020 [V]) | low traffic; unfunded maintenance; hollowing-out of lagging places | India's rural roads programme (Asher and Novosad 2020 [V]; PMGSY [M]); Japan's roadside stations [M] | demand in towns; maintenance funding; targeting by DEGURBA classes |
