# Chapter E. Knowledge Programmes and the Transfer of Practice

## Part E. Knowledge Programmes and the Transfer of Practice

*Status:* seed. *Activity:* research (a survey for the design of a plug-in of the Urban Studio on kybernesis). *Compiled:* 2026-11-19.

*Markers.* **[V]** verified in this session against a primary record (the issuing body's page, the report itself, or the DOI's registry record at Crossref); **[S]** confirmed only from secondary sources (search summaries, library or bookseller records, other authors' accounts); **[M]** from memory, not verified. Where a bibliographic record is verified but a claim about its content rests on secondary accounts, the claim says so.

---

### E.1. Concepts: A Knowledge Programme as a Steered System

A knowledge programme such as the Tokyo Development Learning Center's is itself a system steered through evidence. Its state is not observed directly. What it acts on (the capacity of a client city's agencies to carry out a reform, and the course of a financed operation) is latent; what it observes are traces: applications, needs statements, action plans, session records, follow-up surveys, outcome statements written by participants and task teams, and the documents of operations. Its actions are a repertoire of formats (technical deep dives, peer exchanges, twinning, advisory missions, knowledge notes, grants for pilots), each with a mechanism by which it is meant to change that latent state. Its results are claims of contribution, never of sole attribution, because a knowledge exchange is almost always a small part of a larger effort.

Three distinctions organise what follows.

1. **Learning and change.** The development literature separates the immediate result of a learning activity (a change in the cognition or behaviour of an agent) from the subsequent change in the institutions that condition a development goal. The World Bank Institute's Capacity Development Results Framework names these "learning outcomes" and "capacity indicators" respectively (§3.1). Most evaluation of knowledge exchange stops at the first; operations care about the second.
2. **Contribution and attribution.** Whether an exchange caused an operation's outcome is rarely identifiable by comparison with what would have been; contribution analysis, outcome harvesting and process tracing instead build and test a causal story with explicit evidence (§3.4, §3.5). In kybernesis's terms each such conclusion needs a standing (formal: clear, arguable or untenable under the readings of its terms) and a measure (statistical: a likelihood ratio, a posterior, an interval).
3. **Transfer and copying.** A practice that worked in a Japanese city is evidence about a mechanism in a relational system, not a recipe. Transfer is the lens the brief describes: an interpretation *f* of the target in the source's terms and a realisation *g* of the source's actions in the target, valid when the mechanism commutes within a tolerance. Cartwright and Hardie's support factors (§5.3) and the historical record of Japanese land readjustment abroad (§5.5) show what that tolerance depends on.

---

### E.2. The Tokyo Development Learning Center and the Tokyo DRM Hub

#### E.2.1 Mandate and Organisation

The World Bank's TDLC page describes the centre as "a leading global knowledge hub with a mission to identify Japanese and global best practices, generate new insights, and share actionable solutions", "to maximize the impact of World Bank Group–financed urban development projects in developing countries"; it was founded in 2004 as a partnership of the World Bank and the Government of Japan, is funded by Japan's Ministry of Finance, and is managed by the Bank's Urban, Subnational Finance, Tourism, and Disaster Management Global Department [V: TDLC About page]. The public pages list four integrated activities: Technical Deep Dives, Operational Support, Partnership and Collaboration, and Outreach and Dissemination [V: TDLC Program page]. The phrase "TDLC Practitioner Programs" and the "learning-to-action journey" (needs identification, collaborative learning, the Technical Deep Dive in Japan, advisory support, community engagement, results capture) appear in the October 2026 posting for the Urban Analyst [S: the posting as mirrored on an aggregator] and in a listing of the WBG Academy's Practitioner Programs on GFDRR's site [S: search summary; the page refused automated reading]. The public programme pages read in this session do not yet use that framing; it appears to be the vocabulary of the programme's next phase.

*Caveat on phases.* The FY2019–20 annual report says TDLC completed Phase 3 (FY2016–FY2020) and gives Phase 4 as FY2020–2024 [V: annual report text]; a June 2024 brief calls the current programme "Phase IV Program (FY2021 to FY2025)" and reports consultations with six partner cities to inform Phase V [V: TDLC brief, 21 June 2024]. The two dates differ; the later brief is likely the operative one.

#### E.2.2 Technical Deep Dives

The TDD is TDLC's "signature one-week cohort-based knowledge acceleration program" [V: FY19–20 annual report]. As documented:

- **Selection.** Topics are demand-driven, and participants are selected to maximise impact on an existing Bank operation in design or implementation [V: FY19–20 annual report]. Delegations come with their World Bank task team leaders (TTLs).
- **Delivery.** Expert presentations from Japan and elsewhere, site visits, peer exchanges including "shift-and-share" sessions in which country teams rotate between tables to compare challenges and solutions, one-to-one sessions, and sometimes e-learning modules beforehand [V: TDD page; FY19–20 annual report].
- **Action planning.** Delegates work with their TTLs on an action plan with specific "asks" stating how the Bank and TDLC can support it; the plans link to Operational Support [V: FY19–20 annual report]. The current TDD page speaks of "implementation-ready action plans" followed by technical assistance and expert dispatches to project sites [V: TDD page]. The April 2025 city-finance TDD, for example, ended with five shared commitments (diversified municipal financing strategies, better own-source revenue, capacity for performance-based grants, formula-based capital allocation, debt management frameworks) [V: event implementation report].
- **Scale.** FY2020: three TDDs convened 85 client officials from 29 cities in 27 countries and 41 TTLs, linked to 49 projects worth US$8.77 billion; Phase 3 (FY16–FY20) cumulatively held 28 TDDs with 1,462 participants from 80 countries [V: FY19–20 annual report]. The 20th-anniversary story reports "over 50 TDDs to date", "contributing to 430 projects across 87 countries worth $75 billion" [V: TDLC brief, 8 October 2024].
- **Themes.** FY2020: solid waste (with plastics), low-income urban settlements, creative cities; a TOD TDD was cancelled for COVID-19 [V: FY19–20 annual report]. FY2026: solid waste for a circular economy (25–29 May 2026), integrated urban recovery in Ukraine (6–10 April 2026), city finance (9–13 March 2026), PPPs for affordable housing, urban planning and land for growth, cities for tourism and jobs, urban flood management [V: TDD page]. An urban planning and land TDD ran 27–31 October 2025 in Tokyo, Kyoto and Osaka with about forty participants from ten countries, and an urban flood management TDD 2–6 February 2026 in Tokyo and Shizuoka [S: search summaries of TDLC pages].

*Remark.* "Contributing to 430 projects … worth $75 billion" counts operations linked to participants; it is a measure of reach, not of effect. A tool should keep the two apart (§7.3).

#### E.2.3 Operational Support and the City Partnership Program

Operational Support provides "customized advisory and technical support" after a TDD, just-in-time or extended [V: TDLC Program page; FY19–20 annual report]. In FY2020 it supported 19 projects (US$2,431 million), and over Phase 3 104 projects in 35 countries [V: FY19–20 annual report].

The City Partnership Program began in 2016 with Kitakyushu, Kobe, Toyama and Yokohama, chosen by a committee; Fukuoka and Kyoto joined in 2018, Hiroshima in 2023, Shizuoka and Sapporo in 2025, and Tokyo and Osaka collaborate outside it [V: CPP page; TDLC briefs of June 2025]. (The TDLC home page and the 2024 anniversary story still say seven cities [V], so the count depends on the page.) Cities are chosen for "their leadership, practical knowledge, and operational expertise"; they speak at TDDs, are the subjects of customised case studies, and send experts on Operational Support [V: CPP page; anniversary story]. Two documented examples: Yokohama's participatory design approach (its "City Design Sketchbook") used in workshops for Panama City and Barranquilla, and Fukuoka's wastewater operation and maintenance shared with Ahmedabad under the Gujarat resilient cities project, with visits both ways; the story reports no quantitative results [V: anniversary story on city partnership]. In the June 2024 consultation, cities asked for "deeper engagement and co-ownership in the design of knowledge events", for opening the programme to local enterprises, academia and youth, and for documentation of outcomes in participating countries [V: brief of 21 June 2024].

#### E.2.4 Knowledge Products

FY2020 produced 13 knowledge notes and research products (five Japan case studies and methodologies, five frontier or cross-cutting studies, three others) and 15 events reaching over 3,000 people [V: FY19–20 annual report]. Recent examples include a knowledge note on Yokohama's property tax practice (June 2025), written because the TDD session on it was rated highly, and a note on lessons from Hiroshima's reconstruction for urban land [S: search summaries of TDLC pages]. TDLC's earlier and allied publications on Japanese practice include *Transforming Cities with Transit* (Suzuki, Cervero and Iuchi 2013) [V] and *Financing Transit-Oriented Development with Land Values* (Suzuki, Murakami, Hong and Tamayose 2015), which draws on Tokyo and Hong Kong [V].

#### E.2.5 The Tokyo Disaster Risk Management Hub

The Hub was "Established in 2014 … in acknowledgement of the learning emerging from the 2011 Great East Japan Earthquake and Tsunami", operates under GFDRR, and runs the Japan–World Bank Program for Mainstreaming DRM in Developing Countries, which as of January 2026 had given "over $190 million in grants" to "over 300 technical assistance projects in more than 110 countries" [V: Tokyo DRM Hub page]. It funds technical assistance grants for preparing and implementing resilient operations, a Global Knowledge Program, TDDs on DRM (integrated urban flood risk management, 2016; seismic risk, 2018), the deployment of Japanese and global experts, and knowledge notes such as the four *Learning from Japan's Experience in Integrated Urban Flood Risk Management* notes of 2020 [V: Hub page]. Its claim that the programme contributed to "close to 80%" of supported countries institutionalising DRR as a national priority is the Bank's own [V: Hub page; not independently checked]. The precursor knowledge programme, *Learning from Megadisasters* (Ranghieri and Ishiwatari, eds., 2014), consolidated 36 knowledge notes from a joint Japan–World Bank study [V: DOI 10.1596/978-1-4648-0153-2].

#### E.2.6 What Is Public about Design, Delivery and Evaluation

Public: the TDD format and its selection rule, action plans with asks, the link to Operational Support, counts of participants and linked lending, themes, partner cities, examples of uptake told as stories (a 2016 flood-risk TDD informing Antananarivo's PRODUIR project of 2018; Narayanganj; Tanzania's DUTP drawing on TOD) [V: anniversary story], and a 2019 mid-term review survey of 173 respondents [V: FY19–20 annual report]. Not found in public: a results framework for the programme with indicators and baselines, follow-up rates of action plans, any comparison of linked and unlinked operations, or a method for attributing contribution. TDLC says it "collects feedback from project teams" on how TDDs shaped preparation and implementation [V: anniversary story]. As a trust-funded programme it is expected to keep an indicative results framework at trustee level, though the Bank's guidance exempts "small, one-off activities such as a single training or workshop" from separate frameworks [V: Guidance on Managing World Bank Trust Funds for Results, November 2024]. The gap between reach and contribution is where a tool can help most.

---

### E.3. Frameworks for Designing Knowledge Exchange and Capturing Its Results

#### E.3.1 The Capacity Development Results Framework

Otoo, Agapitova and Behrens (2009) define a capacity development programme as "any coherent set of learning activities that is intended to facilitate locally owned sociopolitical, policy-related, and organizational change in pursuit of a specific development goal" [V]. The framework has four elements: a development goal; **three capacity factors** (conduciveness of the sociopolitical environment, efficiency of policy instruments, effectiveness of organisational arrangements), each with standard indicators (for the first: commitment of leaders, compatibility with social norms, stakeholder participation, stakeholder voice, accountability of public service providers, transparency of information); a change process driven by **agents of change**; and learning activities. It types **six learning outcomes** in three groups: altered status (raised awareness, enhanced skills), altered processes (improved consensus/teamwork, fostered coalitions/networks) and new products (formulated policy/strategy, implemented strategy/plan). It distinguishes "learning outcomes", which "measure change at the level of the agent", from "capacity indicators", which measure "whether the actions taken by the agent of change after learning have a favorable impact on the larger system". Its programme cycle has four stages and ten steps, from validating the goal and assessing capacity factors (needs assessment) through monitoring learning outcomes and capacity indicators to a final assessment with follow-up actions [V: the working paper].

For kybernesis the CDRF is unusually useful: its capacity indicators are the latent state to be estimated, its learning outcomes are evaluation operators at the agent level, and its stage 1 is a declared needs-assessment procedure.

#### E.3.2 The Art of Knowledge Exchange

The World Bank's *Art of Knowledge Exchange: A Results-Focused Planning Guide for Development Practitioners* (2nd ed. updated, 2015; led by Shobha Kumar with Leonard, Watkins, Vovides and Kerby), drawn from over 100 exchanges financed by the South-South Facility, sets five steps: Anchor (development goal, institutional challenge, change objective), Define (participants, intermediate outcomes, knowledge providers), Design and Develop, Implement, Measure and Report [V: the guide]. It names **five intermediate outcomes**: new knowledge, enhanced skill, improved consensus, enhanced connectivity, and new and improved actions, the first four "individually or cumulatively" leading to the last; knowledge exchange addresses "cognitive (know why), relational (know who), and behavioral (know how) gaps" [V]. It insists that exchange "is more likely to catalyze progress towards the change objective than to achieve the objective on its own" [V]. Its instruments include workshops, study tours, multi-stakeholder dialogues and consultations, expert visits, conferences and communities of practice [V].

#### E.3.3 Training Evaluation: Kirkpatrick and Its Critics

Kirkpatrick's four levels (reaction, learning, behaviour, results) originate in a four-part series, "Techniques for Evaluating Training Programs", in the *Journal of the American Society of Training Directors* from November 1959, where they were called "steps" [S: Kirkpatrick's own retrospective as quoted in secondary sources]; the standard book is *Evaluating Training Programs: The Four Levels* (Berrett-Koehler, 1994) [S]. Holton (1996), "The flawed four-level evaluation model", argues it is a taxonomy rather than a causal model, with weak evidence that the levels are causally linked [V: DOI 10.1002/hrdq.3920070103; content as commonly summarised, S]. Brinkerhoff's Success Case Method studies the extreme successes and failures among trainees to learn what enabled or blocked use [V: DOI 10.1177/1523422304272172]; it is a natural complement to small cohorts such as a TDD's.

#### E.3.4 Outcome-Centred Methods

**Outcome Harvesting** (Wilson-Grau and Britt 2012, rev. 2013, for the Ford Foundation's MENA office) "does not measure progress towards predetermined outcomes", but "collects evidence of what has been achieved, and works backward to determine whether and how the project or intervention contributed"; an outcome is "a change in the behavior, relationships, actions, activities, policies, or practices of an individual, group, community, organization, or institution", after IDRC's Outcome Mapping. Its six steps: design the harvest; review documentation and draft outcome descriptions; engage with change agents; substantiate (with independent, knowledgeable sources); analyse and interpret; support use [V: the brief]. The method is elaborated in Wilson-Grau's *Outcome Harvesting: Principles, Steps, and Evaluation Applications* (Information Age Publishing, 2018/2019) [S].

**Most Significant Change** (Davies and Dart 2005, version 1.00) collects stories of change in declared domains and has panels at successive levels select the most significant, with the reasons recorded; its ten steps run from raising interest and defining domains to verification, quantification, secondary analysis and meta-monitoring, and revising the system [V: the guide]. MSC makes explicit whose values judge significance, which is exactly kybernesis's "standards held by someone".

#### E.3.5 Causal-Explanatory Methods

**Contribution analysis** (Mayne 2001, *Canadian Journal of Program Evaluation* 16(1)) builds a contribution story along a theory of change and strengthens it with evidence on each link and on rival explanations [S]; Mayne (2012), "Contribution analysis: Coming of age?" [V: DOI 10.1177/1356389012451663]. Befani and Mayne (2014) combine it with process tracing, using the probative value of evidence to update confidence in each causal link [V: DOI 10.1111/1759-5436.12110]. **Process tracing** is set out in Beach and Pedersen (2019) [V: DOI 10.3998/mpub.10072208]. **Realist evaluation** (Pawson and Tilley 1997, *Realistic Evaluation*) explains outcomes as mechanisms fired in contexts (CMO configurations) and asks "what works, for whom, in what circumstances" [S]; its reporting standards are RAMESES II [V: DOI 10.1186/s12916-016-0643-1]. For transfer, realist evaluation is the bridge: a CMO configuration is a partial statement of the mechanism and of the support factors a target must have.

#### E.3.6 Adaptive Approaches

Andrews, Pritchett and Woolcock's **problem-driven iterative adaptation** (PDIA) answers "isomorphic mimicry", in which governments change what they look like rather than what they do, with locally nominated problems, positive deviance and experimentation, tight feedback loops and broad sets of agents, instead of transplanted "best practice" [V: *World Development* 51 (2013) 234–244, DOI 10.1016/j.worlddev.2013.05.011; *Building State Capability* (OUP 2017), DOI 10.1093/acprof:oso/9780198747482.001.0001]. "Looking like a state" names the techniques of persistent failure [V: DOI 10.1080/00220388.2012.709614]. The World Bank's Global Delivery Initiative (launched 2014) commissioned delivery case studies of "non-technical" implementation challenges under a taxonomy of delivery challenges [S]. PDIA matters here twice: it is a design for the client's reform, and its loop (try, learn, adapt) is kybernesis's policy loop applied to a programme's own formats.

#### E.3.7 Results Frameworks and the Tracing of Advice into Operations

A results framework, in the Bank's current trust-fund guidance, has a development objective ("a clear statement of the intended measurable outcome(s)"), results indicators at the objective level and intermediate results, and an M&E plan, underpinned by a theory of change that states "the critical assumptions … outside the scope of the project" [V: November 2024 guidance]. In operations the development objective is the PDO in the financing agreement or project appraisal document, and IEG notes that the two can differ [S]. The Bank's Implementation Status and Results reports (ISRs) rate progress during implementation [M].

Tracing advisory work into operations is weakly institutionalised. IEG's *Results and Performance of the World Bank Group 2022* found that country self-evaluations rarely cover the use and influence of advisory services and analytics (ASA); where they mention policy dialogue (21 of 50 sampled countries), 19 credit ASA with facilitating it, but fewer than half report higher levels of influence such as uptake in government programmes or operations; IEG's 2025 concept note says the Bank lacks an evaluative framework for ASA [S: search summaries of IEG documents; IEG's site refused automated reading]. IEG's earlier *Learning and Results in World Bank Operations: How the Bank Learns* (2014) examined how knowledge is acquired, captured and transferred in lending [S]. The World Bank Group's *Knowledge Compact for Action* (FY2024) and its new Scorecard of 22 indicators frame knowledge as central [S]. A knowledge programme that records its contribution claims with evidence would supply what IEG finds missing.

---

### E.4. Needs Identification and Community Engagement

#### E.4.1 Training Needs Analysis and Knowledge Gaps

Training needs analysis classically works at three levels, organisation, task and person (McGehee and Thayer 1961, *Training in Business and Industry*) [M]; Iqbal and Khan (2011) review the concept and propose a model [V: DOI 10.1108/03090591111138017]. In development, needs are framed against a goal: the CDRF's stage 1 validates the development goal, assesses capacity factors and decides which changes learning can facilitate [V], and the Art of Knowledge Exchange anchors an exchange in a change objective agreed with counterparts before choosing participants and instruments [V]. TDLC's rule that participants are selected to maximise impact on an existing operation makes the operation's open problems the unit of need [V]. A knowledge gap is thus a need in the needs domain's sense: a gap judged by a standard, here the standard of what the operation's PDO requires and of what the counterpart says it lacks.

#### E.4.2 Citizen Engagement and Grievance Redress

The World Bank's *Strategic Framework for Mainstreaming Citizen Engagement in World Bank Group Operations* (2014) followed the President's 2013 commitment to beneficiary feedback in 100% of projects with clearly identifiable beneficiaries, tracked through the corporate scorecard; IDA later added an indicator of beneficiary feedback at design [S]. Under the 2016 Environmental and Social Framework, **ESS10** requires borrowers to identify stakeholders (with attention to the disadvantaged or vulnerable), prepare a Stakeholder Engagement Plan, disclose information and carry out "meaningful consultation", "a two-way process" that begins early, continues as risks arise, rests on prior disclosure in local languages, "considers and responds to feedback", is free of manipulation and coercion, and "is documented and disclosed"; borrowers must run a grievance mechanism proportionate to the project's risks (ESS10 ¶¶21–22, 26–28) [V: ESS10 guidance note, which reproduces the standard]. The Bank's Grievance Redress Service, established in 2015, lets people complain directly to the Bank, between project mechanisms and the Inspection Panel [S]. For a learning-to-action journey these are both topics (Japanese *machizukuri* and Yokohama's participatory design are transferable practices) and evidence: consultation records and grievance logs are traces of how a transferred practice lands in a community.

---

### E.5. Policy Transfer and Lesson-Drawing

#### E.5.1 Transfer and Lesson-Drawing

Dolowitz and Marsh's review asked who transfers, what, from where, with what degree of transfer and with what constraints [V: *Political Studies* 44(2) (1996) 343–357]; their 2000 article added a continuum from voluntary to coercive transfer and three routes to failure, **uninformed** (too little known of how the policy works at its origin), **incomplete** (crucial elements left behind) and **inappropriate** (insufficient attention to differences of context) [V: *Governance* 13(1) 5–23; the three failure types as summarised by secondary sources, S]. Marsh and Sharman distinguish diffusion from transfer [V: DOI 10.1080/01442870902863851]; Stone treats transfer as translation [V: DOI 10.1080/01442872.2012.695933]. Rose's lesson-drawing asks whether a programme that works elsewhere could work here, through a model of its cause-and-effect and a prospective evaluation [V: "What is lesson-drawing?", *Journal of Public Policy* 11(1) (1991) 3–30; *Learning from Comparative Public Policy* (Routledge 2005), DOI 10.4324/9780203585108; content S].

#### E.5.2 Policy Mobilities

Geographers study how urban policies travel through circuits of consultants, conferences, study tours and best-practice databases, and mutate on the way [V: McCann, *Annals of the AAG* 101(1) (2011) 107–130; Peck and Theodore, *Geoforum* 41(2) (2010) 169–174; Temenos and McCann, *Geography Compass* 7(5) (2013) 344–357]; *Mobile Urbanism* (McCann and Ward, eds., 2011) collects the programme [S]. González (2011) documents "urban policy tourism", the short study trips through which the Bilbao and Barcelona "models" spread, and shows their messages mutate as they circulate [V: *Urban Studies* 48(7) 1397–1418, DOI 10.1177/0042098010374510; content from the abstract]. A TDD is a study trip; the literature's warning is that what is learnt on a visit is a curated story of a mechanism, not the mechanism.

#### E.5.3 Effectiveness Here: Support Factors and Transportability

Cartwright and Hardie's *Evidence-Based Policy* argues that "it worked there" supports "it will work here" only if the policy plays the same causal role here, which requires the **support factors** it needs to be present; randomised trials establish the first premise only for the population studied [V: DOI 10.1093/acprof:osobl/9780199841608.001.0001; Cartwright, "Will this policy work for you?", *Philosophy of Science* 79(5) (2012) 973–989; Deaton and Cartwright, *Social Science & Medicine* 210 (2018) 2–21]. Pearl and Bareinboim formalise **transportability**: given a selection diagram marking where source and target differ, an effect in the target is computable from source experiments and target observations exactly when a transport formula exists [V: *Statistical Science* 29(4) (2014) 579–595; Bareinboim and Pearl, *PNAS* 113(27) (2016) 7345–7352]. The infrastructure already holds both: the policy domain's `transfer` (Cartwright and Hardie with the theory's support factors) and peira's transport checked on a selection diagram.

#### E.5.4 Fidelity, Adaptation and the Failure of "Best Practice"

Implementation science names the tension between fidelity to a programme's core and fit to a new setting [V: Castro, Barrera and Martinez, *Prevention Science* 5(1) (2004) 41–45]; the usual resolution separates core components (the mechanism) from adaptable periphery. Development critiques go further: Pritchett and Woolcock show that solutions designed as universal remedies fail where discretionary, transaction-intensive services are concerned [V: *World Development* 32(2) (2004) 191–212]; Grindle argues for "good enough governance" over long lists of best practice [V: *Governance* 17(4) (2004) 525–548]; Woolcock argues that case studies are how external validity of complex interventions is judged, by attention to causal density, implementation capability and expected trajectories [V: *Evaluation* 19(3) (2013) 229–248]. The failure modes for a programme exhibiting Japanese practice are therefore: copying form without mechanism (isomorphic mimicry), transfer without the support factors (incomplete or inappropriate transfer), the curated visit (policy tourism), and mistaking linked lending for effect.

#### E.5.5 Japanese Practice Abroad

**Land readjustment.** JICA's *Land Readjustment: Solving Urban Problems Through Innovative Approach* (De Souza, Ochi and Hosono, eds., 2018) records the dissemination [V: JICA Ogata Research Institute page; chapter 4 read in full]. JICA and the former Ministry of Construction ran training from 1983; from FY1986 to FY2014, 363 participants from 68 countries attended [V]. Two approaches were used [V]:

- *A full set* of long-term experts, training and technical cooperation projects (Thailand, Indonesia, Malaysia). In **Thailand**, after a 1987–89 study, a cabinet decision of 1992 set up a Land Readjustment Committee; three technical cooperation projects over fifteen years (DMUD 1999–2005; promotion 2005–2009; self-sustainability and dissemination 2010–2014) led to the Land Readjustment Act of 2004, a fund, ten pilot projects of which two were nearly complete in 2014 (Phitsanulok, 21 ha and 82 owners; Rama 9 Park, 8.8 ha and 32 owners), and Thailand itself hosting study tours for JICA's course from 2014. In **Malaysia** no project was carried out: the state's strong power of expropriation under the Land Acquisition Act removed the need, and complex tenures (99-year leaseholds, inherited multiple ownership, unknown owners) blocked replotting. **Indonesia** ran urban land consolidation from 1981 (Renon, Denpasar, 77.3 ha); from 1981 to 1991, with no law on land readjustment, 58 projects on 3,196 ha with 17,859 landowners proceeded by owners' consent [V].
- *Training with follow-up* (Nepal, Colombia). **Nepal** built a land pooling system with little Japanese input; the chapter names two support factors: a legal framework (the 1988 Town Development Act) that allowed projects without new law, and strong demand for housing sites in the Kathmandu Valley, so that projects paid for themselves. **Colombia**: country courses 1998–2002 (39 people), a follow-up expert 2000–2003, regional courses 2003–2007 (64 participants, 29 Colombian) and a technical cooperation project 2003–2008, in which candidates from the Andes were pre-trained by former Colombian trainees. Law 388 of 1997 required every city to prepare a land-use plan (POT), and former trainees became its driving force; by 2013 land readjustment and similar projects numbered about fifty, and Colombian experts advised Curitiba from 2012. Mongolia and Afghanistan considered the method in master plan studies [V].

Sorensen (2000) warned that the Japanese model presented abroad by Japanese experts idealised the practice, whose reliance on consensus, persuasion and public initiative was not well understood internationally [V: *Habitat International* 24(1) 51–73, DOI 10.1016/S0197-3975(99)00029-6; content S]. Hong and Needham (2007) analyse land readjustment's economics, law and collective action in Germany, Japan, the Netherlands and Israel [V: Lincoln Institute]. UN-Habitat's *Remaking the Urban Mosaic: Participatory and Inclusive Land Readjustment* (2016), developed with Medellín, adds safeguards for tenants and informal residents [S].

**Disaster risk management.** JICA's BOSAI project (2007–2012) in six Central American countries reached its capacity targets in 90% of target municipalities but 68% of communities, and only partially in national agencies and SE-CEPREDENAC; the ex-post evaluation rated it C, "partially satisfactory" [V: ex-post evaluation by H. Sonoda]. Its lessons bear directly on transfer: activities in pilot sites were not recorded or evaluated, so good practice could not be systematised and spread; sites were chosen without criteria; a single design matrix could not fit six institutional settings; classroom training in Japan worked only when returning trainees became counterparts in the project; and disaster prevention must be taken where it is a local priority, such as places with recent disasters [V].

**Solid waste.** From 2004 Kitakyushu's KITA and the NGO Pusdakota adapted a composting method (the Takakura method) to Surabaya; Kurniawan et al. (2013) examine the lessons of that adaptation for city-to-city cooperation and urban co-benefits [V: *Journal of Cleaner Production* 58, 43–50, DOI 10.1016/j.jclepro.2013.08.002]. Secondary accounts credit scale-up to community engagement (women's groups, NGOs, media), a focus on organic waste first and replication to other cities [S: IGES documents, refused to automated reading].

**City-to-city cooperation.** Tjandradewi, Marcotullio and Kidokoro evaluated Yokohama–Penang cooperation [V: *Habitat International* 30(3) (2006) 357–376]; by secondary account they examined commitment, community-wide participation, mutual understanding, reciprocity and concrete results [S]. Tjandradewi and Marcotullio (2009) surveyed CITYNET managers on key success factors [V: *Habitat International* 33(2) 165–172].

#### E.5.6 Support Factors Read from These Cases

Across the cases the same conditions recur and can be declared as support factors of a transfer: (1) a legal basis that permits the practice without new legislation, or a reform that creates one (Nepal's 1988 Act; Colombia's Law 388); (2) the absence of a cheaper substitute (Malaysia's power of expropriation); (3) tenure simple enough for the mechanism (replotting); (4) demand that makes the practice self-financing (Kathmandu housing sites); (5) local champions placed where they can act, trained and then employed as counterparts (Colombia's trainees; BOSAI's lesson); (6) political priority for the problem (BOSAI: recent disasters); (7) a local adapter and community intermediaries (Pusdakota); (8) a design that records, evaluates and systematises pilots so that they can spread (BOSAI's failure); (9) time: Thailand took about seventeen years from study to Act.

---

### E.6. Knowledge Products and the Synthesis of Lessons

#### E.6.1 What Makes a Product Usable

The evidence-informed policy literature asks of a brief that it address a priority problem, describe the options and their effects with the quality of the evidence graded, state what is known of equity and of implementation, and say how applicable the findings are to the reader's setting [V: Lavis et al., SUPPORT Tool 13, *Health Research Policy and Systems* 7(Suppl 1) (2009) S13; content S]. Two tools are directly reusable: **GRADE-CERQual**, which grades confidence in findings of qualitative synthesis by methodological limitations, coherence, adequacy and relevance [V: Lewin et al., *Implementation Science* 13(Suppl 1) (2018)], and the **TRANSFER approach**, which assesses whether review findings transfer to a context by named factors agreed with decision-makers [V: Munthe-Kaas et al., *BMC Medical Research Methodology* 20 (2020) 11]. Weiss's distinction of instrumental, conceptual and symbolic uses of research [V: *Public Administration Review* 39(5) (1979) 426–431] and the role of knowledge brokers [V: Ward, House and Hamer, *Evidence & Policy* 5(3) (2009) 267–279] tell a programme what "uptake" may mean. A usable knowledge note for a task team therefore has: the operational problem; the Japanese or global practice as a mechanism (CMO); evidence with its grade; the support factors and what replaced them where they were missing; costs and timelines; a transferability section by declared factors; contacts in the source city.

#### E.6.2 Evidence Gap Maps

Evidence and gap maps lay interventions against outcomes and show where systematic reviews and studies exist and where they do not [V: Snilstveit et al., *Journal of Clinical Epidemiology* 79 (2016) 120–129; White et al., Campbell guidance, *Campbell Systematic Reviews* 16(4) (2020) e1125]. For a knowledge programme, a map of practices × outcomes × contexts (country income, city size, legal tradition) shows which Japanese practices have been tried abroad and with what evidence; tekmerion's `reviews` already produces gap maps.

#### E.6.3 Synthesis Across Cases

Lessons across exchanges can be synthesised by **realist synthesis**, which reviews evidence for programme theories rather than pooled effects [V: Pawson et al., *Journal of Health Services Research & Policy* 10(Suppl 1) (2005) 21–34; RAMESES standards, *BMC Medicine* 11 (2013) 21]; by **QCA**, which finds the combinations of conditions sufficient or necessary for an outcome across a moderate number of cases (Ragin 1987, *The Comparative Method*) [M]; and by process tracing within cases (§3.5). Land readjustment's record (Thailand, Nepal, Colombia, Malaysia, Indonesia) is a small-N set suited to crisp-set QCA over the support factors of §5.6, with process tracing inside Colombia and Thailand. zetesis holds both.

---

### E.7. The Loop of a Knowledge Programme, Block by Block

This section reads the loop of a knowledge programme through kybernesis's blocks. Throughout, "the state" means two latent objects: a client city's capacity, read through the CDRF's three factors and their indicators, and the course of the operation the exchange serves, read through its PDO and indicators.

#### E.7.1 Collection

What is gathered, and by which designs:

- **Needs and knowledge gaps.** Requests from task teams and client agencies (TDLC's topics are demand-driven [V]); a structured needs assessment of the capacity factors relevant to the operation's goal (CDRF steps 1–3 [V]); anchoring interviews on the change objective (AoKE step 1 [V]); a pre-TDD questionnaire to delegations. Design: purposive, tied to operations; selection on the outcome is built in, which matters for any later comparison.
- **Participants' learning.** Pre- and post-tests of knowledge, self-assessments of skill, polls during sessions (TDLC used an app for registration, surveys and polls [V]), reaction surveys (Kirkpatrick level 1).
- **Outcomes.** Follow-up at fixed intervals on action-plan items; outcome statements drafted from documents and confirmed with change agents (Outcome Harvesting steps 2–3 [V]); stories in declared domains (MSC [V]); feedback from TTLs (TDLC [V]); operations' documents (PADs, restructuring papers, ISRs, ICRs).
- **Context of transfer.** For each practice, its mechanism and support factors in the source city (from partner cities and case studies), and the same factors observed in the target.

#### E.7.2 Evidence

The traces a programme leaves, each a holding with provenance: applications and nomination letters; needs assessments; delegation profiles; agendas and session briefs; presentation materials; site-visit notes; polls and surveys; action plans with "asks"; Operational Support requests, terms of reference and mission reports of dispatched experts; follow-up surveys; outcome statements with their substantiation; MSC stories with the panels' reasons; knowledge notes and their downloads; and the operations' documents that cite or reflect the exchange. In kybernesis's terms these are reports with sources; several (a TTL's account and the delegation's own) may share an origin and must be counted once (tekmerion's dependent reports).

#### E.7.3 Interpretation

The open terms, each with readings and their sources:

- **"Capacity"**: individual competence (Kirkpatrick level 2); the CDRF's three institutional factors and their indicators [V]; AoKE's institutional capacity challenges [V]. Which reading a claim uses changes what counts as evidence.
- **"Uptake"**: Weiss's instrumental, conceptual or symbolic use [V]; IEG's ladder for ASA, from facilitating dialogue to uptake in government programmes, country strategy and operations [S]; for TDLC, an action-plan item carried into an operation's design.
- **"Outcome"**: Outcome Harvesting's change in behaviour, relationships, actions, policies or practices of a social actor [V]; the Bank's change in condition or behaviour of target groups from use of outputs [V: trust-fund guidance]; Kirkpatrick's "results" [S]. These are not the same set.
- **"Contribution"**: a plausible, evidenced contribution story (Mayne) [S/V]; a verifiable connection (Outcome Harvesting) [V]; a counterfactual effect. "Contributing to 430 projects" uses none of them; it is linkage.
- **"Transferable"**: the same causal role with support factors present (Cartwright and Hardie) [V]; an effect computable by a transport formula on a selection diagram (Pearl and Bareinboim) [V]; transferable by declared context factors (TRANSFER) [V]; the lesson-drawer's judgment (Rose) [S].
- **"Best practice"**, **"good practice"**, **"best fit"**: the programme's mission speaks of "best practices"; the critiques of §5.4 suggest each be read as a mechanism with conditions.

Each claim is read under every combination of these readings (tekmerion's `construal`), giving its standing: a contribution clear under every reading, arguable, or untenable.

#### E.7.4 Estimation

What is better estimated than observed:

- **A client city's capacity factors**, latent and measured by several indicators: by factor or latent class models (tyche `latent`) or item response models (nomos `psychometrics`) on ordinal indicators, with change over cohorts by mixed models (tyche `mixed`).
- **Participants' learning**: change scores with item response models where tests exist; otherwise ordinal self-reports.
- **The persistence of action-plan items**: time to implementation or abandonment, censored at the last follow-up, by survival methods (peira `survival`), pooling across cohorts.
- **Proportions in small cohorts** (share of delegations reporting use): Wilson or exact intervals (tyche `probability`); pooling across many small cohorts by small-area models (peira `small_areas`).
- **Contribution to an operation's change**: posterior confidence in each link of the contribution story from the probative value of the evidence (process tracing in zetesis `tracing`, with tekmerion's channels of credibility for each source).
- **What would have been**: a comparison of linked and unlinked operations on ISR ratings is confounded by selection (participants are chosen for operations in preparation); quasi-experimental designs (peira `quasi`) are possible only where assignment had an arbitrary element (oversubscription, postponement such as the cancelled TOD TDD of 2020), and sensitivity bounds (peira `sensitivity`) should accompany any estimate.
- **The validity of a transfer**: the tolerance within which the mechanism commutes, estimated from the support factors observed in the target and their effect in past transfers.

#### E.7.5 Evaluation

Operators that yield judgments, each with its scale and its judge:

| Operator | Scale of its result | Who holds the standard |
|---|---|---|
| Reaction | ordinal (Likert) | participants |
| Learning (knowledge, skill) | interval if tested by IRT; ordinal if self-rated | the programme, by a declared test |
| Intermediate outcomes (AoKE five; CDRF six) | nominal category × count; ordinal strength | participants and TTLs, coded by two coders with agreement |
| Change in capacity indicators | ordinal per indicator | client agency and task team, by the CDRF's declared indicators |
| Uptake in operations | ordinal ladder (dialogue → design → financed component → indicator moved) | the TTL and the operation's documents |
| Outcome significance | ordinal, by panel | MSC panels; Outcome Harvesting users |
| Contribution | standing (clear, arguable, untenable) and measure (posterior or likelihood ratio) | substantiators; an independent reviewer |
| Value for money | ratio (cost per substantiated outcome); expected value of a study | the donor and the programme |

The needs domain's principle (a need is a gap judged by a standard, with the standard named) applies directly: a "knowledge gap" is a need judged by an operation's standard, and every evaluation names whose standard it is.

#### E.7.6 Policy Making

How a programme chooses, in kybernesis's three loops:

- **Decisions** (within a cohort): which delegations to admit (TDLC selects to maximise impact on existing operations [V]), which sites and speakers, which asks to support with Operational Support. Each is a decision under a belief about the state; the policy domain's `options` and `adoption` value the choice and the worth of learning more first.
- **Policies** (across cohorts): which practices, cities and formats to offer. Variation: alternative formats (a TDD with pre-learning, a TDD followed by a twinning, peer pre-training by former participants as in Colombia [V]). Selection: on effects estimated against what would have been, or, more honestly for most programmes, on substantiated contribution per cost, with the uncertainty carried. Retention: formats kept with their lineage, so that a later cohort can see what each descended from (heuresis's lineage, archeion's journal). Online control of claims across cohorts (tyche's e-LOND) keeps the programme from retaining formats on noise.
- **The framework** (rules of change): the programme's own results framework and its revision, under the donor's rules [V: trust-fund guidance], and the partner cities' asks for co-ownership [V].

Whether a practice is judged transferable is a policy decision on an argument: the practice's mechanism stated as a theory of change, its support factors listed (§5.6), each observed or not in the target with its evidence, the transfer's standing under the readings of "transferable", and, where an experiment exists, the transport formula checked.

#### E.7.7 Actions: The Repertoire

Each action acts on a part of the state through a mechanism. The repertoire, with what would show it worked:

1. **Study tours and technical deep dives.** Mechanism: seeing a practice working, peer conversation and a deadline to plan raise awareness and consensus and seed action plans. States: AoKE new knowledge, consensus; CDRF commitment of leaders. Evidence: action-plan items later found in an operation's design or a client decision, substantiated. Risks: policy tourism [V: González 2011]; the curated model mistaken for the mechanism [V: Sorensen 2000, content S]; selection of senior travellers who do not implement. Examples: TDLC's TDDs [V]; JICA's land readjustment courses [V]. Support factors: delegates with authority over the operation; the TTL in the delegation; follow-up funded in advance.
2. **Peer exchange** (shift-and-share, peer reviews). Mechanism: comparison with peers facing the same problem; relational learning. States: connectivity, consensus. Evidence: continued contact, joint work. Risks: talk without action. Example: TDLC's shift-and-share [V].
3. **Twinning and city-to-city cooperation.** Mechanism: a sustained relationship in which a source city's staff adapt a practice with a local partner. States: skills, organisational arrangements. Evidence: practice in operation after the partnership; outcome statements. Risks: dependence on the source city's budget and enthusiasm; reciprocity lacking. Examples: Kitakyushu–Surabaya [V: Kurniawan et al. 2013]; Yokohama–Penang [V record; content S]; Fukuoka–Ahmedabad [V]. Support factors: commitment of both cities, a local intermediary, concrete results early [S].
4. **Participatory workshops.** Mechanism: a structured method brings citizens and officials to shared proposals. States: stakeholder voice, consensus. Evidence: proposals taken into plans; consultation records under ESS10 [V]. Example: Yokohama's design sketchbook in Panama City and Barranquilla [V].
5. **Communities of practice.** Mechanism: sustained mutual engagement around a practice [V: Wenger 1998, DOI 10.1017/CBO9780511803932; content M]. States: connectivity, skills. Evidence: activity, problems solved, network measures. Risks: decay without a convenor.
6. **Secondments and expert dispatch.** Mechanism: tacit knowledge carried by a practitioner into the client's work. States: skills; organisational arrangements. Evidence: mission reports; changes in procedures. Examples: Japanese experts deployed by the DRM Hub [V] and by TDLC's Operational Support [V]; JICA long-term experts in Thailand [V].
7. **Advisory missions and just-in-time technical assistance.** Mechanism: an answer at the moment of a design choice. States: the operation's design. Evidence: the choice recorded in a PAD or aide-mémoire. Example: TDLC Operational Support, 104 projects in Phase 3 [V].
8. **Toolkits, knowledge notes and case studies.** Mechanism: codified knowledge for many readers. States: awareness. Evidence: citations in operations (IEG found such references rare for ASA [S]). Risks: codified form without mechanism. Examples: IUFRM knowledge notes [V]; the Yokohama property-tax note [S].
9. **Action plans with follow-up.** Mechanism: commitment, a list of asks, and a schedule of support. States: implemented strategy/plan (CDRF). Evidence: items implemented over time, with censoring. Example: TDLC's action plans with asks [V].
10. **Training with follow-up and trainers of trainers.** Mechanism: trainees return to posts where they can act, are followed by an expert, and train the next cohort. States: skills, coalitions, policy. Evidence: former trainees in decisive posts; projects carried out. Example: Colombia's land readjustment programme, 1998–2008 [V]. Support factors: a legal reform creating demand (Law 388) [V].
11. **Grants for pilots.** Mechanism: a demonstration that makes the practice visible and lets it be systematised. States: implemented plans; evidence for later scale-up. Evidence: pilots completed, evaluated and copied. Risks: pilots not recorded or evaluated, so nothing spreads [V: BOSAI ex-post evaluation]. Examples: Thailand's ten land readjustment pilots with a fund [V]; the Japan–World Bank DRM grants [V].

#### E.7.8 Results Capture

Results capture closes the loop and feeds selection. The documented practices are: TDLC's feedback from project teams and its stories of uptake [V]; AoKE's step 5 (synthesise data, measure and report intermediate outcomes) [V]; the CDRF's step 10 (assess learning outcomes and capacity indicators, specify follow-up) [V]; Outcome Harvesting's substantiated outcome statements [V]; MSC's selected stories with reasons [V]; and the trustee-level results framework [V]. What is missing in public (§2.6) and in the Bank generally (§3.7) is a record that ties each claimed result to its evidence, its readings and its standing. A sealed journal (archeion) of outcome statements, substantiations and contribution claims, written as W3C PROV, would make results capture auditable and would let the policy loop select formats on what was substantiated rather than on what was linked.

---

### E.8. Mapping to kybernesis and What a New Module Needs

**Already in the infrastructure.** The policy domain (`change` for theories of change with the weakest link, `evidence` for pooling and grading, `transfer` for Cartwright and Hardie, `options` and `adoption` for adopt, reject or study first by the value of information); tekmerion (`reviews` with gap maps, `warrant`, `construal` for readings of open terms, Schum's channels of credibility, dependent reports counted once, panels); zetesis (`coding` with codebooks and agreement of coders, `qca`, `cases` for selection, `tracing` with Van Evera's tests); peira (`surveys`, `survival`, `synthesis` with transport on selection diagrams, `quasi`, `sensitivity`, `protocol` for plans and deviations, `small_areas`); tyche (`latent`, `mixed`, `networks`, `testing` with e-LOND, `decision`); nomos (`psychometrics`, agreement of coders in `measurement`, `appraisal` with Delphi and multi-criteria analysis); the needs domain (a gap judged by a standard; search by evolution); heuresis (`evolution` with lineage); archeion (sealed journal, PROV provenance, datasets with codebooks); kategoria (schemas stated as categories).

**What a new module would need** (its placement and name are the user's decision; the policy domain's planned `programmes` and `monitoring` rows are candidates):

1. A **schema of a knowledge programme**: programme, cohort, delegation, participant, operation (with PDO and indicators), action plan, ask, support request, follow-up, outcome statement, substantiation, contribution claim, practice, source city, target city.
2. **Codebooks as data**: the CDRF's six learning outcomes and three capacity factors with their indicators; AoKE's five intermediate outcomes; Kirkpatrick's four levels as an ordinal scale; IEG's ladder of uptake. Declared, not hard-coded.
3. **Outcome statements** in Outcome Harvesting's form (outcome, significance, contribution), with substantiation as reports through tekmerion's channels.
4. **Contribution claims** to an operation's indicator, built as a process-tracing argument with a standing and a measure.
5. A **register of practices**: each a mechanism stated as a theory of change (CMO), with its support factors, evidence and the transfers on record (successes, partial successes and non-adoptions such as Malaysia's).
6. **Transfer appraisal as a lens**: *f* (the target read in the source's terms: which support factors hold), *g* (the source's actions realised in the target), and a tolerance, calling the policy domain's `transfer` and peira's transport.
7. **Selection of practices, cities and formats** as the policy loop: variation, selection on substantiated contribution per cost with uncertainty, retention with lineage.

---

### E.9. Tables

#### Table 1. Methods and Instruments

| Method or instrument | What it captures | Evidence it needs | Scale type of its result | kybernesis block | In the infrastructure |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CDRF needs assessment (stage 1) | capacity factors bearing on a goal | interviews, documents, indicators | ordinal per indicator | collection; estimation | new codebook; tyche `latent` |
| CDRF learning outcomes | six agent-level changes | surveys, products, follow-up | nominal × count; ordinal | evaluation | new codebook; zetesis `coding` |
| AoKE intermediate outcomes | five changes from exchange | baseline/follow-up, outcome statements | nominal × count | evaluation | new codebook |
| Kirkpatrick levels | reaction, learning, behaviour, results | surveys, tests, observation | ordinal (levels); interval (tests) | evaluation | nomos `psychometrics` |
| Success Case Method | what enabled or blocked use | interviews with extreme cases | qualitative; case set | evidence collection; interpretation | zetesis `cases`, `coding` |
| Outcome Harvesting | substantiated outcomes and contributions | documents, change agents, substantiators | nominal outcomes; contribution standing | evidence; evaluation | tekmerion channels; new statement type |
| Most Significant Change | significant change by declared values | stories; panel selections with reasons | ordinal (selection) | evaluation (standards held by someone) | tekmerion panels; zetesis `coding` |
| Contribution analysis | plausibility of a contribution story | theory of change, evidence per link, rivals | standing + confidence | evaluation; interpretation | policy `change`; zetesis `tracing` |
| Process tracing | probative value of evidence for a mechanism | within-case observations | posterior odds | estimation | zetesis `tracing` |
| Realist evaluation / synthesis | CMO configurations | mixed evidence, programme theory | qualitative configurations | interpretation; transfer | zetesis `qca`, `sets`; new CMO register |
| PDIA | problem-driven iteration | problem constructions, iteration logs | process record | policy making (variation) | heuresis `evolution` (by analogy) |
| Results framework (PDO, indicators) | intended outcomes and their measures | M&E plan, baselines | ratio/ordinal per indicator | evaluation; policy | policy `change` |
| ISR ratings | an operation's progress | supervision documents | ordinal | estimation (state of operation) | new reader |
| Training needs analysis | gaps at organisation, task, person | job analysis, surveys | nominal/ordinal | collection | needs domain |
| ESS10 consultation and grievance logs | stakeholder views and complaints | records of consultation, grievances | nominal × count; time | evidence | archeion holdings |
| Dolowitz–Marsh / Rose analysis | what is transferred and why it fails | case documents | categories | interpretation; transfer | new |
| Cartwright–Hardie support factors | will it work here | mechanism, factors in target | standing | transfer | policy `transfer` |
| Transportability | effect in target from source | selection diagram, experiments | estimate or refusal | transfer; estimation | peira `synthesis` (transport) |
| GRADE-CERQual / TRANSFER | confidence and transferability of findings | qualitative synthesis | ordinal grades | evaluation of evidence | tekmerion `warrant`, `reviews` |
| Evidence gap map | where evidence exists | studies coded | counts by cell | evidence | tekmerion `reviews` |
| QCA | conditions sufficient/necessary across cases | coded cases | Boolean solutions | estimation; synthesis | zetesis `qca` |
