# Chapter F. Methods of Estimating and Evaluating Risk

## Part F. Methods of Estimating and Evaluating Risk

*Status: draft. Part of a survey prepared for tools serving the remit of an Urban Analyst in disaster risk management (risk assessment and mapping, risk reduction, preparedness, risk financing), built on kybernesis. Disaster-risk frameworks and indicator systems (Sendai, the UNDRR scorecard, the City Resilience Index, Lifelines, Unbreakable as a framework, PDNA, Japanese practice) belong to another part and are not repeated here. This part treats the methods by which risk is estimated and evaluated, as a methodologist reads them.*

*Markers.* **[V]** verified in this session against a primary record (a publisher's or issuing body's page, a DOI record, an institutional repository, an arXiv record); **[S]** confirmed only from secondary sources; **[M]** from memory, not verified. DOIs and URLs are given for [V] items; the full list is in §13.

---

### F.1. Risk as a Quantity and as a Judgement

#### F.1.1 The Two Senses of Risk

The word carries two things that kybernesis keeps apart. The first is a *quantity*: a functional of the distribution of loss that a system's state, under the hazards it is exposed to, induces over a period. The annual average loss, the loss at a return period, the probability that a hospital cannot be reached, the expected well-being loss of the poorest fifth are all such functionals. The second is a *judgement*: the claim that a quantity is too high, measured against a standard held by someone (a design code, a tolerance, a budget, a promise in a plan). The first belongs to the block *Estimation* and to the quantitative half of *Evaluation*; the second to *Evaluation*'s comparison with standards, and its standing (clear, arguable, untenable) depends on the readings of open terms such as "acceptable", "critical" or "the 1-in-100-year flood".

The classical decomposition, risk as a convolution of **hazard** (how often an intensity is exceeded at a place), **exposure** (what stands there and with what value) and **vulnerability** (the loss an intensity causes to what stands), is the common structure of every method below. It is an assumption, not an identity: it presumes that the three factor, that losses at different places are summed after the intensity field is known, and that vulnerability is a function of a scalar intensity measure. Each method relaxes part of it.

#### F.1.2 Soundness of a Belief about Risk

kybernesis admits an estimator to *Estimation* when it is **sound**: its belief never excludes the true state. For risk this has a precise reading. A belief is a set of admissible states, or a law over states whose support contains every state the evidence does not exclude, or an interval whose coverage is stated and checked. Three consequences follow, and they recur through this part.

1. **Point estimates are unsound.** An AAL computed with one vulnerability function, one ground-motion model and one exposure dataset excludes, by construction, every state in which another admissible choice is right. It is a face-value estimate, refused by kybernesis for the same reason as its face-value filter.
2. **Epistemic uncertainty is a set before it is a law.** Der Kiureghian and Ditlevsen (2009 [V]) define epistemic uncertainty as what the modeller believes reducible by data or better models, aleatory as what is not, and show that epistemic uncertainty induces dependence among events that is lost if it is modelled as if it were aleatory. A sound belief keeps the set of admissible models (a credal set) and offers a law over it only as a further, declared reading.
3. **Coverage must be tested.** A 90 % interval is sound in kybernesis's sense only to the extent that back-testing (§10.3) shows it covering the truth at about that rate; tyche's conformal intervals and its withholding of estimates outside their area of applicability already carry this discipline for spatial estimates.

---

### F.2. Probabilistic Risk Assessment of Cities

#### F.2.1 The Convolution and Its Parts

Probabilistic seismic hazard analysis, founded by Cornell (1968 [V]), gives the archetype. A source model (where and how often events of each magnitude occur) and a ground-motion model (the law of an intensity measure IM at a site given an event) yield, by the total probability theorem, a **hazard curve**: the annual rate λ(IM > x) at each site. A **fragility function** gives the probability of reaching or exceeding a damage state given IM, conventionally lognormal in IM; Porter, Kennedy and Bachman (2007 [V]) set out how to fit fragility functions from failure data, bounding data, analysis, expert opinion and by updating, and Baker (2015 [V]) shows how to fit them efficiently from dynamic structural analyses (multiple-stripe analysis is more efficient than incremental dynamic analysis for a fixed number of runs). A **damage-to-loss** model turns damage states into cost, casualties or downtime; the performance-based framework of FEMA P-58 composes hazard, response, damage and loss in this way (FEMA P-58, 2012 [M]).

In a catastrophe model the hazard is represented not by curves but by an **event set**: a stochastic catalogue of events, each with a rate and an intensity footprint. Applying vulnerability to each footprint gives an **event loss table**; from it come the **exceedance probability (EP) curve** (occurrence EP for the largest event of a year, aggregate EP for the year's sum), the **average annual loss** AAL = ∫ EP(ℓ) dℓ, the **probable maximum loss** (PML: by convention the loss at a stated return period, a reading that must be declared), and tail measures such as the tail value at risk. A practitioner's account of the whole chain, from event sets to the uses of EP curves in insurance and public policy, is Mitchell-Wallace, Jones, Hillier and Foote (2017 [V]).

The infrastructure already holds the last stage: tyche's `descriptive` computes annual losses and exceedance curves from event losses, and layers of a loss per year or per event from a record or a published curve; the Freetown work recomputed a published loss table from its 1,943 events. What it lacks is the first stages: an event set or hazard curves, and vulnerability as fitted fragility.

#### F.2.2 Open Engines and Data Standards

- **OpenQuake** (Pagani et al. 2014 [V]), the Global Earthquake Model's engine, computes hazard (classical PSHA, event-based, scenario) and risk (scenario damage and loss, event-based risk, classical risk); its risk side is described by Silva, Crowley, Pagani, Monelli and Pinho (2014 [S]). GEM's global seismic risk model (Silva et al. 2020 [V]) runs hazard models, a global exposure dataset and fragility and vulnerability functions through it to give average annual losses and losses at return periods. Its licence (AGPL v3 for the source) matters for any coupling; the infrastructure's rule is to own the interfaces and borrow implementations only where licences permit.
- **Oasis Loss Modelling Framework** is a not-for-profit, model-agnostic platform for running catastrophe models packaged in a standard form (hazard footprints, vulnerability, financial terms), released under a BSD 3-clause licence in 2018 (Oasis LMF [S]).
- **CAPRA**, developed with World Bank and Inter-American Development Bank support, computes EP curves, AAL and PML for earthquake, hurricane, flood, landslide and volcanic hazards (Cardona et al. 2012 [S]); its application to Barcelona is Marulanda et al. (2013 [S]).
- **Hazus**, FEMA's standardised methodology, now maintains earthquake, flood, hurricane and tsunami models with analysis levels from national default inventories (Level 1) to local inventories and hazard (Levels 2–3) (FEMA Hazus [V]).
- **The Risk Data Library Standard** (GFDRR) describes datasets of hazard, exposure, vulnerability and loss by metadata and packaging; it does not standardise their contents (RDLS [V]). It is the natural exchange format for a work that must read others' risk data and say where each came from.
- **J-SHIS** (NIED) publishes Japan's national probabilistic seismic hazard maps on a 250 m mesh, with an API returning, for a mesh cell, the hazard curve (exceedance probability over 30 or 50 years against seismic intensity), for each model version and for an average (AVR) or maximum (MAX) case of the source model (J-SHIS [V]). For Tokyo the hazard curve is therefore an input to be read, not a quantity to be built, and the AVR/MAX choice is itself an open term with two declared readings.

#### F.2.3 Uncertainty: Logic Trees and What They Are Not

Epistemic uncertainty in hazard is conventionally carried by a **logic tree**: alternative source and ground-motion models on branches, weighted by experts. Bommer and Scherbaum (2008 [V]) show the use and misuse of logic trees: weights are degrees of belief, not frequencies of being right, and the branches are rarely mutually exclusive and collectively exhaustive. The mean hazard curve over a tree is a weighted average of curves none of which need be true. In kybernesis's terms the sound belief is the *set* of branch curves (or the band between declared fractiles with the tree's incompleteness stated), and the mean is a further reading taken in Evaluation, never the belief itself. The same holds for vulnerability: the spread over admissible fragility or depth–damage functions (§6.2) is often larger than the spread over hazard.

A second epistemic gap is **incompleteness of the event set**: a short catalogue may lack the extremes that matter. Woo (2019 [V]) proposes *downward counterfactual* search, asking how recorded events could have turned out worse, as a disciplined way to add plausible extremes without overfitting to history.

#### F.2.4 The Return Period as an Open Term

"The 1-in-100-year event" has at least three readings: an annual exceedance probability of 0.01; a mean recurrence interval of 100 years under stationarity; and, for a structure of design life *n*, the probability 1 − 0.99ⁿ of at least one exceedance. Under non-stationarity these part. Milly et al. (2008 [V]) argue that stationarity can no longer be the default assumption of water-resource risk assessment; Salas and Obeysekera (2014 [V]) extend return period and risk to non-stationary extremes through a geometric law with changing exceedance probabilities and show the estimates can differ noticeably from stationary ones. A work must therefore declare which reading of a return period it uses, with its source (a code, a hazard map, an analyst), exactly as tekmerion's `construal` reads any open term.

Extreme-value methods (the generalised extreme-value and Pareto laws fitted by maximum or profile likelihood) give return levels with intervals (Coles 2001 [M]); the infrastructure does not yet hold them, and they belong in tyche.

#### F.2.5 Validation against Losses

Validation is the weakest link of the chain. Molinari et al. (2019 [V]), from workshops of more than fifty experts, find validation of flood risk models often neglected and call for better data, benchmark solutions and end-user involvement. Jongman et al. (2012 [V]) compared seven flood damage models on events in Germany and the United Kingdom and found outputs most sensitive to the vulnerability component, the depth–damage function outweighing asset values. Wagenaar et al. (2016 [V]), by Monte Carlo over a library of 272 damage functions from seven models, find damage estimates uncertain by a factor of about 2 to 5, greatest for shallow floods and small events, enough to shift dike investments between over- and under-investment. Trigg et al. (2016 [V]) compared six global flood hazard models over Africa: they agree on flood extent only some 30–40 % of the time. A sound belief about loss in a data-scarce city must therefore be wide, and a method that reports one number from one model chain is not admissible to *Estimation*.

---

### F.3. Multi-Hazard, Compound and Cascading Risk

#### F.3.1 Interacting Hazards

Gill and Malamud (2014 [V]) review 21 natural hazards in six groups and identify 90 interactions in which one hazard triggers another or raises its probability; Kappes et al. (2012 [V]) set out the difficulties at each step of multi-hazard risk analysis: incomparable intensity measures, vulnerability to successive processes, and the ranking of threats. The methodological core is that a multi-hazard risk is not the sum of single-hazard risks: triggering, shared drivers and changes of vulnerability between events (a building weakened by a first earthquake) all enter.

#### F.3.2 Compound Events and Copulas

Zscheischler et al. (2018 [V]) argue that methods considering one driver at a time can underestimate risk from compound events; their typology (Zscheischler et al. 2020 [V]) distinguishes preconditioned, multivariate, temporally compounding and spatially compounding events, with soft boundaries between them. The statistical tool is the **copula**, which separates the marginal laws of drivers from their dependence. Salvadori et al. (2016 [V]) give a copula-based framework for multivariate hazard scenarios and failure probabilities. Wahl et al. (2015 [V]) found the joint occurrence of storm surge and heavy rainfall higher on the Atlantic and Gulf coasts of the United States than on the Pacific, and rising at many major cities over the last century.

For kybernesis the decisive point is that a **joint return period has several readings** (the "OR" event that either driver exceeds, the "AND" event that both do, the Kendall level set of the copula), which can differ by large factors for the same design: an open term to be read under each. The choice of copula family, and above all its tail dependence, is epistemic; a sound belief keeps admissible families as a set. The infrastructure has no copulas; they belong in tyche.

#### F.3.3 Interdependent Infrastructure and Cascades

Rinaldi, Peerenboom and Kelly (2001 [V]) distinguish dependencies and interdependencies among infrastructures and treat infrastructures as complex adaptive systems; their kinds of interdependency (physical, cyber, geographic, logical) remain the usual vocabulary. Ouyang (2014 [V]) groups the methods into empirical, agent-based, system dynamics, economic, network and resilience-based approaches. Four families matter here.

1. **Percolation on networks.** Callaway et al. (2000 [V]) give exact percolation thresholds for random graphs of arbitrary degree distribution; Albert, Jeong and Barabási (2000 [V]) show that scale-free networks tolerate random failure but break under attack on hubs. Buldyrev et al. (2010 [V]) show that two interdependent networks can fragment abruptly after the failure of a small fraction of nodes, and that a broader degree distribution makes interdependent networks more vulnerable, the opposite of a single network. *Caveat.* These results hold for ensembles of random graphs; urban lifelines are spatially embedded, nearly planar and engineered with redundancy, and their thresholds must be computed on the network itself, not borrowed from theory.
2. **Flow redistribution.** Motter and Lai (2002 [V]) model cascades in which the load of a failed node moves to others, which fail when their capacity is exceeded; capacity margins become the parameter that decides whether a cascade is local or global.
3. **Input–output inoperability.** Haimes and Jiang (2001 [V]) adapt Leontief's model to the propagation of inoperability among infrastructures; Santos and Haimes (2004 [V]) model demand reductions after an attack and rank sectors by the losses they suffer. The model is linear, static and blind to redundancy.
4. **Adaptive regional input–output.** Hallegatte (2008 [V]) adds production capacity, forward and backward propagation and adaptive behaviour; for Katrina it estimates total costs of $149 billion against $107 billion of direct losses, the construction sector's capacity to over-produce and the time scale of adaptation being the most influential parameters.

The infrastructure holds the substrate of the first three: morphe's flows in networks (greatest flow and least cut, flows over time), urban risk management's `lifelines` (every link's closure answered at once by the forest of shortest paths, redundancy by link-disjoint paths), civil engineering's water criticality, physis's Kirchhoff networks and economics' Leontief model in `production`. What it lacks is **coupling across networks** (a failure of power that stops pumping, a road closure that stops repair crews) and the adaptive input–output model.

#### F.3.4 Systemic Risk

The Global Assessment Report 2019 gave a chapter (its second, "Systemic risks, the Sendai Framework and the 2030 Agenda") to systemic risk (UNDRR 2019 [S]); UNDRR's commentary describes systemic risks as emergent and not necessarily visible to approaches that add hazard to hazard. The exact wording of its definition could not be verified this session and is not quoted here. Methodologically, systemic risk names what the families of §3.3 compute when the system's own structure (couplings, shared dependencies, feedbacks) generates or amplifies loss. For kybernesis it is not a separate quantity but a demand on *Estimation*: the state must include the couplings, and the belief about them must be sound.

---

### F.4. Criticality and Resilience of Dynamical Systems

#### F.4.1 Two Meanings of Resilience

Holling (1973 [V]) distinguished the stability of an equilibrium (return after small disturbance) from **resilience**, the capacity of a system to absorb change and persist, measured by the size of the disturbance it can take before moving to another regime. Pimm (1984 [V]) reviewed the components of stability, among them the rate of return to equilibrium after a perturbation; this "engineering" resilience is the dominant eigenvalue of the linearisation. The two can disagree completely: a state may return quickly from small shocks and lie near the edge of its basin. **Basin stability** (Menck, Heitzig, Marwan and Kurths 2013 [V]) measures the second by the probability that a random perturbation of declared law returns the system to the state, a non-local measure that remains computable in many dimensions; their application showed why more regular power grids synchronise more robustly. kinesis already computes basins with each end named, equilibria, Lyapunov spectra, landscapes and Kramers's escape time, so basin stability is a short addition there, given a model.

#### F.4.2 Critical Transitions and Their Early Warnings

Scheffer et al. (2009 [V]; 2012 [V]) review evidence that, near a fold bifurcation, **critical slowing down** raises variance and lag-1 autocorrelation and can change skewness; Dakos et al. (2012 [V]) give the methods (rolling-window variance and autocorrelation, spectral reddening, detrended fluctuation analysis, model-based indicators) with their choices of window, detrending and significance. **Flickering**, switching between alternative states before a transition, raised variance while autocorrelation and skewness fell 10–30 years before the eutrophication of Lake Erhai (Wang et al. 2012 [V]).

Their failure modes are as well documented as their successes.

- **Transitions without a bifurcation.** Ashwin et al. (2012 [V]) distinguish bifurcation-induced, noise-induced and rate-induced tipping; only the first is announced by slowing down. Ditlevsen and Johnsen (2010 [V]) found no early warning before the Dansgaard–Oeschger events and argue that variance and autocorrelation must rise together to signal a bifurcation. Hastings and Wysham (2010 [V]) show regime shifts in ecological models that occur with no warning.
- **False alarms and the limits of detection.** Boettiger and Hastings (2012 [V]) show that indicators' performance varies widely between datasets and argue for model-based uncertainty quantification.
- **Empirical record.** Burthe et al. (2016 [V]), on 126 long ecological series of 55 taxa, found true positives in only 9 % of cases by variance and 13 % by autocorrelation.
- **Learned indicators.** Bury et al. (2021 [V]) train a deep network on a universe of models and report higher sensitivity and specificity than generic indicators on 268 series, with a prediction of the bifurcation's type.

kinesis's "signs of criticality" (slowing down, fluctuation analysis, avalanches, branching ratio) implement the generic indicators. In kybernesis their honest role is not *Estimation* (they do not give a sound belief that a transition is near) but a trigger for *Inquiry*: a rising indicator is evidence that warrants a closer look, with its false-alarm rate stated.

#### F.4.3 Resilience Curves and the Resilience Triangle

Bruneau et al. (2003 [V]) define community seismic resilience through reduced failure probabilities, reduced consequences and reduced time to recovery, in technical, organisational, social and economic dimensions; the loss of quality of a system Q(t) integrated over the recovery period (the "resilience triangle") is its measure, and the four properties robustness, redundancy, resourcefulness and rapidity are recalled from the paper (detail [M]). Cimellaro, Reinhorn and Bruneau (2010 [V]) make resilience a dimensionless functional of the functionality curve, including losses and the recovery path, propagating all uncertainties through fragilities. Hosseini, Barker and Ramirez-Marquez (2016 [V]) review definitions and measures across domains and find many overlapping with robustness, fault tolerance and survivability.

The open terms are concrete: the baseline Q(t₀) (functionality before the event, or a target), the **control time** over which the area is integrated, the functionality measure itself (customers served, trips completed, beds available), and what counts as "recovered". The result is on a ratio scale only once all four are declared.

#### F.4.4 Thresholds, Heavy Tails and Self-Organised Criticality

Bak, Tang and Wiesenfeld (1987 [V]) proposed that some extended systems evolve to a critical state with power-law avalanches. Heavy tails in disaster losses are often attributed to such mechanisms, but the evidence must first establish the tail. Clauset, Shalizi and Newman (2009 [V]) give the method: maximum-likelihood fitting above an estimated lower bound, a goodness-of-fit test, and likelihood-ratio comparison with alternatives (lognormal, stretched exponential); many claimed power laws fail it. With the few decades of loss data a city has, the tail exponent is rarely identified, and a sound belief must keep heavy-tailed and lighter-tailed laws both admissible.

#### F.4.5 What Is Usable on Urban Data

| Method | Usable on urban data when | Evidence needed |
|---|---|---|
| Basin stability | a model of the system's dynamics is declared (power-flow, water network, a market) | the model, a law of perturbations, its parameters' ranges |
| Linear return time | the system is near an equilibrium and small shocks are of interest | a model or a long series around equilibrium |
| Early-warning indicators | long, regularly sampled series under slow forcing (water pressure, grid frequency, traffic speeds) | the series, the forcing's time scale, a null model for false alarms |
| Resilience curve | functionality is recorded through an event and its recovery | outage or service records with timestamps |
| Percolation thresholds | computed on the actual network, never borrowed from random-graph theory | the network with capacities and dependencies |
| Heavy-tail fits | at least several hundred losses or a declared regional pool | a loss record with its reporting threshold |

---

### F.5. Risk under Deep Uncertainty

Where probabilities of futures cannot be agreed (climate, land use, policy, behaviour), a family of methods replaces the expected loss by robustness across a set of futures. Marchau, Walker, Bloemen and Popper (2019 [V], open access) gather them.

- **Robust decision making** (Lempert, Popper and Bankes 2003 [V]) runs a model over a large ensemble of plausible futures and seeks near-term, often adaptive, strategies that perform acceptably across them, usually judged by regret or a satisficing threshold.
- **Scenario discovery** (Bryant and Lempert 2010 [V]) mines the ensemble, typically by the patient rule induction method, for simple boxes in the space of uncertain inputs where a strategy fails; the boxes are the scenarios, found rather than assumed. The EMA Workbench (Kwakkel 2017 [V]) is an open Python toolkit for exploratory modelling, scenario discovery and multi-objective robust decision making.
- **Dynamic adaptive policy pathways** (Haasnoot, Kwakkel, Walker and ter Maat 2013 [V]) sequence actions with their adaptation tipping points (the conditions under which an action ceases to meet its objective), so that a plan becomes a graph of pathways with signposts and triggers, demonstrated on the Rhine Delta.
- **Info-gap decision theory** (Ben-Haim 2006 [V]) ranks options by the largest horizon of uncertainty around a nominal estimate within which a requirement is still met. Sniedovich (2010 [V]) argues that this robustness is a local radius of stability and an instance of Wald's maximin, unsuited to severe uncertainty when the nominal estimate is poor; the critique bears directly on soundness, since a horizon around a wrong estimate may exclude the truth.
- **Decision scaling** (Brown, Ghile, Laverty and Li 2012 [V]) reverses the impact chain: first find, by stress tests, the climate conditions under which a decision changes, then ask how plausible those conditions are.

*How risk is estimated here.* The belief is a set of futures (or a credal set of laws), sound when it contains the true future; the evaluation is a robustness or regret operator on that set, on an ordinal scale for ranking strategies (regret itself is on a ratio scale in the loss's units). tyche already holds credal sets with Γ-maximin, α-maxmin, maximality, E-admissibility and minimax regret, and heuresis can search a strategy space under many objectives; missing are scenario discovery (PRIM), pathway graphs with tipping points, and info-gap robustness, which could be stated with Sniedovich's caveat as a declared reading.

---

### F.6. Data-Scarce Estimation

#### F.6.1 Exposure from Remote Sensing

Building footprints and heights are now estimated where they are not surveyed. Google's Open Buildings detected buildings across Africa from 50 cm imagery with a U-Net pipeline trained on 1.75 million labelled instances (Sirko et al. 2021 [V]; the abstract gives 516 million footprints, later releases more). GHS-BUILT-H R2023A gives average net building height on a 100 m grid for 2018 from ALOS and SRTM surface models and a Sentinel-2 composite (Pesaresi and Politis 2023 [V]). The infrastructure's Urban Science Workbench already reads Overture's buildings and falls back to GHS-BUILT-H, drawing estimated heights paler; the methodological addition is to carry the estimate's error into exposure value and floor area, and to withhold estimates outside the area where the height model was validated (tyche's area of applicability).

#### F.6.2 Vulnerability Transferred between Regions

Huizinga, de Moel and Szewczyk (2017 [V]) give global depth–damage functions: concave curves for each continent from a literature survey, with country maxima from regressions on development indicators, intended for countries with no damage model. Their convenience hides the danger shown in §2.5: vulnerability dominates loss uncertainty (Jongman et al. 2012 [V]) and admissible damage functions spread estimates by a factor of 2 to 5 (Wagenaar et al. 2016 [V]). The sound practice is to keep the library of admissible functions as a credal set, weight it only by declared evidence of similarity in construction, and widen the belief accordingly.

#### F.6.3 Bayesian Updating of Fragility

Singhal and Kiremidjian (1998 [V]) revise analytical fragility curves by Bayesian updating with observed damage (reinforced concrete frames in the 1994 Northridge earthquake), giving confidence bounds; Porter et al. (2007 [V]) include updating among their methods. The structure is a binomial or ordinal model of damage counts given IM, with a prior from analysis or transferred curves; tyche's generalised linear models and `bayes` already fit it, and the domain module that would name building classes and damage states is missing. Its soundness depends on the IM at each damaged building being estimated with its error (§8.1), not taken at face value.

#### F.6.4 Crowdsourced Intensity and Damage

USGS "Did You Feel It?" turns internet reports into macroseismic intensity maps within minutes (Wald et al. 2011 [V]); xBD provides some 850,000 building annotations with ordinal damage labels from pre- and post-event imagery (Gupta et al. 2019 [V]), with a strongly imbalanced label distribution. PAGER's empirical fatality model expresses the fatality rate as a lognormal function of shaking intensity with country or regional parameters fitted to deaths from 1973 to 2007 (Jaiswal and Wald 2010 [V]). Each is a channel of evidence with its own reporting process: self-selection in crowdsourced reports, misclassification in labels learned from imagery. tekmerion's channels of credibility and peira's imperfect-detection models are the right frame for them.

---

### F.7. Social Vulnerability and Well-Being Risk

#### F.7.1 Indices of Social Vulnerability

Cutter, Boruff and Shirley (2003 [V]) reduced 42 county variables by factor analysis to 11 factors explaining about 76 % of variance and summed them into the Social Vulnerability Index (SoVI); the CDC/ATSDR index of Flanagan et al. (2011 [V]) used 15 tract variables (now 16 in four themes). Their critiques concern construct validity and the arbitrariness of construction:

- Tate (2012 [V]), by uncertainty and sensitivity analysis over construction choices, shows rankings depend on them and identifies the influential stages;
- Rufat, Tate, Emrich and Antolini (2019 [V]), regressing Hurricane Sandy outcomes on four indices with flood exposure controlled, find they explain applications for assistance well and property loss poorly, and that weighted and profile configurations do better than the prevailing indices;
- Spielman et al. (2020 [V]) find SoVI internally inconsistent (repeated measurements of one place from the same data differ strikingly) and theoretically inconsistent (a rising unemployment rate often *lowers* measured vulnerability), and caution against its use in policy.

*Scale type.* A sum of standardised factor scores is at best ordinal across places within one computation; comparing scores across computations or years is not meaningful. kybernesis's `kernel` (scales and meaningfulness) should refuse an operator that treats such an index as interval-scaled. The infrastructure holds the tools for doing better: nomos's composite indicators with weight stability, nomos's psychometrics (classical test theory, item response), tyche's factor and latent class analysis, zetesis's concept structures (Goertz), and peira's small-area estimation.

#### F.7.2 Asset Losses and Well-Being Losses

Hallegatte, Bangalore and Vogt-Schilb (2016 [V]) model socioeconomic resilience as the ratio of asset losses to well-being losses: welfare losses from Mumbai's 2005 floods were almost double the asset losses, and across 90 countries a dollar of flood asset loss is equivalent on average to $1.6 of lost national income. Walsh and Hallegatte (2020 [V]) model household recovery in the Philippines: the poorest fifth bear 9 % of asset losses and 31 % of well-being losses, and almost half a million people a year face transient consumption poverty from disasters; priorities among regions change with the metric. Markhvida, Walsh, Hallegatte and Baker (2020 [V]) chain hazard, damage, household recovery and consumption for a Hayward fault scenario: poorer households bear 19 % of asset losses and 41 % of well-being losses.

The open terms are the utility function (the elasticity of marginal utility), the discount rate, the reconstruction rate and its dependence on savings, insurance and transfers, and the equivalence between well-being and consumption. The result is on a ratio scale in equivalent consumption only under those readings, and its ranking of interventions can turn on them, which tekmerion's `construal` would report as pivots. The household model belongs to social provision or risk management as a new module; demography's life tables and social provision's `households` supply parts.

---

### F.8. State Better Estimated than Observed

Much of the state that risk depends on is never observed directly, or is observed sparsely, late or with error. For each, the sound course is an estimate conditioned on what is observed, with its error.

#### F.8.1 Hazard Intensity at a Place

After an earthquake, intensity measures are recorded at a few stations. Worden et al. (2018 [V]) condition a ground-motion model's multivariate normal law on uncertain observations across space and across spectral periods, which is how ShakeMap estimates shaking everywhere; it is Gaussian-process conditioning, which tyche's `spatial` already performs one observation at a time. Before an event the intensity is a hazard curve (J-SHIS for Japan, §2.2); for floods, a hydraulic model's depth conditioned on gauges and flood marks. *Evidence:* station records with their site conditions, a ground-motion or hydraulic model, a correlation model, macroseismic or crowdsourced reports as a second channel (§6.4).

#### F.8.2 Building Vulnerability

A building's fragility is estimated from its class (structure, material, height, age, code era), itself estimated from footprints, heights, census age bands, rapid visual screening (FEMA P-154 [M]) and permits, then updated by observed damage (§6.3). *Evidence:* an inventory with its sampling design (peira's surveys), class-to-fragility mappings with their sources, damage observations with their intensity estimates.

#### F.8.3 Network Functionality

Whether a road, pipe or substation functions during and after an event is observed only in part (outage reports, probe vehicle speeds, pressure loggers). The network model gives functionality from component states, and component states are estimated from fragility and intensity; observations then condition the joint state, a hidden-state problem for which tyche's Bayesian networks and hidden Markov models are suited. *Evidence:* the network with capacities and dependencies, component fragilities, partial service observations with their reporting delays.

#### F.8.4 Behaviour: Departure, Route and Compliance

Evacuation depends most on when people leave. Lindell and Perry's (2012 [V]) Protective Action Decision Model explains protective response by perceptions of threat, of protective actions and of stakeholders, with facilitators and impediments; Huang, Lindell and Prater's (2016 [S]) meta-analysis of hurricane evacuation finds official warnings, residence type and risk area, environmental and social cues, and expected personal impact consistently significant, and expected (hypothetical) responses similar to actual ones for 10 of 17 variables. For Japan, the Cabinet Office's 2011 interviews after the Great East Japan Earthquake classed 57 % of 870 respondents as evacuating immediately, 31 % after an errand and 11 % only when the tsunami was imminent (Cabinet Office 2011 [S]). The infrastructure's Miyako case already uses "leaving as in 2011" as a declared departure law; the estimate of departure is better made as a law with its uncertainty, conditioned on survey and drill data, than taken from a single survey. *Evidence:* post-event surveys, drills (whose departures are earlier than in real events, a known bias [M]), warning logs, mobile-phone presence data.

#### F.8.5 Exposure by Time of Day

Who is where when the hazard arrives is estimated by carrying census night-time population to day-time places (dasymetric allocation under control totals), which tyche's change of support already does; mobile data or counters condition it.

---

### F.9. The Repertoire of Inquiries and Their Worth

#### F.9.1 What Can Be Asked

| Inquiry | Uncertainty it reduces | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Geotechnical and site investigations (boreholes, microtremor, Vs30) | site amplification, liquefaction | spatially local; worth highest where many assets share a site class |
| Building inventory and screening | class and fragility of exposed stock | sampling design decides what can be generalised |
| Household surveys | social vulnerability, insurance, savings, evacuation intentions | stated intentions differ from behaviour; calibrate against events |
| Sensors (strong motion, water level, pressure, structural health monitoring) | intensity, functionality, deterioration | value depends on the decision they inform (§9.2) |
| Drills | departure, walking speed, bottlenecks | biased toward earlier departure than real events [M] |
| Pilots and randomised evaluations | the effect of a measure on behaviour or welfare | e.g. anticipatory cash before a forecast flood in Bangladesh: recipients 36 % less likely to go a day without eating and more likely to evacuate (Pople et al. 2021 [V]) |
| Post-event damage surveys | fragility, model validation | the only direct evidence for back-testing |

#### F.9.2 Judging Worth before the Inquiry Is Made

The worth of an inquiry is the improvement it is expected to bring to a decision. The **expected value of perfect information** bounds it; the **expected value of sample information**, by preposterior analysis, values a particular design (Raiffa and Schlaifer 1961 [M]). Straub (2014 [V]) shows how structural reliability methods compute the value of information efficiently where nested simulation would be prohibitive, with monitoring of a deteriorating structure as the example. Where inquiries are sequential, the decision to inspect, monitor or act is a POMDP. **Adaptive monitoring** (Lindenmayer and Likens 2009 [V]) asks that monitoring be designed from well-posed questions and a conceptual model and that it evolve as questions change; it is the institutional counterpart of value-of-information analysis.

Two screening tools precede a full analysis. Sobol' indices of the loss with respect to its inputs show which uncertainty dominates the *quantity*; scenario discovery (§5) shows which uncertainty changes the *decision*. Only the second bears on worth: a dominant input that changes no decision is not worth inquiring into.

The infrastructure is well placed: tyche's `decision` holds EVPI, EVSI, the tests worth running before separate decisions within a budget (a knapsack of their EVSIs), POMDPs with intervals and HSVI; the policy domain's `adoption` holds the study worth doing over every size and the cost of waiting; tyche's `uncertainty` holds Sobol' and Morris. Missing is the binding of these to risk models: a preposterior analysis whose prior is an EP curve set and whose sample is a survey of buildings or a sensor network.

---

### F.10. Results Capture for Risk Reduction

#### F.10.1 Avoided Losses after an Event

Avoided loss is a counterfactual: the loss that would have occurred without the measure, less the loss that did. Two routes estimate it.

- **Model-based.** Run the risk model on the event's estimated intensity footprint (§8.1) with and without the measure (a retrofit, a levee, a warning). Its credibility rests on the vulnerability model's validity, so the avoided loss inherits the factor of 2 to 5 of §2.5 unless the model has been back-tested.
- **Empirical comparison.** Kreibich et al. (2022 [V]) assembled 45 pairs of floods or droughts in the same areas: risk management generally reduced impacts, but a second event much more hazardous than the first almost always did more harm; the two exceptions (Barcelona among them) combined governance, investment in structural and non-structural measures, and early warning.

#### F.10.2 Counterfactual Estimation and the Economic Case

Ex ante, the National Institute of Building Sciences' *Natural Hazard Mitigation Saves* (2019 [V]) estimates, for the United States, about $11 saved per $1 for adopting current model codes and $6 per $1 for federal mitigation grants, with geographic variation; Mechler (2016 [V]), restricting a review to risk-based estimates, finds benefits about four times costs on average but calls the evidence base limited and not very solid, and recommends cost-effectiveness, multi-criteria and robust approaches beside cost–benefit analysis. Ex post, the identification problem is that of any policy evaluation: synthetic control (Cavallo, Galiani, Noy and Pantano 2013 [V] used it to find that only extremely large disasters reduce output, and even that effect disappears when two cases of revolution are controlled for), differences in differences, matched comparisons. peira holds all three and its sensitivity analyses; Woo's (2019 [V]) downward counterfactuals extend the evidence to near misses.

#### F.10.3 Back-Testing Risk Models

A model's EP curve predicts how often losses exceed levels. Over *n* years the count of years exceeding the modelled 1-in-*T* loss is binomial with mean *n*/*T* if the model is right, so exceedance counts, probability integral transforms of observed annual losses and scoring rules (Brier, continuous ranked probability score) test calibration. Molinari et al. (2019 [V]) describe current practice as rare and data-starved. *Caveat.* Rare events give back-tests little power: a decade cannot confirm a 1-in-100-year loss, only refute gross miscalibration at short return periods. A sound claim of results is therefore stated with its standing: an avoided loss estimated from an unvalidated model is *arguable*, and the readings on which it turns (the counterfactual intensity, the vulnerability function, the baseline exposure) are named.

---

### F.11. Methods by What They Estimate

| Method | What it estimates | Assumptions | Open terms needing readings | Scale type of result | Evidence needed | kybernesis block and laws kept | In the infrastructure |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| PSHA / hazard curves (Cornell 1968) | annual rate of exceeding an intensity at a site | Poisson occurrence; source and ground-motion models; ergodicity | source model (AVR/MAX in J-SHIS), ground-motion model, site class | rate (ratio), probability (absolute) | catalogues, faults, strong-motion records | Estimation; sound only as a set over logic-tree branches | read from J-SHIS; no own engine (new adapter) |
| Event-set catastrophe model | event loss table, EP curve, AAL, PML | hazard × exposure × vulnerability factor; losses summed per event | return period reading, PML convention, occurrence vs aggregate | money (ratio), probability (absolute) | event set, exposure, vulnerability | Estimation → Evaluation; total, deterministic with keyed draws; sound as a band | tyche `descriptive` (EP, AAL, layers); event sets new |
| Fragility fitting (Porter 2007; Baker 2015) | P(damage state ≥ ds │ IM) | lognormal in IM; scalar IM sufficient | damage-state thresholds, IM choice | probability (absolute) | analyses or damage data with IM | Estimation; sound with parameter intervals | tyche GLMs fit it; domain module new |
| Bayesian updating of fragility (Singhal 1998) | posterior fragility | prior admissible; IM at sites known with error | prior source, damage states | probability (absolute) | observed damage, estimated IM | Estimation; sound if prior support is wide | tyche `bayes`; module new |
| Transferred depth–damage functions (Huizinga 2017) | damage ratio given depth | similarity of construction across regions | which library, maxima, similarity | ratio (0–1) | literature curves, development indicators | Estimation; sound only as a credal set over the library | new (library as data) |
| Logic trees (Bommer 2008) | epistemic spread of hazard | branches exclusive, exhaustive | weights, fractiles | set of curves | expert elicitation | Estimation keeps the set; the mean is an Evaluation reading | new (credal sets exist in tyche) |
| Extreme-value analysis | return levels with intervals | max-stability; stationarity or declared trend | block or threshold, stationarity reading | units of the variable (ratio) | long records | Estimation; sound with profile intervals checked | new (tyche) |
| Copulas (Salvadori 2016) | joint exceedance of drivers | margins fitted; dependence family | joint return period (OR/AND/Kendall), family | probability (absolute) | joint records | Estimation; sound as a set of families | new (tyche) |
| Interdependent percolation (Buldyrev 2010) | fragmentation under failures | random graphs, dependency links | what is "functional", coupling rules | fraction (absolute) | coupled networks | Evaluation of a model state | morphe flows; coupling new |
| Flow redistribution (Motter 2002) | cascade size | load moves by declared rule; capacity margin | capacity, failure rule | count, fraction | network with loads, capacities | Evaluation; deterministic | physis Kirchhoff, morphe flows; cascade module new |
| Inoperability input–output (Haimes 2001) | inoperability by sector | linear, static, no redundancy | interdependency matrix, demand shock | fraction (absolute) | input–output tables | Evaluation | economics `production` (Leontief); IIM small extension |
| Adaptive regional input–output (Hallegatte 2008) | indirect economic loss over recovery | capacity constraints, adaptation time | over-production capacity, adaptation time | money (ratio) | regional tables, direct losses | Evaluation; sound as a range over parameters | new (economics) |
| Basin stability (Menck 2013) | probability of returning after a shock | a declared model and perturbation law | perturbation law, "same state" | probability (absolute) | a model with parameter ranges | Evaluation of a model | kinesis basins; measure new (short) |
| Early-warning indicators (Dakos 2012) | approach to a bifurcation | slow forcing; fold bifurcation; stationarity in window | window, detrending, threshold | trend statistic (ordinal) | long regular series | Inquiry trigger, not Estimation (not sound) | kinesis signs of criticality |
| Resilience curve (Bruneau 2003; Cimellaro 2010) | loss of functionality over recovery | functionality measurable; baseline known | baseline, control time, measure, "recovered" | ratio once declared | service records | Evaluation | new (risk management `recovery`) |
| Heavy-tail fit (Clauset 2009) | tail law of losses | i.i.d. above x_min | x_min, alternatives | exponent (ratio) | many losses | Estimation; sound only with alternatives kept | new (tyche) |
| Robust decision making, scenario discovery (Lempert 2003; Bryant 2010) | strategies robust across futures; failure scenarios | ensemble spans plausible futures | the ensemble, regret or threshold | ordinal ranking; regret ratio | a model, ranges of inputs | Estimation as a set; PolicyMaking | tyche credal decisions, heuresis; PRIM new |
| Adaptive pathways (Haasnoot 2013) | sequence of actions with tipping points | tipping points identifiable; signposts observable | objectives, triggers | graph of pathways | models, monitoring | PolicyMaking → Decision | new |
| Info-gap (Ben-Haim 2006) | robustness horizon | nominal estimate near truth (Sniedovich 2010) | uncertainty model, requirement | horizon (ratio, local) | a nominal model | Evaluation; not sound when nominal is poor | new, as a declared reading |
| Social vulnerability index (Cutter 2003) | relative vulnerability | factors capture construct; additive | variables, weights, sign of each | ordinal within a computation | census data | Evaluation; refuse interval operations | nomos composites, psychometrics, tyche latent |
| Well-being losses (Hallegatte 2016; Markhvida 2020) | consumption-equivalent loss | utility form, recovery dynamics | elasticity, discount rate, reconstruction rate | ratio under readings | household surveys, asset losses | Evaluation; sound as a range over readings | new (social provision or risk management) |
| ShakeMap conditioning (Worden 2018) | intensity at every site after an event | multivariate normal; correlation model | ground-motion model, correlation | units of IM (ratio) | station records, model | Estimation; sound with posterior variance | tyche `spatial` conditioning; GMPEs new |
| Value of information (Straub 2014) | worth of an inquiry | a declared decision and prior | the decision, costs, prior | money (ratio) | prior risk model, inquiry design | Decision (inquiry) | tyche EVPI/EVSI, knapsack of tests; binding new |
| Avoided loss, back-testing | effect of a measure; model calibration | counterfactual identified; model validated | counterfactual intensity, baseline | money (ratio); test statistic | post-event damage, exposure | Evaluation of results | peira synthetic control, DiD; tyche testing |

### F.12. Methods by Block of kybernesis

| Block | Methods | Evidence | Better estimated than observed | Open terms | In the infrastructure |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Collection | inventories, surveys, sensor networks, crowdsourced reports, remote sensing of footprints and heights | images, census, stations, reports | building class, height, occupancy at time of day | sampling frame, what counts as a building | data library (Overture, GHS-BUILT-H, GHS-POP, J-SHIS to add); peira surveys |
| Evidence | channels of reports (crowdsourcing, labels from imagery), custody of records | reports with origins and timestamps | the event behind dependent reports | credibility of a channel, reporting threshold | tekmerion channels, located evidence, shared origins; archeion custody |
| Interpretation | readings of return period, joint return period, damage-state thresholds, "critical", "functional", PML convention, AVR/MAX source model | codes, hazard maps, standards, analysts' choices | — | each named here | tekmerion `construal`; apodeixis D(φ) |
| Estimation | hazard curves and event sets, logic trees kept as sets, fragility fitting and updating, transferred vulnerability as credal sets, copulas, extreme values, ShakeMap conditioning, network state from partial service, departure laws | records, models, damage, surveys, drills | intensity at a place, vulnerability, network functionality, departure | model choices, priors, perturbation laws | tyche `spatial`, `bayes`, GLMs, BN, HMM, change of support, credal sets; hazard engines, copulas, EVT, fragility module new |
| Evaluation | EP, AAL, PML, TVaR; importance measures; lifeline criticality; cascades; basin stability; resilience curves; social vulnerability (ordinal); well-being losses; robustness and regret | the belief from Estimation; standards held by someone | indirect loss, well-being loss | scale types, baselines, control time, utility and discounting | tyche `descriptive`; risk management `criticality`; urban risk `lifelines`, `response`; disaster risk `evacuation`; kinesis basins; nomos composites; cascades, recovery, well-being new |
| PolicyMaking | robust decision making, scenario discovery, pathways, cost–benefit and multi-criteria appraisal, risk layering | the evaluated risks, costs, futures | — | discount rate, regret vs threshold, tipping points | nomos `appraisal`; risk management `financing`; tyche credal decisions; heuresis; PRIM and pathways new |
| Policy | the declared plan with triggers and signposts | the plan's text and its schedule | — | trigger levels, responsibilities | policy domain (theories of change); kybernesis architecture |
| Decision (intervention) | retrofit, levee, warning, insurance layer, evacuation plan | the policy and current belief | — | thresholds of action | tyche `decision` (POMDP, one-step); calx submodular placement (refuges, stations) |
| Decision (inquiry) | value of information, adaptive monitoring, early-warning triggers, drills, pilots | prior risk model, inquiry designs and costs | which uncertainty matters to the decision | the decision, budget, horizon | tyche EVPI/EVSI and knapsack of tests; policy `adoption`; kinesis signs of criticality; binding to risk models new |
| Results (closing the loop) | avoided-loss estimation, synthetic control, paired events, back-testing of EP curves | post-event damage, outcomes, comparison units | counterfactual loss | counterfactual intensity, baseline exposure | peira quasi-experiments and sensitivity; tyche testing and scoring; back-testing routine new |

# Chapter G. Methods of Economic Evaluation and Allocation

## Part G. Methods of Economic Evaluation and of Allocation

*Draft, 2026-11-19, compacted at the coordinator's request. Markers: **[V]** read this session, or record retrieved from publisher or issuing body; **[S]** confirmed only from repositories, indexes or summaries; **[M]** from memory, unverified. Full citations in G.10.*

### G.1 Evaluation as an Operator on a Belief

An economic evaluation takes a belief over consequences and *readings* of open terms (discount schedule, value of statistical life, weights, horizon, counterfactual, numeraire) and returns a verdict on a scale. Uncertainty about consequences belongs to Estimation; the choice of readings is a choice among standards held by someone (a manual, a bank, a council). Kybernesis's dual verdict fits: "BCR > 1" has a *standing* over admissible readings and a *measure* under each. A statement is meaningful when invariant under its scales' admissible transformations (Stevens 1946 [M]; Roberts 1979 [M]; Bouyssou et al. 2006 [V]). ENPV is ratio-scale money; multi-attribute value at best interval; outranking ordinal. Changing the discount rate is not an admissible transformation but a different operator, hence a reading.

### G.2 Cost–Benefit Analysis in Development Practice

**Structure.** With-and-without comparison at economic prices (transfers removed, border prices, conversion factors, shadow wage), discounted to ENPV, EIRR or BCR, with sensitivity and switching values (Belli et al. 2001 [V]; ADB 2017 [V]). The EIRR may not be unique; BCR rankings depend on the cost/negative-benefit convention and are optimal only for divisible projects.

**World Bank.** IEG found CBA's share at appraisal falling for decades (IEG 2010 [V]). An advisory 2016 note links the rate to growth by Ramsey: elasticity 1–2, so growth *y*% gives *y*–2*y*%; 3% growth gives 6%, 1–5% gives 2–10%; NPVs over a range; full-life horizons; declining growth implies declining rates (World Bank OPSPQ 2016 [V]). The 2024 shadow-price-of-carbon note (replacing 2017) requires runs without, low, high and at the switching value [S].

**ADB.** Minimum required EIRR 9%, 6% for social, selected poverty-targeting and environmental projects; cost-effectiveness where benefits cannot be monetised; social cost of carbon $36.30/tCO₂e (2016 prices) rising 2% a year (ADB 2017 [V]).

**UK Green Book 2026.** STPR 3.00% (years 1–30), 2.50% (31–75), 2.25% (76+); distributional weight = (national median ÷ group median equivalised income)^1.25, weighted shown beside unweighted; WELLBY valuation admitted (HM Treasury 2026 [V]). MCDA only at the longlist, with swing weights; CBA or CEA at the shortlist (HM Treasury 2024 [V]). Climate guidance: high-warming sensitivity and real options for policies beyond 2040 (Defra 2025 [V]).

**Japan.** MLIT's guideline (2004, rev. 2008): 4% "for the time being", set by the opportunity cost of capital from real 10-year JGB yields; value of life ¥226 million from a Cabinet Office CV (MLIT 2008 [V]). From FY2024 reference values 1% and 2% (20- and 30-year average real yields 0.96%, 1.58%). In April 2026 CEFP members proposed about 2%; the Cabinet's Basic Policy (21 July 2026) commits to comprehensive evaluation not over-relying on B/C and to reviewing the rate; MLIT's committee took it up on 7 October 2026 (MLIT 2026 [V]). The flood manual (rev. April 2020) computes expected annual damage reduction; only monetisable categories enter B/C (MLIT 2020 [V]; later editions [S]). JICA re-calculates FIRR/EIRR ex post (JICA [V]).

**Discounting.** *r* = ρ + η*g*. Uncertain persistent rates imply declining certainty-equivalent rates (Weitzman 2001 [S]; Arrow et al. 2013 [S]); experts accept a 2% median risk-free rate (Drupp et al. 2018 [V]). US A-4's 2023 2% default was rescinded in 2025 [S]. The reading set is plural and unstable: report standing over it.

**Time, life, health, carbon.** Travel-time values need a declared social weighting (Mackie et al. 2001 [S]). VSL income elasticity ≈0.5–0.7 in the US, >1 elsewhere (Viscusi and Masterman 2017 [V]). Reference Case defaults: US VSL $9.4m, ratio 160 to GNI per head, elasticity 1.5, floor 20× GNI; alternatives ratio 100 or 160 with elasticity 1.0; discounting at 3% and twice near-term growth (Robinson et al. 2019 [V]; Robinson, Hammitt and O'Keeffe 2019 [S]). GDP-multiple cost-effectiveness thresholds are criticised (Bertram et al. 2016 [S]). Target-consistent carbon prices (World Bank) and damage-based social costs (ADB) answer different questions.

### G.3 Non-Market Valuation and Benefit Transfer

Hedonic and travel-cost methods reveal values; contingent valuation and choice experiments state them, under guidance on consequentiality, incentive compatibility and scope (Johnston et al. 2017 [S]). Transfer errors: measurement, generalisation, publication bias (Rosenberger and Stanley 2006 [S]); median absolute error 39% over 1,071 errors, smaller for function transfers (Kaul et al. 2013 [S]); a transferred value must carry its error. Avoided damage is a counterfactual functional of two exceedance curves. The *triple dividend* adds stimulated activity and co-benefits to avoided losses (Tanner et al. 2015 [V]); *wellbeing losses* weight losses by consumption effects on the poor (Hallegatte et al. 2017 [S]). Both are alternative Evaluation operators on one Estimation output.

### G.4 Cost-Effectiveness and Multi-Criteria Analysis

CEA and cost-utility give ratios meaningful once outcome and comparator are fixed; they need a threshold or budget to decide. MAVT/MAUT is additive under mutual preferential independence, with weights as scaling constants (Keeney and Raiffa 1976 [M]), hence swing weights (DCLG 2009 [V]; HM Treasury 2024 [V]). AHP (Saaty 1980 [M]) shows rank reversal when alternatives are added (Belton and Gear 1983 [S]; Dyer 1990 [S]). Outranking: ELECTRE (Roy 1991 [V]), PROMETHEE (Brans and Vincke 1985 [V]), partially non-compensatory, yielding partial preorders. Admission tests on a finite instance: invariance under each criterion's admissible transformations; invariance under adding a dominated option (AHP fails); preferential independence for additive forms; weighted sums of raw or ordinal scores are not meaningful.

### G.5 Under Uncertainty and Over Time

Real options value flexibility as the best adaptive policy minus the best fixed design (Dixit and Pindyck 1994 [M]; de Neufville and Scholtes 2011 [S]; Defra 2025 [V]). Adaptive pathways sequence actions with tipping points and triggers (Haasnoot et al. 2013 [S]); they are a PolicyMaking structure appraised by NPV, regret or MCDA. RDM runs strategies over ensembles and scores regret (Lempert et al. 2003 [M]); no method is best, a menu is recommended (Hallegatte et al. 2012 [V]); robustness metrics rank differently, so the metric is a reading (McPhail et al. 2018 [S]). Value of information decides intervention versus inquiry (tyche `decision`, policy `adoption`).

### G.6 Allocation

Capital investment planning: inventories, envelope, scoring, ranking, monitoring (Kaganova 2011 [V]; Farvacque-Vitkovic and Kopanyi 2014 [V]); funding down a score ranking is not optimal for indivisible projects, and cost inside the score double-counts the budget. Portfolio problems: knapsack, multidimensional and integer programmes with equity and dependency constraints (Salo et al. 2011 [V]); robust portfolio modelling classifies projects as core, borderline or exterior over admissible weights, a standing over readings (Liesiö et al. 2007 [S]). Participatory budgeting: the method of equal shares satisfies extended justified representation in polynomial time (Peters et al. 2021 [V]). Risk finance: layering by frequency and severity (World Bank 2014 [V]); instruments compared by economic opportunity cost including late funds (Clarke et al. 2016 [V]); pre-arranged, rule-based finance makes disasters "dull" (Clarke and Dercon 2016 [S]); readings: cost multiples, opportunity cost of reserves, cost of delay, basis risk. PPPs: only when CBA-justified, better value than public procurement and fiscally responsible, tested against a contestable comparator (PPP Reference Guide 3.0 [V]); they do not free public funds (Engel et al. 2014 [S]). Land value capture: sixty countries' practice (OECD and Lincoln Institute 2022 [V]); capitalised gains must not be added to the user benefits they capitalise [M].

### G.7 Distribution and Incidence

Weights follow from a social welfare function (Adler 2016 [S]); simpler weights on wellbeing indices may approximate (Fleurbaey and Abi-Rafeh 2016 [S]). Fiscal incidence: CEQ method (Lustig 2018 [V]). Utility subsidies often target poorly; connection subsidies do better (Komives et al. 2005 [V]); median targeted programme gives about a quarter more to the poor than universal allocation (Coady et al. 2004 [V]). Inclusion enters as weights (Evaluation), constraints (PolicyMaking) or non-compensatory standards (MCDA); report standing over η ∈ {0, 1, 1.25, 2}.

### G.8 Results Capture

Ex post evaluation compares appraised with realised values: JICA's EIRR recalculation [V]; Norway's ex post road CBAs (Odeck and Kjerkreit 2019 [S]). Cost underestimation as strategic misrepresentation (Flyvbjerg et al. 2002 [S]); reference class forecasting sets an uplift from the class's realised/forecast distribution (Flyvbjerg 2006 [V]); disputed by Love and Ahiaga-Dagbui (2018 [S]). In kybernesis, realised values return through Collection; the reference class is an Estimation belief; class membership is an open term. *Not yet surveyed:* MLIT's ex post evaluation practice; UK optimism-bias guidance.
