# Chapter H. Tourism

## Part H. Tourism: Promotion, Development and Management, Read Through the Blocks of the Loop

### H.0 Scope, Markers and Limits

This part treats tourism as a system to be steered: a destination whose state is known only through evidence, on which actions of promotion, development and management are taken, and whose response is then shown. It is organised by the eight blocks of kybernesis (Collection, Evidence, Interpretation, Estimation, Evaluation, PolicyMaking, Actions, Results capture). Statistical frameworks and headline indicators (IRTS 2008, the Tourism Satellite Account: Recommended Methodological Framework 2008, the UN Tourism Statistical Framework for Measuring the Sustainability of Tourism adopted in 2024; arrivals, spending, intensity) are covered in the part on productive cities and are cited here as known **[M]**.

Markers: **[V]** verified this session against the issuing body's own page, the report or its DOI record (where a page refused direct fetching, its text was read through the search index of that same page, and this is said); **[S]** confirmed only from secondary sources (press, trade journals, catalogues); **[M]** from memory, not verified.

*Caveat.* The session's shared allowance of web searches was exhausted before Nara, Bhutan's visitor fee, Barcelona's licences and several academic critiques could be checked; those items stay **[M]** and should be verified before use.

### H.1 Concepts: The Destination as a Steered System

A destination is a place where a market for visits, a stock of assets, a resident population and coordinating institutions meet. **Promotion** acts on demand (who comes, when, from where); **development** on supply and assets (products, infrastructure, skills, investment, heritage); **management** on the joint state of visitors, residents and assets (flows, crowding, prices, rules, the distribution of benefits). The World Bank's review of a decade of its tourism work states the governing lesson: benefits are not automatic, and growth without management undermines its own base (H.2.1). The loop keeps apart what is observed, what is believed, whose standard judges it, and what is done.

### H.2 Frameworks and International Practice

#### H.2.1 The World Bank Group

The Bank's tourism topic page gives the framing figures used in its operations: tourism at about 10% of global GDP (US$11.6 trillion) in 2025, 366 million jobs, and US$2 trillion of international visitor spending in 2025; it names Destination Management Organizations among what it helps establish, and lists operations in Indonesia, Madagascar, Sierra Leone, Pakistan (eight Destination Management and Investment Plans), Albania and Cabo Verde **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/ext/en/topic/tourism).

The *Tourism for Development* knowledge series supplies the design tools:

- *20 Reasons Sustainable Tourism Counts for Development* (2017) **[V]** (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/28388, read through the index; direct fetch refused).
- *Women and Tourism: Designing for Inclusion* (Twining-Ward and Zhou, 2017), a gender lens for each project stage, including monitoring **[V]** (https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/401321508245393514/pdf/120477-WP-PUBLIC-Weds-oct-18-9am-ADD-SERIES-36p-IFCWomenandTourismfinal.pdf).
- *Tourism Theory of Change* (Twining-Ward, Messerli, Sharma and Villascusa Cerezo, 2018), a step-by-step guide to designing and evaluating tourism projects through a theory of change **[V]** (http://hdl.handle.net/10986/35459).
- *Tourism Diagnostic Toolkit* (IFC, 2019), to be used with the theory of change **[S]**.

On heritage, *The Economics of Uniqueness: Investing in Historic City Cores and Cultural Heritage Assets for Sustainable Development* (Licciardi and Amirtahmasebi, eds., 2012, CC BY 3.0 IGO) argues that historic cores are assets whose conservation pays when it is joined to urban development, with Throsby's conceptual chapter on heritage economics **[V]** (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/12286). On shocks, *Rebuilding Tourism Competitiveness: Tourism Response, Recovery and Resilience to the COVID-19 Crisis* (policy note, July 2020) sets out dependence, resilience risk and a menu of responses **[V]** (https://openknowledge.worldbank.org/handle/10986/34348), followed by *Expecting the Unexpected: Tools and Policy Considerations to Support the Recovery and Resilience of the Tourism Sector* (2022) **[S]**.

*Tourism for Development: Lessons Learned from a Decade of World Bank Experience* (copyright December 2025) is summarised by its authors in "Six ways to make tourism projects work for people, places, and prosperity" (Twining-Ward, Bellas Suárez and Wilson, 12 February 2026) **[V]** (https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/psd/six-ways-to-make-tourism-projects-work-for-people--places--and-p). The Group mobilised more than US$10 billion for tourism in 80 countries over the decade. The six ways read as a specification of the loop: understand demand first (proxies such as flight tracking, card spending and booking platforms where official data are missing); build governance (public–private DMOs); support small firms (about 80% of the sector's jobs), women-owned ones especially; target infrastructure at high-potential circuits; use tourism to conserve (certification, park fees, bed taxes); measure outcomes with a common theory of change and core indicators.

TDLC's Technical Deep Dive *Cities for Tourism and Jobs* (1–5 December 2025) brought over forty officials from nine countries to Kyoto (visitors balanced with residents and heritage), Sapporo (a gateway dispersing visitors), Hokkaido (year-round products) and Otaru (citizen-led regeneration); participants drafted action plans **[V]** (https://www.worldbank.org/en/programs/tokyo-development-learning-center/tdd/cities_for_tourism_and_jobs_tdd1). Its blog, "Resilient tourism starts with residents" (Mahgoub, Miki and Chun, 17 February 2026), draws three lessons (start with residents, spread benefits, build feedback loops) and names Barcelona's resident perception surveys as a feedback instrument **[V]** (https://blogs.worldbank.org/en/sustainablecities/resilient-tourism-starts-with-residents). An earlier TDD on resilient cultural heritage and sustainable tourism was held in Tokyo and Kyoto in 2017 with the city of Kyoto, UNESCO and Ritsumeikan University **[S]**.

#### H.2.2 UN Tourism (formerly UNWTO)

- *UNWTO Guidelines for Institutional Strengthening of Destination Management Organizations (DMOs)* (2019), from marketing boards to management bodies, on UNWTO.QUEST's criteria **[V]** (https://www.untourism.int/global/publication/unwto-guidelines-institutional-strengthening-destination-management-organizations-dmos-p, read through the index).
- *'Overtourism'? Understanding and Managing Urban Tourism Growth beyond Perceptions* (2018; DOI 10.18111/9789284419999) studies eight European cities and proposes 11 strategies and 68 measures (dispersal, regulation, local benefit, engagement, monitoring) **[V]** (https://www.untourism.int/global/publication/overtourism-understanding-and-managing-urban-tourism-growth-beyond-perceptions); a second volume of case studies followed in 2019 **[S]**.
- The International Network of Sustainable Tourism Observatories (INSTO) monitors economic, environmental and social impact at destination level on 11 mandatory issue areas, under a framework presented to the Executive Council in November 2016 **[V]** (https://www.untourism.int/sustainable-development/un-tourism-international-network-of-sustainable-tourism-observatories). The eleven are, from memory, local satisfaction, economic benefit, employment, seasonality, energy, water, wastewater, solid waste, governance, climate action and accessibility **[M]**.
- *Achieving the Sustainable Development Goals through Tourism: Toolkit of Indicators for Projects (TIPs)* (UN Tourism and JICA, July 2023, 296 pp.) gives project-level indicators by SDG target, explicitly not exhaustive **[V]** (https://www.jica.go.jp/activities/issues/private_sec/__icsFiles/afieldfile/2024/02/15/2023-achieving-the-sustainable-development-goals-through-tourism-toolkit-of-indicators-for-projects-(tips).pdf).
- *Global Report on Women in Tourism, Second Edition* (2019; with UN Women, GIZ, the World Bank Group and Amadeus): women are 54% of tourism employment against 39% in the wider economy; the pay gap is 14.7% against 16.8% **[V]** (https://www.untourism.int/publication/global-report-women-tourism-2-edition).

#### H.2.3 OECD, GSTC, WEF and the European Parliament

*OECD Tourism Trends and Policies 2024* (DOI 10.1787/80885d8b-en, CC BY 4.0) covers 50 countries and recommends helping destinations spread and manage visitor flows and helping firms adopt digital and green practices **[V]** (https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/oecd-tourism-trends-and-policies-2024_80885d8b-en.html).

The GSTC Destination Criteria v2.0 (adopted 6 December 2019) are grouped in four sections (sustainable management; socio-economic; cultural; environmental), each criterion with performance indicators and SDG tags **[V]** (https://www.gstc.org/wp-content/uploads/GSTC-Destination-Criteria-v2.0-with-SDGs.pdf). They are the nearest thing to a declared standard against which a destination's state can be judged criterion by criterion.

The WEF *Travel & Tourism Development Index 2024* scores 119 economies on 5 dimensions, 17 pillars and 102 indicators; Japan ranked third after the United States and Spain **[V]** (https://www.weforum.org/publications/travel-tourism-development-index-2024/, read through the index; direct fetch refused). *Caveat.* It is national, input-oriented, partly opinion-based and weighted by choice; it says little of a city or of who gains (critiques: Croes and Kubickova 2013 **[M]**).

The European Parliament's study *Research for TRAN Committee – Overtourism: Impact and Possible Policy Responses* (Peeters et al., October 2018, 41 case studies) concludes that no common set of indicators can be defined, names tourism density (bed-nights per km²) and intensity (bed-nights per resident) and proximity to airports, cruise ports and World Heritage Sites as the most relevant indicators, and recommends monitoring the sentiment of residents, hosts and visitors **[V]** (https://researchdata.brighton.ac.uk/id/eprint/149/).

### H.3 Japanese Practice

#### H.3.1 The National Frame

The *Tourism Vision to Support the Future of Japan* (明日の日本を支える観光ビジョン, March 2016) set for 2030 60 million foreign visitors, ¥15 trillion of their spending, 36 million repeaters, 130 million foreign nights in regional areas and ¥22 trillion of domestic spending **[V]** (MLIT white paper, https://www.mlit.go.jp/hakusyo/mlit/r03/hakusho/r04/html/n2251000.html, read through the index). The fourth Tourism Nation Promotion Basic Plan (31 March 2023, FY2023–2025) turned from numbers to value: 100 regions working on sustainable destination management, ¥200,000 per foreign visitor and two regional nights per visitor by 2025 **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/news02_000507.html, read through the index).

The fifth Basic Plan (Cabinet decision 27 March 2026, FY2026–2030) keeps 60 million and ¥15 trillion and adds new targets on repeaters, regional nights, overtourism and the industry **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/news02_00077.html). Its figures, from trade reporting, are 40 million repeaters, 130 million foreign regional nights, ¥250,000 per visitor, 100 regions balancing visitors with residents' lives (from 47), ¥30 trillion of domestic spending and ¥6.8 trillion of accommodation value added; tourism is called a "strategic industry" **[S]** (Travel Voice, 27 March 2026). Arrivals were about 36.87 million in 2024 and 42.68 million in 2025 (JNTO estimate), with spending of about ¥9.5 trillion in 2025 **[S]**.

The International Tourist Tax (国際観光旅客税) rises to ¥3,000 per departure from 1 July 2026, its uses being a comfortable travel environment, information, and regional resources, now including overtourism countermeasures and dispersal **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/seisaku_seido/kihonkeikaku/inbound_kaifuku/ryokakuzei.html); the previous rate was ¥1,000 **[S]**. The *Package for the Prevention and Control of Overtourism* (18 October 2023) has three pillars: crowding and manners, dispersal to regional areas, and tourism with residents **[V]** (Ministerial Council, https://www.kantei.go.jp/jp/singi/kankorikkoku/dai21/gijisidai.pdf, read through the index); its subsidy's second round (25 July 2025) selected 7 region-wide plans and 36 demonstration projects **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/news08_00024.html).

#### H.3.2 Registered DMOs

The Japan Tourism Agency (JTA) registers a *kankō chiiki-zukuri hōjin* (観光地域づくり法人, the Japanese DMO) that meets five requirements: a destination strategy with indicators and data collection; strategy-based work in a PDCA cycle; consensus among diverse stakeholders; an established organisation; stable finance. Registration lasts three years and needs twelve months of prior activity; the page (updated 24 July 2026) names three types: wide-area, prefectural and local **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/seisaku_seido/dmo/toroku.html). In 2022 there were 241 registered and 70 candidate DMOs **[S]**.

The KPIs changed on 24 July 2026 (in force 1 August). Formerly four were mandatory: travel spending, overnight stays, visitor satisfaction and repeat rate **[S]**. Now travel spending is a mandatory KGI; of eight KPI viewpoints, four are mandatory (spending per visitor, overnight stays, visitor satisfaction and **resident satisfaction**), with examples such as workers' wages and seasonality; from April 2027 a renewal adds the economic ripple effect to the KGI, measured once in three years **[S]** (Travel Voice, 26 July 2026). In the loop's terms, the revision changes *who holds the standard*: residents join visitors as judges of the state, and seasonality and wages become admissible features.

#### H.3.3 Local Levies and Visitor Management

- **Kyoto.** An accommodation tax in force since 1 October 2018, revised from 1 March 2026 into five bands per person-night: ¥200 under ¥6,000; ¥400 to ¥20,000; ¥1,000 to ¥50,000; ¥4,000 to ¥100,000; ¥10,000 above (formerly three bands, ¥200, ¥500, ¥1,000) **[V]** (https://www.city.kyoto.lg.jp/gyozai/page/0000236942.html; the city's table is poorly formatted and was checked against hotel notices). The *Kyoto Tourism Comfort Map* (Kyoto City Tourism Association, funded by the levy) forecasts crowding at 11 spots on five levels, hourly, from smartphone location data **[V]** (https://ja.kyoto.travel/comfort/kyoto/). Express buses EX100/EX101 (from June 2024, ¥500) keep visitors off residents' routes **[S]**; a ¥200 resident fare against about ¥360, residents identified through the My Number card, is proposed for FY2027 **[S]**.
- **Miyajima** (Hatsukaichi): a visitor tax of ¥100 per visit (¥500 a year) from October 2023, collected with the ferry fare **[S]**.
- **Mount Fuji**: from 2025 a ¥4,000 fee on all four trails and a cap of 4,000 climbers a day on the Yoshida trail **[S]**.
- **Hokkaido**: a prefectural accommodation tax from 1 April 2026, its revenue for products, reception, crises such as disasters, and communities; Sapporo, Otaru, Niseko, Rusutsu, Kutchan, Hakodate, Furano and eleven other municipalities levy their own on top **[V]** (https://hokkaido-shukuhakuzei.pref.hokkaido.lg.jp/).
- **Venice** (a comparison): an access fee for day visitors on 54 days in 2025, €5, or €10 booked late; 723,497 paid, €5.42 million collected, with crowding little changed **[S]** (Euronews, 8 September 2025).

#### H.3.4 Cases and the Support Factors Their Transfer Needs

- **Setouchi Triennale** (since 2010): art as a product for depopulating islands; 1,084,128 visits in 2025 and ¥19.5 billion of ripple effect in Kagawa against ¥10.3 billion in 2022 (Bank of Japan Takamatsu branch) **[S]**. *Support factors:* a patron and curator, ferries, willing island hosts, a triennial rhythm.
- **Hida-Takayama**: inbound work since the 1980s (guides in five languages from 1990, a public–private promotion convention) **[S]**; foreign overnight guests 612,204 (2019), 769,743 (2024), 978,312 (2025) **[S]**. *Support factors:* a coherent townscape, a rail gateway, continuity of the municipal team, off-season foreign demand.
- **Niseko and Kutchan**: snow sold abroad, foreign investment, municipal levies **[V]** (Hokkaido page); land prices and workers' housing strained **[M]**. *Support factors:* a unique asset, international air access, land-use rules, housing for workers.
- **Kyoto**: dispersal, crowding information, segregated buses, a banded levy, residents as judges. *Support factors:* a levy power, location data, a forecasting operator, surveyed residents.
- **Nara**: not yet surveyed.

#### H.3.5 Data Systems

The JTA produces arrivals and departures, the Travel and Tourism Consumption Survey, the Accommodation Survey, the Inbound Consumption Survey (formerly the Consumption Trend Survey for Foreigners), tourist counts on common standards, travel agency sales, economic ripple effects and a TSA **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/siryou/toukei/). The Accommodation Survey (since 2007) covers establishments licensed under the Inns and Hotels Act, all those with ten or more employees and a sample of the smaller; private lodging (*minpaku*) is outside it **[S]**. The common standards for prefectural tourist counts date from 2009, revised in 2013 and 2023 **[S]**. RESAS (public since April 2015) shows foreigners' stays from NTT DOCOMO's Mobile Spatial Statistics and domestic origins from Agoop's data **[S]**.

#### H.3.6 Accessibility, Safety and Cooperation

The JTA certifies tourism facilities for "barrier-free of the heart" (from 2021; e.g. 741 in Kanto by October 2026) **[V]** (https://www.mlit.go.jp/kankocho/content/002044776.pdf, through the index); its *Safety tips* application (since 2014) pushes disaster alerts to visitors in 15 languages **[S]**. JICA treats tourism as a means of regional management rather than an end, names leakage and overtourism as risks, and cooperates with UN Tourism under a 2017 agreement **[V]** (https://www.jica.go.jp/activities/issues/private_sec/tourism/index.html).

### H.4 The Blocks

#### H.4.1 Collection

Six designs, each with its mechanism of inclusion: (1) **visitor surveys** at frontiers and sites, where interception samples visits in proportion to stay, so weights are needed (peira's surveys and raking); (2) **registers** (accommodation and levy returns, tickets), exhaustive within scope and blind to day visitors, *minpaku* and friends' homes; (3) **counts and cameras**;

4. **Mobile positioning**: Ahas et al. (2008) found correlations up to 0.99 with accommodation statistics in Estonia's most visited regions, lower where transit dominates **[V]** (DOI 10.1016/j.tourman.2007.05.014). Eurostat's feasibility study (June 2014) found the data consistent over time but unlikely to replace the indicators of Regulation 692/2011, and access, under privacy law, the main barrier **[V]** (https://unstats.un.org/unsd/trade/events/2014/beijing/documents/phonedata/Mobile%20Phone_Consolidated%20report.pdf).
5. **Platforms**: listings, prices and reviews. Inside Airbnb's data are CC BY 4.0 for the last twelve months, older data on request, with no scraping or republishing **[V]** (https://insideairbnb.com/data-policies/); commercial panels (AirDNA) and review sites forbid redistribution **[M]**. Card transactions are a proxy the Bank recommends where official data are missing **[V]** (six ways, H.2.1); their licences are bilateral.
6. **Residents' sentiment**: surveys on declared scales, repeated (Barcelona, H.2.1); now a mandatory DMO KPI in Japan (H.3.2).

#### H.4.2 Evidence

Biases: mobile data by operator share, roaming and suppression of small cells; platforms show offers, not stays; reviews favour the dissatisfied; Overture and OSM places show businesses, not whether visitors use them; night lights mix residents and visitors. tekmerion's located evidence holds them, a camera, a phone count and a ticket count at one gate in one hour being three reports, not three proofs.

#### H.4.3 Interpretation

Open terms, each with several admissible readings: **visitor** and **usual environment** (IRTS **[M]**; a phone seen twice a month may be a commuter or a visitor); **tourism-characteristic industry** (TSA **[M]**; sales to visitors are an allocation); **overtourism** (UN Tourism's "beyond perceptions"; the European Parliament's practical definition), a state judged against someone's standard; **carrying capacity**, which limits of acceptable change replace with declared indicators, standards, actions and monitoring in nine steps (Stankey et al. 1985, USDA Forest Service GTR INT-176) **[V]** (https://www.fs.usda.gov/Internet/FSE_DOCUMENTS/fseprd1179724.pdf); a destination's **stage** (Butler 1980, *Canadian Geographer* 24(1): 5–12, DOI 10.1111/j.1541-0064.1980.tb00970.x) **[V]**; **resident** (Kyoto's planned fare), **regional area** (地方部), **local**, **repeater**. tekmerion's `construal` reads a finding under every combination and says what it turns on.

#### H.4.4 Estimation

The state variables that matter, and how each is better known:

- **Visitor stock by place and hour**: mobile aggregates, counts and accommodation returns joined and calibrated to registers (peira's small areas; kinesis's `scales` under control totals). Exposure to hazards is this stock at the wrong hour, and no register holds it.
- **Spending and impact**: survey means times stock, allocated by card or platform shares, carried through input–output with leakage. Crompton (2006, *J. Travel Research* 45: 67–82, DOI 10.1177/0047287506288870) shows most impact studies are commissioned to legitimise a position **[V]**; Dwyer, Forsyth and Spurr (2004, *Tourism Management* 25(3): 307–317, DOI 10.1016/S0261-5177(03)00131-6) prefer CGE to multipliers **[V]**; Faber and Gaubert (2019, *AER* 109(6): 2245–2293, DOI 10.1257/aer.20161434) find large local gains in Mexico largely offset in aggregate **[V]**.
- **Demand ahead**: reviewed by Song, Qiu and Park (2019, *Annals of Tourism Research* 75: 338–362, DOI 10.1016/j.annals.2018.12.001) **[V]**; Google Trends nowcasts by mixed-data sampling (Bangwayo-Skeete and Skeete 2015, *Tourism Management* 46: 454–464, DOI 10.1016/j.tourman.2014.07.014) **[V]**; gravity from utility (Morley, Rosselló and Santana-Gallego 2014, *Annals* 48: 1–10, DOI 10.1016/j.annals.2014.05.008) **[V]**. Nguyen (2020, IMF WP/20/169, *Japan's Inbound Tourism Boom*), a panel ARDL on 34 markets, finds visa relaxation for over 40 countries in 2013–2018 contributed substantially and disasters had large, prolonged effects **[V]** (https://www.imf.org/en/publications/wp/issues/2020/08/21/japans-inbound-tourism-boom-lessons-for-its-post-covid-19-revival-49618).
- **Housing pressure**: in Barcelona Airbnb raised rents 1.9% and transaction prices 4.6% on average, 7% and 17% in the top decile (Garcia-López et al. 2020, *J. Urban Economics* 119: 103278, DOI 10.1016/j.jue.2020.103278) **[V]**; in the United States, 1% more listings raised rents about 0.018% (Barron, Kung and Proserpio 2021, *Marketing Science*, DOI 10.1287/mksc.2020.1227) **[V]**.
- **Residents' sentiment**: a latent trait (nomos's IRT, by ward through small areas); Sharpley (2014, *Tourism Management* 42: 37–49, DOI 10.1016/j.tourman.2013.10.007) finds the literature measures perceptions, not responses **[V]**.
- **Seasonality, job quality, leakage, environmental load**: derived from the above; not yet surveyed in depth.

#### H.4.5 Evaluation

Features on declared scales, each judged by a standard someone holds: intensity and density (ratio; a city's thresholds); crowding at a node (ratio, or Kyoto's five ordinal levels; visitors' comfort, residents' access); residents' satisfaction (ordinal items, latent scores; residents' own standard, now a Japanese DMO KPI); visitor satisfaction and repeat rate (operators'); GSTC compliance (nominal per criterion); seasonality (ratio on [0, 1]; workers' and operators'); shares of jobs and procurement for women, youth and local firms (the government's inclusion standard); heritage condition (ordinal; heritage authorities'); expected loss to tourism (an insurer's or ministry's tolerance). Needs, problems, risks and assets are then declared readings of these features; kybernesis's dual verdicts (a standing and a measure kept apart) suit them, since "overtourism here" may be clear under residents' standard and untenable under operators'.

#### H.4.6 PolicyMaking

Options are generated from a theory of change (H.2.1), from the repertoire of H.4.7, from UN Tourism's 68 measures, and from practices elsewhere. They are appraised by cost–benefit or multi-criteria analysis (nomos), with the value of information deciding whether to pilot first (the policy domain's `adoption`). A practice is transferred only through an argument: Cartwright and Hardie (2012, *Evidence-Based Policy: A Practical Guide to Doing It Better*, Oxford University Press) separate efficacy *there* from effectiveness *here*, and ask whether the cause can play the same role here and which support factors must be present, found by horizontal and vertical search **[S]**. The policy domain's `transfer` and tekmerion's `warrant` already implement the argument; what tourism adds is the catalogue of support factors (H.3.4, Table 2).

#### H.4.7 Actions

The repertoire, with mechanisms, evidence, risks, examples and support factors, is Table 2. Inquiries (a resident survey, a pilot levy, a purchase of mobile data) are actions on the belief. Utah's *Mighty 5* campaign is a cautionary case: success at some parks was followed by congestion and "demarketing" (H.4.8).

#### H.4.8 Results Capture

Synthetic control: Drugova, Kim and Jakus (2021, *Tourism Economics* 27(8): 1759–1778) find the *Mighty 5* campaign raised visits at three of five parks, the others following national trends **[V]** (https://econpapers.repec.org/RePEc:sae:toueco:v:27:y:2021:i:8:p:1759-1778). Visa waivers, levies and festivals adopted at different dates call for peira's staggered adoption; dispersal and levies displace visitors, which peira's interference measures. Monitoring: INSTO, GSTC, TIPs, the Bank's core indicators, Japan's DMO KGI and KPIs. Promoters' impact studies overstate (Crompton), so the counterfactual is declared before the data arrive (peira's protocols).

### H.5 Mapping to the Infrastructure

What exists is listed by block in Table 1's last column; besides it, markets' Huff and sizing, nomos's stated-choice models with willingness to pay and agents, and the risk domains' evacuation serve tourism directly. What needs a new module (each a candidate for its own decision record; none proposed here as a change):

1. a **visitor-stock estimator**, from mobile, counts and accommodation returns to visitors by place, hour and origin, with the reading of "visitor" declared;
2. a **tourism demand vector and leakage account**, allocating spending to tourism-characteristic industries on the existing input–output tables (a TSA-lite);
3. **mixed-frequency nowcasting** (MIDAS), not in tyche;
4. **seasonality and intensity measures** with their standards;
5. **readers for platform data** (Inside Airbnb within its licence) and housing-pressure indicators;
6. **declared limits of acceptable change**: indicators, standards and triggered actions, as evaluation operators of kybernesis;
7. **levy and fare design**: bands, dual prices and caps, their revenue and incidence on visitors and residents;
8. **visitors in disaster risk**: the visitor stock as a population for the evacuation and warning modules (Miyako already in the infrastructure).

| Actions | promotion; products; infrastructure; regulation; levies; visitor management; capacity; investment; heritage; inquiries | plans, ordinances, budgets | — | instrument definitions (resident fare, band limits) | nomos microsimulation and agents; *new*: levy and fare design |

#### H.6.2 Table 2. The Repertoire of Actions

| Action | Mechanism | States it acts on | Evidence it worked | Typical risks | Japanese or global example | Support factors for transfer |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Promotion and campaigns | information and image shift demand | arrivals by origin; seasonality | mixed; SCM finds effects at some sites only (Utah 2021) | congestion at icons; attribution inflated | Visit Japan **[M]**; Utah *Mighty 5* | an asset that can absorb more; a counterfactual planned before launch |
| Visa relaxation and air links | lower cost of access | arrivals by origin | large long-run effect in Japan 2013–2018 (IMF 2020) | concentration on few markets; shock exposure | Japan's waivers for Southeast Asia | national competence; airport capacity; security screening |
| Products and festivals | new reason to visit; dispersal | regional nights; spending; repeaters | 1.08 m visits and ¥19.5 bn ripple in Kagawa (S) | fatigue; leakage to outside firms | Setouchi Triennale | patron, curator, transport to sites, host communities |
| Infrastructure | access and services | stock capacity; satisfaction | Bank operations report jobs and investment (Indonesia, Sierra Leone) | spread too thin; stranded assets | Indonesia ITDP | targeting of high-potential circuits; maintenance finance |
| Regulation of short-term rentals | supply cap or licence | housing pressure; nights | rents and prices rise with listings (Barcelona, US) | informality; displacement to suburbs | Japan's *minpaku* law 2018 **[M]**; Barcelona **[M]** | enforcement capacity; platform data access |
| Levies and taxes | price and revenue earmarked | demand at margin; funds for management | revenue reliable; demand effect small (Venice S) | regressive on cheap stays; diversion to untaxed lodging | Kyoto bands; Hokkaido; ¥3,000 departure tax; Miyajima | legal power to levy; collection by operators; visible use of revenue |
| Caps and reservations | quantity limit at a node | crowding; safety; condition of asset | Fuji cap rarely binding (S) | queues moved elsewhere; inequity of access | Mount Fuji Yoshida trail; Venice | a single gate; booking system; enforcement |
| Information on crowding and dispersal | shift visits in time and place | crowding by hour; secondary sites' share | plausible, rarely evaluated | displacement to residents' streets | Kyoto Comfort Map (phone data, funded by levy) | location data; forecasting capacity; alternative sites worth visiting |

# Closing Note. The Counts of Items and Markers by Chapter

The counts below were made by the script that assembled this report, from the parts as the agents wrote them. Two counts are made, because the parts cite in two ways. The first counts the entries of a chapter's reference list and the first marker on each entry; where a part gives its references as a compact paragraph (Chapters A and G), the script counts the segments between the separators, and a segment may hold several items (Chapter A's list of DOIs is one segment), so the figure is a lower bound on its items. The second counts every marker token in the chapter's text, including the markers that the parts place on claims and figures as well as on bibliographic items, and so exceeds the number of items; a combined token such as [V/S] is counted under each of its letters. A last column repeats the count the part itself states, where it states one. No marker was changed in assembly.
